* DONE [#D] hermes-mcp fails to start: Starlette removed the on_startup argument
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-04y
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 21:11]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:57]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:57]
:CLOSE_REASON: Migrated build_app() from on_startup=/on_shutdown= to an asynccontextmanager lifespan (works on both the pinned starlette 0.47.2 and 1.x, so the deprecation tripwire is gone). Also added the missing pytestCheckHook: nativeCheckInputs listed pytest/pytest-asyncio/respx and pytestFlags was set, but with no hook nixpkgs ran no check phase and the three test modules had never executed. Tests now run in-build: 20 passed. Deployed and verified -- hermes-mcp active, NRestarts=0, /sse returns HTTP 200.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Surfaced by the same 2026-08-31 flake update (generation 2611).

SYMPTOM: hermes-mcp.service exits 1 on every start, 41 restarts before the
rollback.
  File ".../hermes_mcp/server.py", line NNN, in build_app
      return Starlette(
  TypeError: Starlette.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'on_startup'

CAUSE: the flake update bumped Starlette to a version that removed the deprecated
on_startup/on_shutdown constructor arguments. hermes-mcp 0.2.0 still passes
on_startup.

FIX: migrate build_app() to Starlette's lifespan API -- an async context manager
passed as Starlette(lifespan=...) -- instead of on_startup. This is our own
package (pkgs/), so the change is ours to make rather than an upstream wait.

BLAST RADIUS: the Hermes MCP SSE bridge. HermesMcpBridgeDown fires while it is
out. Hermes itself and its microVM are unaffected.

WORKAROUND IN PLACE: rolled back to generation 2610 with the older Starlette.

* DONE [#D] PostgreSQL cache-hit dips below 85% in bursts; the rule aggregates all databases so one busy DB masks which
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-06u
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 09:07]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-15 Sat 23:15]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-15 Sat 23:15]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

PostgreSQLCacheHitLow went pending 2026-08-15 ~08:00 and has never fired in 7d,
so the 1h "for" clause has been doing its job. Investigated while it was pending.

Measured, hourly aggregate over 12h (threshold 0.85):
  21:06 99.6 | 22:06 83.0 | 23:06 99.5 | 00:06 99.6 | 01:06 99.5
  02:06 49.9 | 03:06 80.8 | 04:06 99.5 | 05:06 99.6 | 06:06 99.6
  07:06 99.5 | 08:06 81.5 | 09:06 81.8

So the baseline is ~99.5% and it drops below threshold in bursts: the 02:06 dip
is the nightly PostgreSQL backup (expected), and the current one is driven
entirely by hass, reading ~208 blocks/s from disk at 63% hit ratio while every
other database sits at 96-100%.

NOT a stuck query. pg_stat_activity on hass shows 3 idle + 1 active, max age 13s.
The reads come from many short queries against a large hot table:
  states 1238 MB (3.6M live tuples, 33M index scans), statistics_short_term 516 MB,
  events 460 MB, state_attributes 235 MB, statistics 231 MB; 2597 MB total.
event_types shows 2.0M seq scans but has 26 rows, so that is optimal, not a defect.

TWO READINGS, and choosing between them is a judgement call rather than a fix:

1. Genuine capacity: the hass recorder working set exceeds what PostgreSQL caches,
   so any busy HA period goes to disk. Would be addressed by shared_buffers /
   effective_cache_size tuning, or by shortening recorder retention.

2. Alert shape: the rule sums blks_hit/blks_read across ALL databases, so it is a
   single global ratio. One busy database at 63% drags the aggregate under 0.85
   even while eleven others are at 96-100%. A per-datname rule would say which
   database is actually degraded instead of reporting a fleet-wide number that no
   single database experiences.

Possible aggravator worth checking together with nixos-h2u: that plug flaps
off<->unavailable ~117x/day, and every flap writes recorder rows.

Not acted on. Tuning PostgreSQL memory and changing an alert's shape are both
choices with real alternatives, so they want a decision rather than an autonomous
edit.

** Close Reason

BOTH HALVES DONE, per John's choice.

1. THE RULE IS NOW PER-DATABASE. PostgreSQLCacheHitLow summed over every database,
   producing one cluster-wide number that no individual database experienced:
   measured live at 81-82% for two hours while hass alone sat at 63% reading ~208
   blocks/s from disk and eleven other databases were at 96-100%. It named no
   culprit. It now groups by (datname) and the annotation reports which database
   is degraded.

   A BUG I INTRODUCED AND CAUGHT BEFORE COMMITTING: the first version wrapped the
   denominator in clamp_min(..., 1) to avoid NaN, which turns a database doing NO
   I/O into 0/1 = 0% -- comfortably under 0.85. template0 fired on the very first
   live test. The bare division is correct instead: 0/0 is NaN and NaN comparisons
   are false, so idle databases drop out by themselves. A '> 1 block/s' gate then
   excludes the near-idle case, sized against live traffic (hass 81 blocks/s,
   postgres 113 blocks/s, template0 exactly 0).

   Verified after switching: promtool 19 rules SUCCESS, the rule is loaded with
   by (datname), and 0 series are firing.

2. MEMORY RAISED. shared_buffers 2GB -> 8GB, effective_cache_size 4GB -> 24GB on a
   62GB host. hass is 2.6GB with a 1.2GB states table and 3.6M live tuples, so its
   hot set alone was a large fraction of a 2GB pool shared with ~26 databases.

   Both are LIVE, verified with SHOW: shared_buffers=8GB, effective_cache_size=24GB,
   and pg_settings reports 0 rows pending_restart.

   CORRECTION recorded in the module: I had commented that shared_buffers would not
   take effect until an explicit restart, because a switch only reloads PostgreSQL.
   That was wrong for this setting -- the switch DID restart postgresql.service (new
   MainPID, ActiveEnterTimestamp moved), so the value went live immediately and the
   ~26 databases plus dependents were bounced as part of a routine switch. The
   comment now says so, and warns that any future edit to this setting belongs in a
   planned window.

#+begin_example
Done. The repository no longer describes Nagios as a live system.

RESULT: zero /nagios/i in any .nix file except one deliberate line recording that the HA
token is owned by root because that user was removed; zero across modules/monitoring/alerts
and modules/monitoring/loki-rules. What remains in docs/ is dated historical records
annotated with [Nagios removed 2026-08-19] rather than rewritten, which is the right
treatment for an audit trail.

FUNCTIONAL INTEGRITY, proven mechanically rather than by eye. The audit reconstructed each
modified .nix into a depth-accurate mirror tree and diffed `nix-instantiate --parse`
output, in which comments do not survive: 18 of 23 modified .nix files are parse-tree
byte-identical, and the 5 that differ do so only inside string literals that are
documentation (config-file comments, an annotation description, a README). All 13 modified
YAMLs load structurally identical except exactly TWO annotation descriptions, both
authorised. Zero changes to any expr, for:, threshold, label, group interval, target list,
option value or import. All 240 .nix files in the repo parse. blackbox-monitoring.nix's 78
probe target strings diff empty.

CORRECTED RATHER THAN STRUCK, which was the point of doing this carefully:
,  * aide.nix and aide-metrics.nix now cite TextfileCollectorStaleDaily. The old claim that
    aide was in NEITHER tier was already false before this removal. They also now
    distinguish aide.prom (on the daily allowlist) from aide_result.prom (not matched by
    the anchored regex, so it falls to the 14-day backstop) -- a real distinction that a
    strike-through would have destroyed.
,  * BlackboxICMPIoTDeviceDown's header was a live instruction that INVERTED on removal:
    "this mirrors the Nagios coverage, do not remove the Nagios side". It now says the
    true and opposite thing -- this is the only IoT ping coverage, do not drop it as
    redundant. Its email-visible annotation lost the false mirror clause and kept
    "warning-only because these devices sleep by design", which is what tells whoever is
    woken not to treat a sleeping battery device as an outage.
,  * open-webui.nix's disabled healthcheck kept its justification, repointed to what
    actually provides liveness now.
,  * parked-alerts.nix survives intact with parkedAlertFiles and its assertion.

A MISTAKE I MADE AND THE AUDIT CAUGHT. Sweeping core/system.nix I deleted "# Nagios runs
podman commands every few seconds to check container health" and left "# Without this,
generates ~5,800 sudo log entries per day" with no antecedent -- exactly the
lost-reasoning failure the brief warned against. Repaired: the comment now states the
surviving driver (root-run container health checks every 30s), and flags that the
~5,800/day figure was measured against the retired system and should be re-measured before
anyone decides the rule still earns its keep. The rule was never the problem; its
justification was.

ALSO FIXED HERE: nixos-eb9, the header claiming TWO textfile cadence tiers when there are
three. Now names all three plus the scrape-error and coverage backstops, and says why it
matters -- landing a collector in the wrong tier is how one ends up flapping or silently
unwatched.

STILL OPEN, filed separately: nixos-zpx (open-webui health rules that can never fire) and

* DONE [#C] nix flake update breaks build: pi-coding-agent 0.84.3 vitest sourcemap error
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-0zi
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 22:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-29 Sat 12:58]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-29 Sat 12:58]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed upstream in nix-config a6ad28de: pi-coding-agent 0.84.3 -> 0.84.4. Verified on vulcan 2026-08-29: the source-build output ssn7yyla...-pi-coding-agent-source-build-0.84.4 is realised, its vitest log shows all test files passing with no unhandled sourcemap error, agent-cat-pi-extension-0.1.0 is in the running closure, and the system toplevel (223rwbwx...) evaluates and is running from the updated lock. flake.lock no longer needs the 943c8e75 workaround pin.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Running 'nix flake update' moves nix-config and nix-config-ai from 943c8e75 to 448d3b18, which pulls pi-coding-agent 0.84.3. Its vitest suite dies with 'SyntaxError: Unexpected token, not valid JSON' in convert-source-map/index.js:83 via extractSourcemapFromFile -- vitest tries to parse a sourcemap comment out of what appears to be a binary file. All 574 tests PASS; it is the unhandled error that fails the test file, and so the derivation. The failure cascades: pi-coding-agent-source-build-0.84.3 -> agent-cat-pi-extension-0.1.0 -> home-manager-path -> user-environment -> etc -> nixos-system-vulcan, so the whole switch fails. Neither package is referenced from /etc/nixos; both arrive via the nix-config/nix-config-ai inputs (git+ssh://gitea/johnw/nix-config). WORKAROUND IN PLACE 2026-08-28: flake.lock reverted to 943c8e75 for both inputs, with only the 'secrets' input re-locked. This means the tree is pinned to older nix-config until fixed upstream. Real fix belongs in the nix-config repo: either exclude the binary fixture from vitest's sourcemap scan, or set doCheck=false for pi-coding-agent.

* DONE [#E] certificate-validation exits 1 and fails daily whenever any cert is inside its 30-day window
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-19n
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-27 Thu 08:45]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-27 Thu 15:53]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-27 Thu 15:53]
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

NOT URGENT and it self-resolves on 2026-09-01, but it is STRUCTURAL rather than a one-off, which is why it is worth recording.

BEHAVIOUR: certificate-validation.service exits 1 whenever its sweep finds any certificate needing attention, and systemd therefore records "Failed with result 'exit-code'". Today's run reported exactly that:
  Certificate Status Summary:
    ✓ Valid: 46 certificates
    ⚠ Warning (30d): 1 certificates
  Attention Required:
    ⚠ nginx/vulcan.lan: expires in 26 days
  certificate-validation.service: Main process exited, code=exited, status=1/FAILURE

The unit did its job perfectly. It is the exit status that turns a successful report into a unit failure.

IT IS ALREADY A STREAK: clean on 08-21 and 08-22, then 'exit-code' on 08-23, 08-24, 08-25, 08-26 and 08-27. The transition on 08-23 is the day vulcan.lan crossed the 30-day threshold, which is also when CertificateExpiringSoon began firing (2026-08-23T11:36). It will keep failing daily until the Sep 1 renewal -- about ten consecutive days.

WHY IT RECURS STRUCTURALLY: this host holds 47 certificates. Every time ANY of them enters its 30-day window, this unit fails daily until that certificate is renewed. So the pattern repeats indefinitely, not just for vulcan.lan.

WHY IT MATTERS AT ALL: the condition is already covered properly by the CertificateExpiringSoon Prometheus alert, so the unit failure is duplicate signalling on a channel (systemd unit state) that is meant to mean "this unit is broken". It lands in the routine "any service failures in the last N hours" sweep and buys an investigation each time. Ten days of daily false failures is exactly how an operator learns to skim past failed units.

CARE REQUIRED IF FIXING -- do NOT simply add SuccessExitStatus=1. The script very likely uses exit 1 for genuine faults as well (unreadable cert, parse failure, missing file), and conflating "a cert is expiring soon" with "validation itself broke" would hide the second. The right shape is a distinct exit code per outcome, e.g. 0 = all valid, 1 = validation error, 2 = warnings present, with SuccessExitStatus=2. That needs a read of certs/ or scripts/ to confirm the current code paths before touching anything.

NOT ACTED ON: it self-resolves at the Sep 1 renewal, and the fix needs the exit-code audit above rather than a quick suppression.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
FIXED and verified 2026-08-27, and the investigation found a SECOND, more serious bug than the one filed.

THE FILED ISSUE: certificate-validation.service exited 1 whenever any certificate sat inside the 30-day window, so systemd recorded a unit failure every day from 08-23 (when nginx/vulcan.lan crossed 30 days) until renewal. Duplicate signalling on the channel that means 'this unit is broken'.

FIX: SuccessExitStatus=1 in modules/services/certificate-automation.nix. The script already had clean severity-separated exit codes -- 2 = expired/critical, 1 = warning, 0 = healthy -- so my earlier worry that 'exit 1 probably also covers genuine faults' was simply wrong; I had not read the script. Exit 2 is deliberately NOT in SuccessExitStatus, so a real fault still fails the unit loudly.

THE BUG I FOUND WHILE READING IT: the CORRUPTED OR INVALID and DATE PARSE ERROR paths appended to attention_required and then 'return 1' WITHOUT incrementing any counter. Since the exit condition only tested expired_certs/critical_certs/warning_certs, an unreadable certificate produced EXIT 0 whenever the others were healthy -- the report printed the problem in 'Attention Required' while the exit status said everything was fine. Silent false negative, and the opposite failure from the noisy one being fixed.

Added an invalid_certs counter, incremented on both paths, reported as 'Unreadable: N certificates', and included in the exit-2 condition so an unparseable certificate is now a hard fault.

VERIFIED:
 - Script run live: exit 1 with 46 valid / 1 warning (nginx/vulcan.lan, 26 days), bash -n clean.
 - Exit logic positive-controlled: unreadable -> 2, all-healthy -> 0.
 - After the switch: SuccessExitStatus=1 applied; the unit ran with ExecMainStatus=1 and Result=success, with ZERO 'Failed with result' lines where it had logged one daily since 08-23.

Note the resulting state is Result=success with ExecMainStatus=1, which is normally the signature of a masked failure. Here it is intentional and documented in both the script and the unit.
#+end_example

* DONE [#C] git-annex sync fails with SQLite ErrorReadOnly: annex databases were zimit-owned at 0644
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-1d0
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 19:19]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 19:19]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-20 Thu 19:19]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed by chowning /tank/Archives/.git to johnw; full analysis in the description. git annex sync now completes cleanly, fsck reports 25 ok / 0 failures, and kiwix-serve still reads all sampled ZIMs through their symlinks.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
`git-annex sync` failed with "SQLite3 returned ErrorReadOnly ... attempt to write a
readonly database" on .git/annex/fsck/<uuid>/fsckdb/db. Same root cause as the fsck
failure (nixos-dhm), different file: the annex DATABASES, not the objects.

WHY THE EARLIER FIX DID NOT COVER IT. nixos-dhm repaired the modes of
.git/annex/objects, which was enough for fsck. It did not touch the sqlite
databases, and those fail for a stricter reason:

  .git/annex/fsck/<uuid>/fsckdb/db   was zimit:johnw 0644
  .git/annex/reposize/db/db          was johnw:johnw 0644  <- worked fine

At 0644 the group has READ only, so johnw could not write the fsck db at all. And
he could not chmod it writable either, because chmod requires OWNERSHIP. The
reposize db has identical 0644 permissions and works, purely because johnw owns it
and git-annex can thaw it. That contrast is the proof: it is ownership, not mode.

Mode repair would NOT have been a durable fix here the way it was for objects.
git-annex actively freezes and thaws these databases on every write, so any mode I
set would be re-frozen and then un-thawable on the next run.

FIXED: chown -R johnw:johnw /tank/Archives/.git

That is the correct ownership rather than a workaround — the repository metadata
belongs to the operator. Verified zimit has no claim on it before acting: the only
git/annex references in the zimit module are comments I wrote, and .git is absent
from zimit-job-runner's ReadWritePaths (/var/lib/zimit /tank/Archives/ZIM
/tank/Archives/work), so the runner literally cannot write there. 740 of 759
inodes were zimit-owned; 0 remain.

REGRESSION-TESTED, because kiwix-serve runs AS zimit and reads ZIMs by following
symlinks into .git/annex/objects:
,  * zimit read 6/6 sampled ZIMs through their symlinks after the chown
,  * kiwix-serve stayed active/running
  This works because objects are mode 444 and their directories 555, so "other"
  retains r-x regardless of owner.

VERIFIED:
,  * `git annex sync` now completes: "recording state in git", "nothing to commit,
    working tree clean", ok — no sqlite error.
,  * `git annex fsck --fast`: 25 files ok, 0 failures.
,  * All annex databases now uniformly johnw:johnw.
,  * Object modes unchanged by the chown: 25 at 444, 0 wrong.

DURABLE: nothing re-applies ownership under .git. The two tmpfiles rules cover only
ZIM and work; the handoff ExecStopPost touches only ZIM_DIR/*.zim. Pre-change
ownership of all 759 inodes was recorded to scratch before acting, so the operation
is precisely reversible.

RECORDED IN THE CODE: modules/services/zimit.nix now states the full ownership
contract for /tank/Archives in one place, including an explicit "do not chown -R
this tree to a single user" with what breaks in each direction — making it all
johnw breaks the crawler, making it all zimit breaks git-annex. Both have now
happened.
#+end_example

* DONE [#B] 569 ZFS snapshots destroyed by a zfs-prune-snapshots m/M unit confusion; PG backup history cut to ~24h
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-2ys
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 11:13]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 12:57]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-20 Thu 12:57]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

On 2026-08-19 at 10:22 PDT, 569 ZFS snapshots were destroyed across 8 datasets by a
single interactive command. tank/Backups/PostgreSQL went from 30-daily/8-weekly/
12-monthly retention to ~24 hours of history. The snapshots are unrecoverable.

WHAT RAN: `sudo zfs-prune-snapshots 1m tank/Backups/PostgreSQL tank/Backups/TechnitiumDNS
tank/Home tank/Models/oMLX tank/Pictures tank/Video tank/work/positron`, from an
interactive TTY (pts/6, PWD=/tank/Backups/Contracts/Positron/nfs), no -n dry-run.

THE TRAP: in zfs-prune-snapshots 1.5.0, the suffix `m` means MINUTES and `M` means
MONTHS (source line 207: `m) seconds=$((num * 60))`). So `1m` pruned everything older
than SIXTY SECONDS, not one month. Confirmed by snapshots taken 22 minutes earlier
being destroyed.

PER-DATASET DESTROY COUNTS from zpool history (on-disk pool metadata, immune to
journal rotation): Video 78, Video/Zoom 76, Pictures 76, Home 76, TechnitiumDNS 75,
Models/oMLX 69, work/positron 65, Backups/PostgreSQL 54.

WHY POSTGRESQL IS THE SHARP ONE: modules/services/postgresql-backup.nix:12 states ZFS
snapshots are the ONLY history layer -- dated tarballs were deliberately removed. And
modules/storage/backups.nix:134 puts "PostgreSQL" in backupExcludes, so it is NOT in
the B2 restic repo either. So for ~24h of wall time the PG mirror had no recoverable
history beyond the live copy. Depth is rebuilding at one snapshot per hour and will
reach full archival depth ~30 days from 2026-08-19.

NOT SANOID: the destroyed set includes an autosnap_..._yearly snapshot only 3 weeks
old, which the archival template (5 yearly / 12 monthly) would never expire. 569
destroys in 7 minutes confined to exactly the named datasets is not a retention pass.
Controls confirm retention is healthy elsewhere: tank/Backups/Git 75 snapshots back to
2025-11-11, tank/Documents 77 back to 2025-03-25.

MONITORING GAP -- FIXED 2026-08-20, and this is the durable part. Nothing detected
this for over a day. ZFSSnapshotStale tests only the age of the NEWEST snapshot, and
zfs_newest_snapshot_timestamp_seconds is one series PER POOL, so it structurally
cannot see history loss: sanoid took a fresh hourly snapshot minutes after the prune
and the gauge never twitched. Added zfs_oldest_snapshot_timestamp_seconds and
zfs_snapshot_count per dataset (60 datasets, verified scraped) plus alert
ZFSSnapshotHistoryTruncated, which fires when the oldest snapshot jumps forward by
more than 7 days inside an hour -- impossible through normal aging.

FOR JOHN, and the only reason this stays open: nothing here is recoverable and nothing
further is actionable by me. Worth knowing (a) the m/M trap, since the same keystroke
would do it again, and (b) that PG's recovery depth is thin until ~2026-09-18.

** Close Reason

Closing: the loss is unrecoverable, the monitoring gap it exposed is fixed and
verified, and recovery is measurably on track. Nothing further is actionable.

RECOVERY VERIFIED 2026-08-20, one day on. tank/Backups/PostgreSQL snapshot mix:
  25 hourly (rolling, capped by the archival template)
   2 daily   <- was 1 the day after the prune; this is the accumulation
   1 weekly, 1 monthly, 1 yearly
sanoid last ran 2 minutes before the check with Result=success. Total count reads
flat at 30 and oldest still reads Aug 19 11:00, which looks like no progress and is
not: hourlies roll at a fixed cap while dailies accumulate one per day, and the
oldest cannot move until the Aug 19 daily itself ages out at 30 days. Counting by
retention CLASS is the instrument that shows progress; counting snapshots is not.

Full archival depth (30 daily / 8 weekly / 12 monthly) returns ~2026-09-18.

THE DURABLE FIX IS SHIPPED (commit 08e1470cf). Nothing was watching history depth,
only recency -- ZFSSnapshotStale reads the NEWEST snapshot and is one series per
POOL, so a 569-snapshot deletion was invisible while sanoid's next hourly kept the
gauge healthy. Added zfs_oldest_snapshot_timestamp_seconds and zfs_snapshot_count
per dataset (60 datasets scraped) plus ZFSSnapshotHistoryTruncated, firing when the
oldest snapshot jumps forward >7d inside an hour, which aging cannot do. Rule reads
health=ok with empty lastError and its metric returns 60 series, so it is not a dead
rule. It cannot be proven to FIRE without destroying snapshots, which I did not do.

THE TRAP IS RECORDED WHERE IT WILL BE READ, not just here: the alert's own
description tells whoever it wakes that in zfs-prune-snapshots `m` means MINUTES and
`M` means MONTHS, and that `1m` prunes everything older than sixty seconds.

RESIDUAL RISK, stated plainly rather than closed over: until ~2026-09-18 the
PostgreSQL mirror's recoverable history is shallower than designed, and it has no
second layer -- it is a single live rsync mirror, excluded from the B2 restic repo
(backups.nix:134), with ZFS snapshots as its only history. A data-loss event needing
a restore older than the current depth would not be servable. That is a known and
now-monitored exposure, not an open task.

* DONE [#D] ResticNoRecentSnapshot fired 4 times in 30 days on merely-slow backups: 30h threshold inside the normal band
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-3yx
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 04:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 04:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-21 Fri 04:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Retuned 30h -> 40h, chosen by replaying candidate thresholds against 30 days of real data: 30h breached 5 fifteen-minute samples, 36h/40h/48h breached zero. 40h keeps 9.8h of headroom above the worst observed normal (30.2h) while staying ~8h below the ~48h a genuinely missed daily run reaches. Verified live: health=ok, expr shows 144000, selects all 10 repositories, and an always-true threshold returns 10 series so the comparison is not dead.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

ResticNoRecentSnapshot's 30h threshold sat INSIDE the normal operating band and
produced four spurious events in 30 days, every one for a backup that was slow
rather than failed.

FOUND BY: a proactive check during an otherwise-empty cycle. Nothing was firing;
this is a rule that had been quietly skating its own edge for at least a month.

THE OLD RATIONALE WAS REASONED, NOT MEASURED. Its comment derived 30h from the
intended schedule -- "backups run daily at 02:00, metrics every 6h, so 30h leaves
a 2h buffer". Reality does not match: the repos back up in SEQUENCE, so a slow run
pushes every repo behind it and peak age drifts well past 26h.

MEASURED over 30 days:
  worst observed peak age    30.2h (Home) -- ABOVE the 30h threshold
  weekly peaks               29.8 / 26.8 / 28.8 / 29.8 / 27.8 h
  current spread             23.2h (Video) to 26.6h (Audio) across 10 repos
The peaks oscillate and do NOT trend upward, so this is a threshold that was always
too tight rather than a symptom being silenced -- worth distinguishing before
raising any alert threshold.

IT DID NOT STAY QUIET. Over the same 30 days ResticNoRecentSnapshot:
  FIRED   Home     07-24 10:00, ~1h
  pending Audio    08-06 08:15
  pending doc      08-07 08:30
  pending Backups  08-11 09:30
Only the `for: 10m` kept the other three from paging.

INSTRUMENT NOTE, because it nearly hid this: my first ALERTS query used step=3600
and returned only ResticRepositorySizeGrowing, i.e. "never fired". At step=1800 the
Home firing appeared, and at step=900 all four events did. A query_range step wider
than the event silently drops it -- so "no ALERTS samples" is only meaningful at a
step finer than the alert's own lifetime.

FIXED: threshold 108000s -> 144000s (30h -> 40h), chosen by replay rather than
judgement. Counting breaching 15-minute samples over 30d:
  30h -> 5 breaches     36h -> 0     40h -> 0     48h -> 0
Anything from 36h up removes the noise. 40h is the balance point: 9.8h above the
worst observed normal, and ~8h BELOW the ~48h a genuinely missed daily run reaches
before its next attempt, so a real failure is still caught with time to act. 48h
would only catch it at the boundary.

VERIFIED after switch: rule health=ok, lastError empty, state=inactive, expr shows
144000. NOT a dead rule -- it selects all 10 repositories, and an always-true
threshold (> 0) returns 10 series, so the comparison mechanics work.

NOT VERIFIED, and cannot be without a real failed backup: that it fires on an
actual miss. What is verified is that it no longer fires on normal operation and
that the metric and comparison are live.

* DONE [#E] restic_last_check_timestamp_seconds HELP text claims it is an integrity-check time; it is the collector pass time
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-4ti
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: docs
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 20:51]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 20:51]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-21 Fri 20:51]
:CLOSE_REASON: HELP text corrected to state it is the collector pass time, not an integrity check. Verified the deployed script carries it, and that all three restic rules stay health=ok/inactive. No monitoring defect existed — ResticMetricsStale uses the metric correctly and documents it as a collector-freshness guard, and integrity-check age is separately guarded by ResticIntegrityCheckStale at 8 days (currently 4.6 days). The metric name is left alone deliberately: renaming would break that rule and any dashboard for a cosmetic gain.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

restic_last_check_timestamp_seconds is NOT a check timestamp. Its HELP text said
"Timestamp of the last check operation"; it actually records the restic-metrics
COLLECTOR PASS time. The name says the same wrong thing.

HOW IT MISLEADS, measured live -- the two metrics differ by two orders of magnitude:
  restic_last_check_timestamp_seconds        0.8 h old   (collector pass)
  restic_integrity_check_timestamp_seconds 110.1 h old   (the real `restic check`)
Reading the first one, all 10 repositories appear to have been integrity-checked
minutes ago. They were checked 4.6 days ago, on the weekly Monday 06:30 timer, and
restic-check.service's own last exit (2026-08-17 06:43:58) agrees with the SECOND
metric, not the first.

restic-metrics.nix:171 writes $TIMESTAMP -- the collector's per-repo run time --
into it, so the value is correct for what it is and only the documentation lied.

NO MONITORING DEFECT, checked before assuming one. The single rule that reads this
metric is ResticMetricsStale (storage.yaml:539), and it is entirely correct: its
comment states it is "its dedicated freshness guard" for restic.prom, sizes 8h as
"6h cadence + ~2h grace", explains why max() collapses the per-repo series, and its
summary reads "last collector pass". Whoever wrote it knew exactly what the metric
contains. Integrity-check AGE is separately and correctly guarded by
ResticIntegrityCheckStale (local-backup.yaml:144) at 8 days against
restic_integrity_check_timestamp_seconds -- currently 4.6 days, within threshold.

So the fix is documentation only: corrected the HELP string to say
"Timestamp of the last restic-metrics COLLECTOR PASS for this repository (NOT a
restic integrity check)". The metric name itself is left alone deliberately --
renaming it would break ResticMetricsStale and any dashboard, for a cosmetic gain.

WHY IT WAS WORTH FIXING: this cost me a real detour. Seeing "0.0 days since last
integrity check" for all 10 repos while the weekly check timer had last run four
days earlier read as either a broken timer or a lying metric, and the HELP text
actively pointed the wrong way. A future reader with a genuine backup concern would
be misled at exactly the wrong moment.

VERIFIED: the deployed collector script
(/nix/store/y3yh07ji91318rmn1jyxc736r5a6pihg-collect-restic-metrics) carries the
corrected HELP; ResticMetricsStale, ResticIntegrityCheckStale and ResticCheckFailed
all read health=ok/state=inactive after the switch; and the two ages remain
correctly distinct at 0.8h vs 110.1h.

NOTE the textfile itself still shows the old HELP until restic-metrics completes
its current pass -- it queries B2 across 10 repositories and legitimately takes
minutes (6h cadence, 8h alert threshold). The deployed script is the artifact that
matters and it is correct.

ALSO CHECKED CLEAN THIS CYCLE: 144/144 Prometheus scrape targets up with none ever
down in 30 days; all 10 restic repositories pass restic_check_success.

* DONE [#E] Correction: the 407 'unaudited' compound rules ARE covered — prometheus-rule-audit checks all 484
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-514
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: docs
:CREATED:  [2026-08-22 Sat 04:45]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-22 Sat 04:46]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-22 Sat 04:46]
:CLOSE_REASON: Correction recorded. prometheus-rule-audit covers all 484 rules continuously (checked 8 min ago, up=1, rules_checked=484 matching the live count) with 0 dead, 0 stale, 0 error, 0 group overruns. All 5 dormant rules verified individually as correctly classified — including GrafanaLoginFailureBurst, which has 0 samples in 90d but 1 within 365d and is genuinely dormant rather than dead. The two audits are complementary: metric-existence across all 484, threshold-sanity across the 77 simple ones.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

CORRECTION to nixos-bkf, which implied a coverage gap that does not exist.

nixos-bkf recorded a threshold audit covering "77 of 484 rules... the other 407 are
compound expressions the mechanical pass cannot parse", and framed that as a partial
audit. For the THRESHOLD dimension that is accurate. But it reads as though 407
rules are unexamined, and for the dimension that matters most on this host -- can a
rule fire at all -- they are not.

modules/monitoring/services/prometheus-rule-audit.nix already audits ALL of them,
continuously, and I did not know it existed before building my own partial version.
Measured this cycle:
  prometheus_rule_audit_up                      1
  last run                                      8.2 minutes ago
  rules_checked_total                           484   <- equals the rules actually
                                                         loaded, so no truncation
  dead_rules_total                              0
  stale_rules_total                             0
  error_rules_total                             0
  group_overrun_total                           0
  dormant_rules_total                           5     (explicitly informational)

It distinguishes DEAD (selects a metric the TSDB has NEVER seen -- the 123-rule
failure class this repo remediated) from DORMANT (metric known to the TSDB, no
series right now -- on-demand counters). That distinction is the whole point and it
is drawn correctly.

VERIFIED THE CLASSIFICATION RATHER THAN TRUSTING IT, on all five dormant rules:
  ZimitCrawlStalled            zimit_running_job_log_age_seconds -- present in 31 of
                               31 samples across the llm-primer crawl window
                               (08-20 18:14 to 08-21 09:14) and absent since. Live
                               when it can be, dormant otherwise: exactly as I built
                               it. Direct proof that dormant != dead.
  GitWorkspaceRepoStale        git_workspace_repo_age_seconds -- 101 samples in 30d,
                               absent now (the shared-textfile behaviour documented
                               in nixos-mfh).
  StockTraderStaleQuotesRejected  1 sample in 30d.
  PrometheusRuleCannotFire     25 samples in 30d (the audit's own metric).
  GrafanaLoginFailureBurst     the interesting one. ZERO samples in 30d AND 90d,
                               which looked like a misclassification -- but the name
                               IS in the __name__ index and there is 1 sample within
                               365d. A Grafana failed-login counter only materialises
                               when someone actually fails a login. Dormant is
                               correct; dead would have been wrong.

SO: the alert surface is fully and continuously audited for dead rules, 0 exist, and
the two audits are complementary rather than overlapping --
  prometheus-rule-audit : does the metric exist?          all 484 rules
  the nixos-bkf pass    : is the threshold inside normal?  77 simple rules
Only the second is partial, and only in the sense that thresholds cannot be checked
mechanically on compound expressions.

LESSON WORTH KEEPING: I built a mechanical rule audit without first checking whether
the repo already had one. It did, and a better one. Check modules/monitoring/services/
for existing collectors before writing an ad-hoc equivalent.

No configuration changed; nothing here is a defect.

* DONE [#B] P1: host cannot be rebuilt -- nix-config sets a starship option home-manager 25.11 lacks
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-5eo
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-18 Tue 19:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-19 Wed 00:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-19 Wed 00:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed by the starship presets compat shim (1b4ae6b7f), verified byte-identical to the pre-breakage config. The host is still blocked, but by the independent nixos-chl.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
THE HOST CANNOT BE REBUILT. Every `/etc/nixos/build` invocation fails during
evaluation, so no configuration change of any kind can be deployed until this is
resolved. Found 2026-08-18 during the health cycle, when a routine switch failed.

    error: The option `home-manager.users.johnw.programs.starship.presets'
    does not exist. Definition values:
      - In `<nix-config>/config/johnw.nix':
          [ "nerd-font-symbols" ]

THE MISMATCH. nix-config's config/johnw.nix line 232 sets

    programs.starship.presets = [ "nerd-font-symbols" ];

`programs.starship.presets` does not exist in the home-manager this host pins
(release-25.11, rev 3ee51fbdac8c). nix-config evidently tracks a newer
home-manager where that option was added.

This is the SAME STRUCTURAL FAILURE as nixos-sil (2026-08-15): vulcan consumes
nix-config with `flake = false`, so nix-config's own inputs never apply here and
version skew between the two surfaces as an eval error with no local cause. The
last such incident also took the host down completely.

TIMELINE, established from git and the running system:
    2026-08-17 23:25   last successful build (running system)
    2026-08-18 14:45   b4ae2f94f  flake.lock: Update  -> nix-config 7e92da92
    2026-08-18 14:59   beef6efe9  flake.lock: Update  -> nix-config aa1b9b89
    2026-08-18 ~15:2x  first failing build

CONFIRMED NOT CAUSED BY LOCAL WORK. The failure reproduces at HEAD with the
working tree stashed, and the health-cycle changes in progress contain zero
references to starship.

BISECTED, and one fix is PROVEN. Reverting nix-config only to 7e92da92 (the
14:45 rev) is NOT sufficient -- it fails identically. Pinning it to
66eaf03ee29d7bb150528707d9fa4755debddfef, the rev in effect before BOTH of
today's updates, evaluates cleanly (exit 0, drvPath produced). So the breaking
change is in nix-config between 66eaf03e and 7e92da92.

THE OPTIONS, all of which are John's call since two of them touch his own
nix-config repo:

  1. Fix it in nix-config -- guard or remove the `presets` line, or make it
     conditional on the option existing. Correct in principle: nix-config should
     work against the home-manager its consumers pin. Fastest if John is in that
     repo anyway, and needs no change here at all.

  2. Pin nix-config back to 66eaf03ee29d in this repo's flake.lock. PROVEN to
     restore buildability, surgical, and trivially reversible. Undoes today's
     two updates, so any other change they carried is lost until nix-config is
     fixed.

  3. Move this host's home-manager off release-25.11 to a rev that has
     `programs.starship.presets`. Matches nix-config's expectation, but changes
     the home-manager version for all 14 home-manager users at once, on a host
     whose stateVersion is 25.11. Highest blast radius of the three.

Recommendation: 1 if John is willing to touch nix-config now, 2 as the immediate
unblock otherwise. NOT done autonomously -- 2 reverts a commit John made
deliberately 20 minutes before it was found, and 1 and 3 are outside this repo
or change a pinned toolchain version.

CONSEQUENCE WHILE OPEN: the four verified monitoring/permissions changes from
this cycle are committed but CANNOT ACTIVATE, and neither can anything else.
#+end_example

:MODIFIED: [2026-08-14 Fri 19:03]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-14 Fri 19:03]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

One file in the Documents Syncthing folder cannot converge because hera and
vulcan disagree about its capitalisation:

  remote  JumpDesktop/Viewer/Servers/Computer - hera - DFD7C7D7-...-052CD807E288.jump
  local   JumpDesktop/Viewer/Servers/Computer - Hera - DFD7C7D7-...-052CD807E288.jump

Syncthing's message: "uses different upper or lowercase characters ... change the
casing on either side to match the other".

Same file, same UUID, differing only in hera vs Hera. macOS is case-insensitive
so hera can hold either form; vulcan's ZFS is case-sensitive and will not create
a second entry differing only in case. Syncthing refuses to guess which side is
authoritative, which is correct behaviour.

NOT a permissions problem. Verified after the ACL mask repair (nixos-g9y) that
the containing directory has mask::rwx with user:syncthing:rwx effective.

IMPACT is negligible and non-blocking: a single 2751-byte JumpDesktop connection
profile. The folder sits at state=idle with the other 27411 files synced and
needBytes=2751.

FIX is a rename on one side, e.g. on vulcan:
  mv '/tank/Documents/JumpDesktop/Viewer/Servers/Computer - Hera - DFD7C7D7-3E37-4EFC-9F9C-052CD807E288.jump' \
     '/tank/Documents/JumpDesktop/Viewer/Servers/Computer - hera - DFD7C7D7-3E37-4EFC-9F9C-052CD807E288.jump'

Left for John: renaming touches the content of his JumpDesktop configuration,
and either side could be the intended spelling. Not an autonomous call.

** Close Reason

Resolved by renaming to lowercase 'hera' at John's direction.

CORRECTION to the original diagnosis: I attributed the conflict to vulcan's ZFS
being case-sensitive. It is not. tank/Documents is casesensitivity=insensitive
with normalization=formD and utf8only=on -- deliberately configured to match
macOS. The dataset therefore stores ONE entry, spelled 'Computer - Hera - ...',
and cannot hold a second differing only in case, which is what Syncthing needed
in order to accept hera's 'Computer - hera - ...'.

A plain mv failed with 'are the same file' for exactly that reason; the rename
needed two steps via a temporary name.

Verified fully converged afterwards:
    needFiles 0   needBytes 0   pullErrors 0
    localFiles 27412 == globalFiles 27412   state=idle
against 1 file / 2751 bytes / 1 pull error before.

* DONE [#C] gitea-runner fails 203/EXEC: act_runner binary renamed to gitea-runner in 3.1.0
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-5v4
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 21:11]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Caused by the userPkgs routing (nixos-7bp), not an upstream nixpkgs bug. Stable 0.2.13 ships bin/act_runner exactly as the stable module expects. ExecStart now resolves to an act_runner that exists; unit active. The overlay symlink shim was reverted as the wrong fix.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Surfaced by 'nix flake update && nixos-rebuild switch' on 2026-08-31 20:27 (generation 2611), which failed with exit 4.

SYMPTOM: gitea-runner-org\x2dbuilder.service exits 203/EXEC immediately.
  ExecStart=/nix/store/1hswpq...-gitea-actions-runner-3.1.0/bin/act_runner daemon --config ...
  Main process exited, code=exited, status=203/EXEC

CAUSE: 203/EXEC means the ExecStart binary cannot be executed. Verified directly --
that path does NOT exist. Listing the package's bin/ shows exactly one binary:

  bin: gitea-runner

So gitea-actions-runner 3.1.0 RENAMED the binary from act_runner to gitea-runner,
and whatever generates the unit still calls the old name.

TO DETERMINE: whether the stale name comes from the nixpkgs
services.gitea-actions-runner module or from our own configuration, and whether
this is already fixed upstream. If upstream, the fix may just be a newer nixpkgs;
if ours, point ExecStart at bin/gitea-runner.

BLAST RADIUS: CI runner only. No user-facing service depends on it.

WORKAROUND IN PLACE: system rolled back to generation 2610, where the older
package still ships act_runner.

Package and module are\nconsistent within stable; there is no nixpkgs bug.\n\nThe 3.1.0 came from userPkgs (nixpkgs-unstable) via commit 89754f599, which\nroutes gitea-actions-runner.nix through the unstable set. * DONE [#D] Verify Grist documents are captured by local-backup after the first post-deploy run
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-60x
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 20:45]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-19 Wed 22:53]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-19 Wed 22:53]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
Confirm that Grist's documents are actually captured by the local backup, after the
first local-backup run following the 2026-08-19 deploy.

WHY THIS NEEDS CHECKING RATHER THAN ASSUMING. Grist keeps its spreadsheets as SQLite
files under /var/lib/grist; only the home database (orgs, users, ACLs) is in PostgreSQL.
So the PostgreSQL backup being healthy says NOTHING about whether the documents are
safe, and there is now one real document.

I told John during the deploy that /var/lib/grist "has not been picked up by
local-backup" and implied it needed wiring. That was misleading. local-backup sweeps
/var WHOLESALE, and there is no exclusion matching /var/lib/grist -- verified by grepping
the exclusion list. So it should be captured automatically at the next run with no
configuration change at all. What was true is only that the run had not happened yet:
Grist was deployed around 19:20 and the timer next fires 2026-08-20 00:06.

WHAT TO VERIFY after that run:
,  * /tank/Backups/Machines/Vulcan/var/lib/grist exists and contains the docs/ tree
,  * the .grist document file is present and non-zero
,  * local-backup's own result for that run was clean (note that rc23 is NOT benign here
    -- .latest means "ran", .lastclean means "worked")

COVERAGE THIS DOES AND DOES NOT GIVE:
,  * tank/Backups has ZFS snapshots (1428 of them), so once mirrored the documents get
    snapshot retention.
,  * NO OFFSITE COPY. "Machines" is in backupExcludes in modules/storage/backups.nix, so
    the mirror is deliberately not pushed to B2. That is consistent with every other
    service on this host -- NodeRED is the single exception -- so it is a known posture
    rather than a defect, but it means a total loss of /tank loses the Grist documents.
    Whether Grist deserves the NodeRED treatment is John's call, not a health-cycle
    decision.

If the verification finds the tree absent, THEN there is real work; until the timer has
fired, there is nothing to fix.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Verified. The Grist document IS captured, and no configuration change was needed.

    /tank/Backups/Machines/Vulcan/var/lib/grist/docs/v6KZyemq6YWDk7d9YctjvM.grist
    180224 bytes -- byte-identical to the source at /var/lib/grist/docs/

Rather than wait for the 00:06 timer, local-backup was triggered by hand. The mirror
directory did not exist before that run and does now, which is the direct evidence that
the wholesale /var sweep picks this path up.

THIS CONFIRMS THE CORRECTION IN THE ISSUE BODY. I had told John during the Grist deploy
that /var/lib/grist "has not been picked up by local-backup" and implied it needed wiring.
It did not: local-backup sweeps /var wholesale and no exclusion matches that path. The only
true part was that the timer had not fired since Grist was deployed.

COVERAGE THIS GIVES, stated precisely so it is not over-read:
,  * The document now sits on tank/Backups, which carries ZFS snapshots (1428 of them), so
    it has snapshot retention from here on.
,  * NO OFFSITE COPY. "Machines" is in backupExcludes in modules/storage/backups.nix, so
    this mirror is deliberately not pushed to B2. That is the same posture as every other
    service on this host except NodeRED. A total loss of /tank still loses the document.
    Whether Grist deserves the NodeRED treatment is John's call, not a health-cycle one.
,  * The window is up to 24 hours: the mirror is nightly, not continuous. A document created
    and lost inside one day is not covered by anything.

A note on reading local-backup's own status: the unit reports `activating` while rsync is
still working through /var even though Result=success and ExecMainStatus=0 are already set,
and .latest means "ran" while .lastclean means "worked". Do not treat either marker or the
active-state alone as proof a specific path was copied -- check for the file, which is what
was done here.
#+end_example

* DONE [#E] etterminal aborts at UserTerminalHandler.cpp:107 on ENOENT (87% of sessions; harmless)
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-6ey
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 00:29]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-14 Fri 01:04]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-14 Fri 01:04]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
`etterminal` (the Eternal Terminal CLIENT binary) has aborted 20 times in 14
days -- about 1.4/day -- and nothing alerts on it.

Full coredump census, 2026-07-31..2026-08-14, classified by signal and cgroup:

    etterminal      SIGABRT   user-session         20
    qemu-x86_64     SIGABRT   nix-build-sandbox    11
    python3.14      SIGBUS    nix-build-sandbox     6
    qemu-x86_64     SIGTRAP   nix-build-sandbox     5
    qemu-x86_64     SIGSEGV   nix-build-sandbox     3
    collect2        SIGABRT   user-session          1
    obr             SIGABRT   user-session          1
    mergiraf        SIGABRT   user-session          1

Everything in the sandbox is expected and correctly suppressed: the
SystemdSegmentationFault rule (loki-rules/systemd-errors.yaml) deliberately
excludes `"COREDUMP_CGROUP":"/system.slice/nix-daemon.service"`, which is why
even the three genuine SIGSEGVs from qemu-user do not alert. That part of the
design is working as documented.

WHY NOTHING FIRES for etterminal: that rule matches only
`segfault|segmentation fault|SIGSEGV`. These are SIGABRT, so a user binary
aborting 1.4 times a day is invisible to alerting. This is a coverage shape
worth knowing about generally -- the crash alert is signal-specific, not
crash-generic.

IMPACT is limited and not a service outage:
  - eternal-terminal.service (etserver, the SERVER) is healthy: active, up
    since 2026-07-03, NRestarts=0. Only the client aborts.
  - 369M across 56 files in /var/lib/systemd/coredump. Small against a 1.7T
    root at 28%, but it only grows.
  - John would notice client crashes directly as dropped sessions, so this is
    probably a known annoyance rather than a silent fault. Possibly related to
    the stale-agent-socket behaviour already recorded for ET.

DELIBERATELY NOT DONE: widening SystemdSegmentationFault to match SIGABRT. That
would alert 1.4x/day on a known-crashy client and add exactly the repeated
noise that has been trimmed elsewhere. Fix the crash, or leave it unwatched --
do not turn it into a daily page.

Next step if this is worth pursuing: get a backtrace with
`coredumpctl info etterminal` (no secrets in that output) and check whether the
aborts cluster around session start, reconnect, or the ssh-agent socket path.
#+end_example

** Notes

#+begin_example
`etterminal` (the Eternal Terminal CLIENT binary) has aborted 20 times in 14
days -- about 1.4/day -- and nothing alerts on it.

Full coredump census, 2026-07-31..2026-08-14, classified by signal and cgroup:

    etterminal      SIGABRT   user-session         20
    qemu-x86_64     SIGABRT   nix-build-sandbox    11
    python3.14      SIGBUS    nix-build-sandbox     6
    qemu-x86_64     SIGTRAP   nix-build-sandbox     5
    qemu-x86_64     SIGSEGV   nix-build-sandbox     3
    collect2        SIGABRT   user-session          1
    obr             SIGABRT   user-session          1
    mergiraf        SIGABRT   user-session          1

Everything in the sandbox is expected and correctly suppressed: the
SystemdSegmentationFault rule (loki-rules/systemd-errors.yaml) deliberately
excludes `"COREDUMP_CGROUP":"/system.slice/nix-daemon.service"`, which is why
even the three genuine SIGSEGVs from qemu-user do not alert. That part of the
design is working as documented.

WHY NOTHING FIRES for etterminal: that rule matches only
`segfault|segmentation fault|SIGSEGV`. These are SIGABRT, so a user binary
aborting 1.4 times a day is invisible to alerting. This is a coverage shape
worth knowing about generally -- the crash alert is signal-specific, not
crash-generic.

IMPACT is limited and not a service outage:
  - eternal-terminal.service (etserver, the SERVER) is healthy: active, up
    since 2026-07-03, NRestarts=0. Only the client aborts.
  - 369M across 56 files in /var/lib/systemd/coredump. Small against a 1.7T
    root at 28%, but it only grows.
  - John would notice client crashes directly as dropped sessions, so this is
    probably a known annoyance rather than a silent fault. Possibly related to
    the stale-agent-socket behaviour already recorded for ET.

DELIBERATELY NOT DONE: widening SystemdSegmentationFault to match SIGABRT. That
would alert 1.4x/day on a known-crashy client and add exactly the repeated
noise that has been trimmed elsewhere. Fix the crash, or leave it unwatched --
do not turn it into a daily page.

Next step if this is worth pursuing: get a backtrace with
`coredumpctl info etterminal` (no secrets in that output) and check whether the
aborts cluster around session start, reconnect, or the ssh-agent socket path.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
2026-08-14 -- backtrace analysis. All twenty crashes are ONE bug, not twenty.

Extracted the first eight frames from every etterminal core and compared their
low 16 bits, which are invariant under ASLR:

    20 x  2134 2120 bb1c 59c0 4e70 a318 b714 9a94
    total traces examined: 20

A single signature, 20/20. Different processes, different ASLR bases, identical
call site. That rules out random memory corruption and points at one
deterministic code path.

Shape of the stack: frames #0-#3 sit at 0x7f.../0x7e... addresses (shared
library) and frames #4-#7 at 0x55.../0x558... (the main executable). SIGABRT
raised from inside libc with application frames below it is the classic
abort()-from-library pattern; for a C++ program like Eternal Terminal the
overwhelmingly likely cause is an uncaught exception reaching std::terminate,
or a failed assertion.

BLOCKED ON SYMBOLS. The nixpkgs eternal-terminal binary is stripped -- every
frame resolves as "n/a (n/a + 0x0)" -- so the crashing function cannot be named
from these cores. Options if this is worth pursuing:
  - rebuild eternal-terminal with `separateDebugInfo = true` (or a debug
    output) via an overlay, then re-symbolize a fresh core;
  - or check upstream: a deterministic abort in a widely used client is likely
    already reported against 6.2.11.
Neither is worth doing on spec for a P4, but the single-signature finding makes
either one cheap to act on, since any single new core reproduces the same path.

Confirms the earlier read: this is a real, repeatable bug rather than
environmental flakiness. It remains client-side only -- eternal-terminal.service
(the SERVER) is still active with NRestarts=0 since 2026-07-03.

2026-08-14 -- ROOT CAUSE FOUND, without needing debug symbols. Eternal Terminal
writes its own client logs to /tmp/etterminal-$USER-*.log, and they name the
crash site directly.

Every one of the twenty cores has a matching log, and all twenty abort at the
SAME source location:

    [FATAL ... terminal-main UserTerminalHandler.cpp:107] Stack Trace:
    ...
    Error: (2): No such file or directory
    [WARNING ... UserTerminalHandler.cpp:107] Aborting application.
        Reason: Fatal log at [/build/source/src/te...]

So: ENOENT (errno 2) is turned into a glog LOG(FATAL), which calls abort()
rather than being handled. That independently confirms the earlier
backtrace-signature finding (20/20 identical low-16-bit frame offsets) by source
line, and it means the symbol-rebuild route recorded earlier is unnecessary.

THIS IS THE NORMAL EXIT PATH, not a rare fault. Of 23 etterminal session logs
present in /tmp, 20 end in this abort and only 3 end cleanly -- 87%. Session
lifetimes before the abort span 4 seconds to 743 minutes, so it is neither a
startup race nor anything on a fixed timer; sessions simply end this way
whenever they end. The last ordinary log line before the fatal is consistently
the client connecting to /var/run/etserver.idpasskey.fifo and reporting a good
socket.

Read together, the most likely story is that etterminal aborts during session
teardown on a path that finds an already-removed file, instead of exiting
cleanly. That fits every observation: John sees no problem, the server is
untouched, and the crash count simply tracks how often he uses ET.

IMPACT: cosmetic. eternal-terminal.service (the SERVER) remains active with
NRestarts=0 since 2026-07-03. Coredump storage is 369M over 56 files against
1.2T free, and systemd-coredump is at its defaults, so it is bounded by MaxUse
(10% of the filesystem) and KeepFree (15%). Nothing is at risk.

Upstream context (searched 2026-08-14): the pattern matches EternalTerminal's
known habit of surfacing recoverable errors as fatal logs -- e.g. issue #75,
where a socket write failure aborts via a glog check rather than being handled.
Nothing found that matches this exact ENOENT-at-teardown signature for 6.2.11.

RECOMMENDATION: close as harmless-upstream-behaviour unless John wants it
reported to MisterTea/EternalTerminal. Filing an upstream issue is an
outward-facing action and is his call, not something to do unasked. There is
nothing to fix on this host: suppressing the coredumps would only hide a
correctly-reported abort, and the disk cost is negligible.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
HARMLESS -- closed by John's decision, 2026-08-14. Fully diagnosed, deliberately not fixed.

etterminal aborts at src/terminal/UserTerminalHandler.cpp:107, where ENOENT
("Error: (2): No such file or directory") is promoted to a glog LOG(FATAL) that
calls abort() instead of being handled. Two independent lines of evidence agree:
  - all 20 cores share one backtrace signature (low-16-bit frame offsets
    2134 2120 bb1c 59c0 4e70 a318 b714 9a94, invariant under ASLR, 20/20);
  - all 20 matching /tmp/etterminal-johnw-*.log files name that same source
    line, which is how the site was identified without debug symbols.

It is the NORMAL exit path rather than a rare fault: 20 of 23 session logs end
this way (87%), and lifetimes span 4 seconds to 743 minutes, ruling out both a
startup race and anything on a fixed timer. The last ordinary log line is always
the client connecting to the etserver fifo. Best reading is an abort during
session teardown on an already-removed file, which matches every observation --
John sees no problem, and the crash count simply tracks how often ET is used.

Verified harmless before closing:
  - eternal-terminal.service (the SERVER) active, NRestarts=0 since 2026-07-03;
  - coredump storage 369M over 56 files against 1.2T free, with
    systemd-coredump at defaults, so bounded by MaxUse (10% of the filesystem)
    and KeepFree (15%). Nothing at risk.
  - no alert is affected: SystemdSegmentationFault matches segfault/SIGSEGV
    only, and these are SIGABRT, so nothing was ever firing or suppressed
    incorrectly.

NOT DONE, on purpose:
  - widening the crash alert to SIGABRT -- it would page ~1.4x/day on a known
    -crashy client, exactly the repeated noise trimmed elsewhere;
  - suppressing the coredumps -- that would hide a correctly-reported abort for
    negligible disk gain;
  - filing upstream at MisterTea/EternalTerminal -- outward-facing, John's call.
    The diagnosis above is complete enough to file from if he ever wants to. The
    pattern matches that project's habit of surfacing recoverable errors as
    fatal logs (cf. issue #75); nothing matching this exact ENOENT-at-teardown
    signature was found for 6.2.11.

Standing note for future health cycles: etterminal cores in coredumpctl are
EXPECTED and benign. Do not re-investigate. The qemu-user and python3.14 cores
alongside them are nix build-sandbox crashes, correctly excluded from alerting
by design.
#+end_example

* DONE [#D] postgres_fdw extension and GRANT for NocoBase exist only in the live database, not in Nix
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-75l
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 23:17]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-16 Sun 10:41]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-16 Sun 00:53]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

NocoBase's foreign-data-wrapper setup was applied by hand and exists only in the
running database. A rebuilt or restored nocobase database loses it silently, and
NocoBase then fails with "permission denied to create extension postgres_fdw"
again.

Applied 2026-08-16, NOT in any Nix module:

  \c nocobase
  CREATE EXTENSION postgres_fdw;                              -- superuser only
  GRANT USAGE ON FOREIGN DATA WRAPPER postgres_fdw TO nocobase;

Why it was needed: postgres_fdw is not a trusted extension
(pg_available_extension_versions: trusted=f, superuser=t), so the unprivileged
nocobase role cannot create it. NocoBase issues
`CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS postgres_fdw;` as nocobase@nocobase on every
external-data-source attempt.

Verified working: SET ROLE nocobase then CREATE SERVER ... FOREIGN DATA WRAPPER
postgres_fdw succeeded and was dropped again.

ALSO APPLIED BY HAND AND LIKELY REDUNDANT: the same extension plus a GRANT to
johnw in the SRP database. Nothing federates FROM SRP -- it is the remote side --
so those can probably be dropped. Confirm with John before removing.

OPTIONS:
  a) Declare it in the nocobase module, e.g. a postgresql-nocobase-fdw oneshot
     ordered after postgresql-setup.service, running the two statements
     idempotently (CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS is already idempotent; GRANT is
     naturally idempotent).
  b) Fold it into services.postgresql.ensureDatabases-adjacent setup if a
     suitable hook exists.
  c) Leave manual and document it.

(a) matches how every other privileged database step here is done
(mkPostgresUserSetup). Worth noting the security shape either way: USAGE on the
FDW lets the grantee make the PostgreSQL server process open outbound
connections and authenticate as whatever a user mapping stores, and mapping
passwords live in pg_user_mapping readable by the mapping owner.

** Close Reason

DECLARED IN NIX and verified live.

modules/containers/nocobase-quadlet.nix now defines postgresql-nocobase-fdw, a
Type=oneshot that runs the two statements I had applied by hand on 2026-08-16:
  CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS postgres_fdw;
  GRANT USAGE ON FOREIGN DATA WRAPPER postgres_fdw TO nocobase;
Both are idempotent, so it is safe on every boot.

Ordered after postgresql-setup.service as well as postgresql.service, and before/
requiredBy postgresql-nocobase-setup.service. That ordering is load-bearing, not
decorative: the nocobase DATABASE is created by ensureDatabases, which
postgresql-setup applies -- connecting before it exists is the same race that made
postgresql-nocobase-setup fail with 'role nocobase does not exist' on the first
activation of this service.

Verified after switching to 5jn2k936agw5azih00qgw5n0rq7rj83v:
  postgresql-nocobase-fdw: ActiveState=active Result=success ExecMainStatus=0
  After: postgresql.service, postgresql-setup.service
  pg_extension: postgres_fdw 1.1 present in the nocobase database
  has_foreign_data_wrapper_privilege('nocobase', ..., 'USAGE') = t
  NocoBase still serving HTTP 200, 0 failed units

Why it mattered: postgres_fdw is not a trusted extension
(pg_available_extension_versions reports trusted=f, superuser=t), so the
unprivileged nocobase role cannot create it and NocoBase's
'CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS postgres_fdw' fails with permission denied on
every external-data-source attempt. Held only in the live database, a restore or
rebuilt database would have lost it silently and reproduced that failure.

STILL MANUAL and left alone deliberately: the same extension plus a GRANT to
johnw in the SRP database, also applied by hand. Nothing federates FROM SRP -- it
is the remote side -- so they are almost certainly redundant, but dropping
privileges is John's call and he has been asked.

** Comments

#+begin_src json
[
  {
    "id": 7,
    "issue_id": "nixos-75l",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "2026-08-16 -- THE REDUNDANT SRP LEFTOVER IS GONE, at John's instruction.\n\nRemoved from the SRP database:\n  REVOKE USAGE ON FOREIGN DATA WRAPPER postgres_fdw FROM johnw;\n  DROP EXTENSION postgres_fdw;\n\nSafe by inspection before acting: SRP held the extension but 0 foreign servers,\n0 user mappings and 0 foreign tables, so nothing used it. Every pg_depend row\nagainst the extension was deptype='e' -- its own member functions and the FDW\nobject -- not an external dependent. DROP was issued WITHOUT CASCADE on purpose,\nso it would have errored rather than quietly destroying anything unexpected.\n\nVerified after: SRP has 0 extensions and 0 pg_foreign_data_wrapper rows.\n\nThe working path is untouched and was proven end to end, not just by catalog\nlookup: the nocobase database still has postgres_fdw 1.1, the nocobase role still\nhas USAGE, both foreign tables and the 'postgres' foreign server remain, and\nSET ROLE nocobase; SELECT count(*) FROM the clusters foreign table returned 64\nrows. NocoBase still serves HTTP 200.\n\nWhy it was redundant: postgres_fdw is a LOCAL-side extension. It belongs in the\ndatabase doing the querying -- nocobase, which holds the foreign tables -- not in\nthe database being read. SRP is the remote end and needs nothing installed. The\noriginal grant went there because the error message named CREATE EXTENSION and\nthe natural reading was that it belonged with the data.\n\nNothing to declare in Nix for this: the SRP state was never declarative, and the\nnocobase half already is, via postgresql-nocobase-fdw.\n",
    "created_at": "2026-08-16T17:41:36Z"
  }
]

* DONE [#A] Blanket userPkgs routing moves 85 core service modules off the stable nixpkgs pin
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-7bp
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 23:36]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Inverted the default in hosts/vulcan/application-modules.nix: modules now get the stable module-system pkgs unless named in needsUserPkgs, which is empty. Full ./build build succeeds with an empty allowlist; dovecot-2.4.5 is absent from the closure and core services are back on stable (postgresql 17.10, step-ca 0.29.0, zfs 2.3.7, samba 4.22.10, systemd 258.7). Switched 2026-09-01, zero failed units. Commit 01e1dcfa1.
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Commit 89754f599 ('Route Vulcan application modules through user nixpkgs',
2026-08-31, refs nixos-fbg) wraps EVERY module in
hosts/vulcan/application-modules.nix with 'pkgs = userPkgs'. userPkgs is built
from inputs.nixpkgs-user, which follows nix-config-ai/nixpkgs =
github:NixOS/nixpkgs/nixpkgs-unstable (locked e8be7818).

That silently moved 89 service modules -- including dovecot, gitea, gitea-
actions-runner, postfix, samba, databases, step-ca -- off the stable
nixos-25.11 pin. Operator intent (stated 2026-08-31) is that only home-manager
environments and services that genuinely need bleeding edge use unstable.

MEASURED BLAST RADIUS. Of ~54 real packages these modules reference, 45 differ
between stable+overlays and userPkgs, including:
  glibc     2.40      -> 2.42          systemd    258.7 -> 261.2
  zfs       2.3.7     -> 2.4.4         postgresql 17.10 -> 18.6 (major)
  python3   3.13.12   -> 3.14.7        dovecot    2.3.21.1 -> 2.4.5
  dovecot_pigeonhole 0.5.21.1 -> 2.4.5 gitea-actions-runner 0.2.13 -> 3.1.0
  step-ca   0.29.0    -> 0.30.2        step-cli   0.28.6 -> 0.30.6
  samba     4.22.10   -> 4.23.10       eternal-terminal 6.2.11 -> 7.0.0
  openssh   10.3p1    -> 10.5p1        podman     5.7.0 -> 5.8.6
Packages showing the same version still DIFFER by store path because they are
rebuilt against unstable's stdenv/glibc.

Two failures already caused (generation 2611, 2026-08-31): dovecot ExecStartPre
ABI mismatch (nixos-crb, 30 min mail outage, 44 messages queued) and
gitea-runner 203/EXEC (nixos-5v4). The NixOS modules themselves still come from
stable, so every one of these is a module/package version mix.

THE ROUTING BUYS ALMOST NOTHING. nixpkgs.overlays on the nixosConfiguration
(flake.nix:321-329) already applies nix-config-ai.overlays.default and .tools,
so the ordinary module-system pkgs ALREADY provides all 100 overlay attributes
(84 default + 16 tools). Scanning all 89 modules for references to those
attributes finds only 4 that use any:
  rclone-cloud-backup.nix (git-ai), pushme-positron.nix (git-ai),
  hera-llm-proxy.nix (llama-swap, omlx), drafts-mcp.nix (drafts-mcp-server)
and even those 4 would still resolve on stable+overlays. The only thing
userPkgs adds is the unstable BASE.

PROPOSED FIX: invert the default in application-modules.nix -- modules use the
module-system pkgs (stable + overlays); an explicit small allowlist opts into
userPkgs. Verify before moving back: stock-trader.nix (see 80d82bbbb 'Restore
stock-trader pandas-ta on user nixpkgs'), home-assistant.nix, vane.nix,
qdrant.nix, hermes-mcp.nix -- these may depend on unstable python.

Blocks nixos-crb and nixos-5v4; both are symptoms.

* DONE [#E] Verified: all 24 PostgreSQL dumps are structurally restorable, with data files and roles
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-7fp
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-22 Sat 00:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-22 Sat 00:46]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-22 Sat 00:46]
:CLOSE_REASON: Verification complete; no defect. 24 live DBs / 24 dump dirs with exact correspondence, pg_restore --list succeeds on all 24, zero zero-byte data files across the whole mirror, all toc.dat from one nightly pass (2026-08-21 02:00-02:05), and globals.sql carries 24 CREATE ROLE statements. The negative control discriminates (corrupt dump -> rc=1, 'did not find magic string'). Does not prove a full restore succeeds — that needs a scratch instance — and the issue says so.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

First structural validation of the PostgreSQL backup mirror. Everything passes.
Recorded because "the backup job exited 0" had never been distinguished from "the
backup is a usable artifact", and because the method is reusable.

COVERAGE IS EXACT: 24 live databases, 24 dump directories, 0 live DBs without a
dump, 0 dump dirs without a live DB.

ALL 24 DUMPS ARE STRUCTURALLY READABLE: pg_restore --list succeeds on every one
(it parses toc.dat and restores nothing). Sample TOC sizes: gitea 1114 entries,
grist 217, mailarchiver 151, hass 93, postgres 3.

THE CONTROL DISCRIMINATES, which is what makes the above mean anything. A
deliberately corrupted dump directory gives rc=1 and "pg_restore: error: did not
find magic string in file header" with 0 usable lines, while a real dump gives
rc=0 and 216 lines. Without that control, "pg_restore --list succeeded" would be
an untested assertion.

PER-TABLE DATA FILES BACK THE TOC. pg_restore --list only reads toc.dat, so a dump
could parse cleanly while its data files were missing or truncated. Checked
separately: every sampled dump has data files present, and across ALL 24 dumps
there are ZERO zero-byte *.dat* files. TOC counts exceed data-file counts by design
-- the TOC includes schema objects (indexes, constraints, functions) while data
files exist only for tables holding rows (gitea 1114 TOC vs 113 data files).

FRESH AND FROM ONE PASS: all 24 toc.dat files are dated 2026-08-21 between 02:00
and 02:05, i.e. a single nightly run rather than a patchwork of stale leftovers.

ROLES ARE CAPTURED: globals.sql present, 6087 bytes, 24 CREATE ROLE statements. A
restore without it would produce databases nobody can log into.

WHAT THIS DOES NOT PROVE, stated plainly: that a full restore succeeds. That needs
a scratch PostgreSQL instance and a real pg_restore, which is a heavier and more
invasive test. What is established is that the artifacts exist for every database,
parse, carry non-empty data, come from one recent pass, and are accompanied by the
role definitions.

MY OWN ERROR, recorded because it is the second occurrence in two cycles: I first
listed the dump directories sorted ascending and cut the output at 20 lines, which
hid the four LARGEST databases (mailarchiver, nodered_events, org, immich) and made
it look as though they were not backed up at all. They are -- 16.2 GB, 3.9 GB,
2.9 GB and 1.2 GB respectively. `head` on a sorted list has now produced a false
alarm twice; sort descending or print the whole thing.

Also worth knowing for future size comparisons: `du -sh` reports ZFS disk usage
while `du -sb` reports apparent size, and on this compressed pool they differ by
several times (hass: 259 MB on disk, 1201 MB apparent). Neither is wrong; they
answer different questions.

ALSO CHECKED CLEAN: last cycle's pending restic-metrics pass completed
(Result=success, 20:59:16) and the corrected HELP string is now in the live
textfile, with the two ages correctly distinct at 3.8h (collector) vs 114.0h
(integrity check).

:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17

:LABELS:   []
