#+begin_example
NagiosServicesCriticalIncreased fired 2026-08-12 18:10Z. The single HARD CRITICAL service is 'PING on b-hyve-sprinkler' (301 services monitored, 0 hosts down).

THE DEVICE IS FINE. Verified at the time of the alert:
  - ping from vulcan: 3/3 replies
  - ARP entry state: REACHABLE
  - Home Assistant has live data from it via the B-Hyve cloud integration —
    sensor.sprinkler_control_state = auto (updated 08:08), valve entities updated 09:36 the same day
  - Nagios' own last_time_ok was only 14.5 minutes before the alert

So this is an intermittent ICMP drop from a WiFi IoT device, not an outage. B-Hyve controllers are known to sleep or deprioritise ICMP.

RECURRENCE: over 7 days, ALERTS shows 629 samples of NagiosServicesCritical and 242 of NagiosServicesCriticalIncreased. nagios_services_critical_total was 0 for the preceding 12 hours, so each episode is short but they repeat.

WHY NOT FIXED HERE: the check lives in the Nagios host definitions, which are in a SEPARATE git repo consumed as the flake input 'nagios' (see CLAUDE.md). Standing commit approval covers /etc/nixos only. Also worth deciding rather than assuming: the right change is probably more retries / a longer check interval for this host, or dropping the PING check entirely given the device is monitored far better through the HA integration than through ICMP.

NOTE: the exporter only publishes aggregate counts (nagios_services_critical_total), not per-service series, so Prometheus alone cannot say WHICH service is critical — identifying it required reading /var/lib/nagios/status.dat. That is worth remembering the next time one of these fires, and is arguably a gap in the exporter.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

Fixed and deployed. Gave b-hyve-sprinkler the same PING thresholds miele-dishwasher already carries (pingWarn 500.0,20% / pingCrit 3000.0,60%) — the established idiom in hosts.nix for WiFi appliances.

Committed in the separate nagios repo as 4c3c463 (it is git+file:///etc/nixos/nagios with no remote, so a local commit suffices), then bumped the flake input 5420c266 -> 4c3c4636 and switched.

VERIFIED LIVE: the running check command for b-hyve-sprinkler PING is now
  check_ping!500.0,20%!3000.0,60%
byte-identical to miele-dishwasher's. nagios.service active, nagios_services_critical_total back to 0.

Rationale recorded in the commit and inline in hosts.nix: when the check went HARD CRITICAL the device answered 3/3 pings from vulcan with ARP state REACHABLE, Home Assistant had live data from it via the B-Hyve cloud integration, and nagios' own last_time_ok was 14.5 minutes earlier. 629 NagiosServicesCritical samples in 7 days. 60% loss still catches a real outage, which presents as 100%.

* DONE [#E] SSH -R 8317 forward collides between overlapping sessions: 13 journal errors/day
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-81n
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: chore
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 12:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-16 Sun 10:56]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-16 Sun 10:56]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

13 occurrences per day in the priority-3 journal, growing (3 in one 4h window on
2026-08-15 morning, 8 in the next). Benign, but it recurs every health cycle and
costs a re-investigation each time, so recording it rather than rediscovering it.

  error: channel_setup_fwd_listener_tcpip: cannot listen to port: 8317
  error: bind [ADDR]:8317: Address already in use
  error: connect_to [ADDR] port 22: failed.

WHAT IT IS. Port 8317 is a loopback-only remote forward (-R 8317) requested by
johnw's incoming SSH sessions. One sshd-session already holds it, bound on both
127.0.0.1 and [::1]; each NEW session requests the same fixed port and loses the
race. The emitting units are session-*.scope, i.e. interactive SSH sessions, not
any managed service.

Already known to the tooling, which is why nothing alerts: the port-drift
exporter names 8317 explicitly in its dual-stack dedup comment as
"(sshd-session, binds 127.0.0.1 + [::1])".

NOT CONFIG DRIFT, and deliberately not added to docs/ports.txt. It is one of
four unregistered loopback listeners; the other three are also transient user
processes (a python3 -m http.server on 18099, and two python3 - stdin processes
on 18123/18124). Registering ephemeral user processes would be wrong -- they
vanish. The port_drift_unexpected_loopback_listeners counter is info-only with no
rule reading it; only the WILDCARD class pages
(UnexpectedWildcardListener, currently 0). So the classification is working as
designed.

IMPACT. The second and later sessions simply do not get the forward, so whatever
uses 8317 in those sessions either fails or silently talks to the first session's
target. No system fault.

NOT ACTED ON, and needs John: the fix is in his CLIENT ssh configuration, which
lives in the nix-config repo, not on vulcan. Options are a dynamic remote port
(-R 0:) with the port read back, ExitOnForwardFailure tuning, or simply accepting
the collision. Choosing among those is his call, and none of it is a vulcan
change.

** Close Reason

RESOLVED BY JOHN, upstream in his nix-config, on 2026-08-16. Closing at his
instruction ("this should no longer be your concern").

State at close, recorded so a future cycle does not re-open this on stale
evidence: port 8317 is STILL bound, and errors are STILL appearing -- 1 in the
last hour, 6 in the last 6 hours. That is expected and not a sign the fix failed.
The sshd-session holding the port has been alive for 1 day 3 hours, so it predates
the config change and still carries the old -R 8317 request. New sessions that
collide with it keep logging:

  error: channel_setup_fwd_listener_tcpip: cannot listen to port: 8317
  error: bind [ADDR]:8317: Address already in use

The errors should stop once that long-lived session ends and no session is
requesting the fixed forward any more. If they are still appearing after every
pre-change session has been recycled, the change did not take -- that, not the
current count, is the test.

Nothing was changed on vulcan for this. What was established here and is worth
keeping: 8317 is a loopback-only remote forward from interactive sessions, it is
already named in the port-drift exporter's dual-stack dedup comment, and it was
deliberately NOT added to docs/ports.txt -- it is one of four unregistered
loopback listeners, the others being an ad-hoc `python3 -m http.server` and two
`python3 -` stdin processes. Registering transient user processes would be wrong;
they vanish. The port_drift_unexpected_loopback_listeners counter is info-only
with no rule reading it, so that classification is working as designed and only
the WILDCARD class pages.

* DONE [#D] AIDE fires a false positive after every nix garbage collection
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-84t
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-17 Mon 10:20]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-17 Mon 10:26]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-17 Mon 10:26]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

AideChangesDetected fired again on 2026-08-17, two days after the .obr exclusion
that fixed the previous instance. DIFFERENT CAUSE, and the earlier fix did not
regress: .obr mentions in the report are 0 and .obr entries in aide.db are 0.

WHAT HAPPENED. nix-gc ran at 00:00:02 and deleted 37,344 store paths (~59 GB,
root fell 462G -> 395G). The 00:09 aide-check then reported:

  Added 0   Changed 0   Removed 10

and every one of the ten was a /nix/var/nix/profiles/system-22NN-link -- old
system generation symlinks that the collection removed. aide.conf line 65
monitors /nix/var/nix/profiles/system READONLY, so it sees them.

Nothing was wrong. The database had been baselined at 21:25 the previous evening,
BEFORE the collection, so the check was comparing against a pre-GC world.

WHY IT WOULD RECUR. nix.gc is weekly (modules/core/base.nix, --delete-older-than
30d) and aide-check is daily, so every collection produces removals that the next
check sees. The existing post-rebuild re-baseline hook cannot help: a GC is not a
rebuild, and a rebuild is not guaranteed to follow one. Honest limit on this
claim -- I have ONE observed collection in the retained journal, not a series, so
the recurrence is reasoned from the mechanism rather than measured across weeks.

FIX: systemd.services.nix-gc.onSuccess = [ "aide-update.service" ], mirroring
what a rebuild already does. onSuccess rather than a timer or ExecStartPost
because it fires only when the collection SUCCEEDED, needs no delay (nix-gc's
deletions are complete when the unit exits, unlike activation where the existing
hook waits 60s), and a failed re-baseline cannot fail the collection.

THE CONTROL IS NOT WEAKENED. The generation links stay monitored -- the READONLY
rule is untouched and the rebuilt database still covers them. What changes is
that a known, scheduled, privileged deletion is followed by a re-measurement,
exactly as a rebuild is. An unexplained removal between collections still
reports.

Cleared the live false positive by running aide-update by hand: it completed in
1s and the published verdict went from changes_detected=1 / removed=10 to all
zeros at 3463 entries.

** Close Reason

FIXED and verified live.

systemd.services.nix-gc.onSuccess = [ "aide-update.service" ] in
modules/security/aide.nix, mirroring what a rebuild already does via the
aide-post-rebuild activation hook.

CAUSE, measured not guessed. nix-gc ran 2026-08-17 00:00:02 and deleted 37,344
store paths (~59 GB; root fell 462G -> 395G). aide-check at 00:09 then reported
0 added, 0 changed, 10 REMOVED -- every one a /nix/var/nix/profiles/system-22NN-link,
i.e. old generation symlinks the collection removed. aide.conf line 65 monitors
/nix/var/nix/profiles/system READONLY, so it sees them. The database had been
baselined at 21:25 the previous evening, before the collection, so the check was
comparing against a pre-GC world. Nothing was wrong.

Confirmed the earlier .obr fix did NOT regress: 0 .obr mentions in the report and
0 .obr entries in aide.db. Different cause entirely.

VERIFIED AFTER SWITCHING to wbcjsp39w4b6i3mibp587npvyspdljwn:
  systemctl show nix-gc -p OnSuccess  ->  aide-update.service
  next collection: Mon 2026-08-24 00:00
  0 failed units, 486 rules with 0 unhealthy and 0 lastError

CONTROL NOT WEAKENED, checked explicitly rather than asserted: the READONLY rule
on /nix/var/nix/profiles/system is still present in the generated aide.conf, and
the rebuilt database still contains 308 generation-link entries. What changed is
that a known, scheduled, privileged deletion is now followed by a re-measurement,
exactly as a rebuild is. An unexplained removal between collections still reports.

Cleared the live false positive by running aide-update by hand: it completed in 1s
and the published verdict went from changes_detected=1 / removed=10 to all zeros
at 3463 entries.

ONE THING I MISREAD ALONG THE WAY, recorded because it wasted a diagnostic step:
after the metric reached 0 the alert kept reading "firing", and I twice treated
that as either lag or a possible dead detector. It was neither. AideChangesDetected
lives in the config_drift group, which evaluates every 900s -- NOT in security_aide
(300s) where I assumed it was. Its last evaluation preceded the re-baseline, so
the alert simply had not been re-evaluated yet. When a rule's state disagrees with
its metric, check WHICH GROUP owns it and that group's interval before concluding
anything is broken.

Recurrence caveat kept from the original report: I have one observed collection in
the retained journal, not a series, so weekly recurrence is reasoned from the
mechanism (weekly GC, daily check) rather than measured across weeks. The next
collection on 08-24 is the real test -- if AideChangesDetected does not fire that
morning, this is confirmed.

* DONE [#E] sockpuppetbrowser logged a nightly false failure: SIGTERM exit 143 counted as exit-code failure
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-8jj
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-27 Thu 00:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-27 Thu 00:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-27 Thu 00:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed and verified; see description. SuccessExitStatus=143 deployed, and a direct SIGTERM to MainPID -- the path that actually produced the nightly failure -- now yields Result=success with zero 'Failed with result' lines.
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

FIXED and verified 2026-08-27.

SYMPTOM: sockpuppetbrowser.service (rootless quadlet under the changedetection user) logged
  Main process exited, code=exited, status=143/n/a
  Failed with result 'exit-code'
on 08-21, 08-22, 08-24, 08-26 and 08-27, each time alongside the nightly podman activity around 00:04. The service was healthy immediately afterwards -- Restart=always brings it straight back -- so nothing was ever broken.

CAUSE: 143 is 128+15, i.e. the container exiting on the SIGTERM podman sends to stop it. KillSignal=15 and SuccessExitStatus was EMPTY, so systemd classified a completely normal shutdown as a failure.

COST: it is noise in exactly the "any service failures in the last 4 hours" sweep, so it triggers a fresh investigation every time anyone looks. It cost one this cycle.

FIX: SuccessExitStatus = "143" in the unit's serviceConfig, in modules/users/home-manager/changedetection.nix.

VERIFICATION -- and the first attempt did NOT prove anything, which is worth recording. `systemctl --user restart` goes through podman stop, a clean ExecStop path that logs "Stopped Podman container" with no "Main process exited" line at all. It is green whether or not the fix is present, so it never exercised the failing path. The real test was sending SIGTERM directly to MainPID, which is what actually produces exit 143:
  before: Main process exited, code=exited, status=143  +  Failed with result 'exit-code'
  after:  Scheduled restart job, restart counter is at 1;  Result=success;  0 'Failed with result' lines
Unit active/running throughout.

NOT A MASKED CRASH: a crashing browser dies on its own signal (SIGSEGV -> 139, SIGABRT -> 134) or a nonzero application code, none of which are 143, and liveness is covered by the port probe rather than the exit status.

DELIBERATELY NOT APPLIED TO nocobase, which fails the same nights with 137 (128+9, SIGKILL). That is podman giving up after the stop timeout because the image's PID 1 is a trap-less shell that never forwards SIGTERM. 137 records a genuinely ungraceful shutdown and should stay visible.

* DONE [#C] pushme convergence: first sustained run in progress (3h+, ~1.5 MB/s)
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-9ue
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 20:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-13 Thu 20:44]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-13 Thu 20:44]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Since the filters landed, EVERY pushme-positron run has ended in 'Failed with result timeout' at exactly TimeoutStartSec=4h, or been killed. Across all time the unit has logged exactly ONE 'Finished Pull', and that was the 2026-08-10 23:16 run which aborted early on the unsupported --crtimes flag. A full pass has therefore never succeeded.

Observed runs:
  14:00:36  timeout (the 10:00:35 run, 4h)
  16:34:51  killed (my cleanup command, not the job's fault)
  16:43:36 -> 20:43:36  timeout, exactly 4h
  20:43:36  new run started by the timer

MEASUREMENTS 2026-08-12 20:44:
  throughput ~1 MB/s (40 MiB written and 39 MiB read in a 40s sample)
  destination referenced 278G; 34,311 files within depth 4 alone
  earlier phases of the same job ran at 7-8 MB/s, so throughput varies hugely
  with file size -- small files over the jump are overhead-dominated

CONSEQUENCE: each timeout raises a critical SystemdServiceFailed, and because the
timer is hourly the job restarts and re-scans from scratch rather than resuming
where it stopped. rsync does skip already-transferred files, so progress is not
lost, but the fixed cost of walking the whole source tree is paid every run.

NOT a filter problem: the exclusions work and reclaimed 150 GiB (obr nixos-kvr).
What remains is genuine data -- e.g. Products/ is 39 GB across 111 ares-* CI
output directories.

OPTIONS, needing John's decision:
  a) raise TimeoutStartSec further (8-12h) so one pass can finish, accepting that
     a genuinely wedged run takes that long to detect
  b) converge once by hand with no timeout, then let the hourly job handle the
     small steady-state delta -- probably the fastest route to a working state
  c) reduce scope further: decide whether Products/ and similar build output
     belong in this backup at all
  d) lengthen the timer interval so runs do not overlap-and-restart so often

Recommend (b) then (a) as a smaller number: converge once, then the ceiling only
ever guards against a real hang.

** Notes

#+begin_example
2026-08-12 -- decision recorded. John chose option (a): "Change the pushme
ceiling to 12 hours. Sometimes it's big." TimeoutStartSec 4h -> 12h, deployed
and verified live (unit file and running instance both report 12h).

Raising it exposed a SECOND blocking path, distinct from the sops-nix
restartUnits one already removed. NixOS `restartIfChanged` defaults to true, so
a switch that CHANGES this unit restarts it -- and because it is Type=oneshot,
switch-to-configuration blocks until the run completes. The very switch that
deployed the 12h ceiling hung, holding /etc/nixos/.nixos-build. At 12h that is
half a day of every rebuild on the host stalled behind a backup.

Fixed with `restartIfChanged = false` (verified X-RestartIfChanged=false; the
follow-up switch completed normally). Config changes now take effect at the
next scheduled run rather than by interrupting a run in flight, which is the
correct semantics for a backup regardless.

Committed as cf28397aa.

STILL OPEN: no full pass has ever completed. The 12h ceiling is untested by a
real run -- the next hourly timer fire is the first trial. What would close
this issue is a logged `Finished Pull` with a non-zero transferred byte count,
followed by a subsequent run that completes quickly (proving convergence, not
just a long first pass).

----------------------------------------------------------------------

2026-08-13 -- the 12h ceiling was NOT the binding constraint, and the root cause
looks like source availability rather than anything on vulcan.

Run 1, 00:01:28 (first under the 12h ceiling): did NOT time out. Died at 00:12
after ~11 minutes with rsync exit 12 --
  "connection unexpectedly closed (690359921 bytes received so far) [receiver]"
Run 2, 00:53 (triggered by hand): failed in under 25 seconds with rsync exit 255
and "Connection closed by UNKNOWN port 65535", 0 bytes received.

So the transport dropped mid-stream, and then 40 minutes later could not be
established at all. The jump host is reachable (DNS resolves, tcp/22 OPEN from
vulcan), so the break is PAST the jump -- consistent with andoria-08 itself
being offline or asleep between the two attempts. Nothing on vulcan changed
between them.

This reframes the whole issue. Raising the ceiling could never have helped: the
job is not running out of time, it is losing its source. Every prior "timeout"
result was the 50m/4h ceiling expiring while rsync sat on a connection that had
already gone quiet.

FIX APPLIED -- rsync resumability (deployed and verified reaching rsync):
    --partial-dir=.rsync-partial
    --timeout=600
Without --partial, a dropped connection discards the entire in-flight file. At
the ~1 MB/s this link sustains, any file needing longer to transfer than the
mean time between drops can NEVER complete -- every run restarts it from zero.
That is a convergence failure no timeout value can fix, and it matches the
observed behaviour exactly: 424G logical in the destination, yet never a
completed pass.

--partial-dir (not bare --partial) keeps partials in a side directory, so the
destination never holds a truncated file under its real name. The path is
relative, so rsync appends its own perishable exclude and --delete will not reap
the partials before they are reused -- checked against the rsync 3.4.1 man page,
and our filter list never mentions .rsync-partial so nothing shadows that rule.
--timeout=600 bounds the other failure shape, a stalled-but-open stream, which
would otherwise sit until the 12h ceiling and waste the entire window.

Verified: both flags appear in the rsync argv pushme actually invoked. NOT yet
verified end-to-end -- run 2 moved 0 bytes, so no partial has been created or
resumed yet. No .rsync-partial directories exist in the destination.

Destination now: tank/Backups/Contracts/Positron used 474G / referenced 274G /
logicalreferenced 424G, 35 top-level entries.

NEEDS JOHN: how available is andoria-08 expected to be? An hourly job against a
machine that sleeps will keep taking partial windows. With --partial-dir each
window now makes forward progress instead of discarding it, which may be enough
on its own -- but if the machine is only reliably up during working hours, the
timer should probably be retimed to match rather than retrying hourly into a
closed door.

ALERT CONSEQUENCE (2026-08-13, needs a call from John):
The unit sits in `failed` between hourly attempts, so SystemdServiceFailed
(critical, for=1m, the host-wide catch-all in alerts/systemd.yaml) is firing on
it -- 539 ALERTS samples in the last 24h. Because the email route repeats at
24h this is roughly one mail a day rather than an hourly page, so it is not
urgent, but it is a critical-severity alert standing permanently true for an
issue already tracked here.

Three options, all real trade-offs, so none taken autonomously:
  (a) time-bounded Alertmanager silence -- reversible, expires on its own, but
      hides a genuine failing unit for its duration;
  (b) add name!="pushme-positron.service" to SystemdServiceFailed, as was done
      for ServiceStuckActivating -- permanent, and a REAL pushme regression
      would then never alert;
  (c) leave it, accepting one mail a day until the sync converges.
(c) is the honest default while the issue is open and being worked. Worth
revisiting if it outlives the investigation.

----------------------------------------------------------------------

OVERNIGHT EVIDENCE (2026-08-13 04:43 cycle) -- the source-availability reading
is now well supported, and the shape is a clean cutoff rather than flakiness.

Four consecutive scheduled runs, all identical:
    01:00:00  rsync 255, "connection unexpectedly closed (0 bytes received)"
    02:02:34  rsync 255, 0 bytes
    03:01:05  rsync 255, 0 bytes
    04:04:43  rsync 255, 0 bytes
Against the one run that did connect:
    00:01:28  ran 11 min, moved 690,359,921 bytes, then rsync 12 (dropped)

So andoria-08 was reachable until ~00:12 and has been unreachable since, with
not a single byte moved in four attempts. Not intermittent -- a hard cutoff.

Re-verified this cycle that the failure is NOT on vulcan's side:
  - jump host resolves and tcp/22 is OPEN from vulcan
  - vulcan's own uplink is fine (external tcp/443 reachable, 3 default routes)
  - nothing on vulcan changed between 00:01 and 01:00 except the rsync flags
So the break remains past the jump, at andoria-08 itself.

The --partial-dir change therefore remains UNTESTED end-to-end: with 0 bytes
moved on every run since it was deployed, no partial has been created and
nothing has been resumed. The destination still holds no .rsync-partial
directories. Its value will only show on the first run that connects, transfers,
and then drops -- which is precisely the 00:01 shape.

This does not change the recommendation, it sharpens it: if andoria-08 is simply
off or asleep overnight, an hourly timer spends the night generating failures
(and keeping SystemdServiceFailed lit) for no possible benefit. Retiming the
timer to andoria-08's actual uptime window would fix the noise and lose nothing.
Needs John's answer on what that window is.

----------------------------------------------------------------------

CORRECTION (2026-08-13 08:45) -- my earlier "andoria-08 is offline or asleep"
conclusion was WRONG. The host is up and authenticating us; the session is being
terminated immediately after successful authentication.

Verbose ssh through the configured jump shows the full handshake succeeding:
  - key exchange completes against andoria-08's ED25519 host key
  - "Host '<remote>' is known and matches the ED25519 host key"
  - "Server accepts key: <pushme identity> ED25519"
  - then, with no command output whatsoever:
      channel 0: FORCE input drain
      Connection closed by UNKNOWN port 65535
So DNS, routing, the jump hop, sshd, the host key and our authorized key are all
fine. What fails is the SESSION, after auth.

An A/B test rules out my own change as the cause:
  TEST B  --rsync-path, WITHOUT --partial-dir  -> rsync 255, 0 bytes
  TEST C  --rsync-path, WITH    --partial-dir  -> rsync 255, 0 bytes
Identical. --partial-dir/--timeout are exonerated; the transfers fail with or
without them. (My first attempt at this test omitted --rsync-path, which the
real job passes, so its result was meaningless -- rerun with the real flag set.)

Invoking the remote rsync binary explicitly
(`/home/jwiegley/.nix-profile/bin/rsync --version`) also returns zero output and
closes. Nothing on the remote gets far enough to speak, not even to report a
missing binary.

"Authentication succeeds, then the session closes silently with no output" is
the signature of a session-level restriction on the account rather than a
network or key problem -- e.g. the login shell set to a non-shell, a
ForceCommand that exits immediately, or an account/PAM session restriction. It
is NOT reachability.

Timeline: the 00:01 run connected and moved 690 MB before dropping at 00:12.
Every attempt since -- 00:53, 01:00, 02:02, 03:01, 04:04, 05:03, 06:00, 07:01,
08:03 -- has returned 0 bytes. So whatever changed on andoria-08 happened in
that window and has persisted for over eight hours, through the start of the
working day.

NEEDS JOHN / the andoria-08 side. Nothing on vulcan can fix a remote account
whose sessions are closed at login, and the constraint that only `rsync` may run
there means I cannot probe further without exceeding it. Worth checking on that
host: the login shell and account status for the pushme user, any ForceCommand /
authorized_keys options on the key we present, and whether
/home/jwiegley/.nix-profile/bin/rsync still exists.

----------------------------------------------------------------------

2026-08-13 12:45 -- still failing, and a monitoring caveat that hid it.

Four more attempts since the last cycle, all identical: 09:02:54, 10:03:00,
11:00:27, 12:04:53, each rsync 255 with 0 bytes received. That is thirteen
consecutive 0-byte runs since 00:53. The session-closed-after-auth condition on
andoria-08 has now persisted for more than twelve hours, across the whole
working morning, so it is not a transient.

CAVEAT -- this cycle's health check initially read CLEAN and it was wrong.
`systemctl --failed` reported 0 and SystemdServiceFailed was not firing, because
an activation at 12:11:34 (John rebuilding directly, not via /etc/nixos/build --
build.log's last entry is 04:46) reset the unit's failed state seven minutes
after the 12:04:53 failure. The unit then reported Result=success while
ExecMainStatus was 1 and the journal recorded "Failed with result 'exit-code'".
The contradiction between Result and ExecMainStatus is the tell. The hourly
failures continue regardless; the next run will re-raise the alert.

Recorded as a durable lesson in memory (feedback_switch_clears_failed_units)
because this is the second time an activation has produced a false clean read on
this exact unit.

----------------------------------------------------------------------

2026-08-13 16:43 -- FIRST SUSTAINED RUN. andoria-08 came back (John confirmed);
a dry-run probe with the real flag set returned exit 0 and itemized the file, so
the session-closed-after-auth condition is gone.

The run started at 13:30:56 and is still going 3h12m later with no error, no
drop and no reconnect -- by an enormous margin the longest this job has ever
run. It is genuinely moving data, not stalled:
    13:39   used 455G  referenced 275G  logical 425G
    16:43   used 450G  referenced 281G  logical 441G
+6G referenced, +16G logical in ~3h, i.e. ~1.5 MB/s, which is the expected
small-file rate for this tree. Two rsync processes alive with accumulating CPU.

At that rate convergence is still slow, but the 12h ceiling gives this run until
~01:30, and for the first time the constraint being tested is throughput rather
than the transport dying.

--partial-dir remains UNEXERCISED, correctly: partials only materialise when a
transfer is interrupted mid-file, and nothing has been interrupted. Zero
.rsync-partial directories in the destination. Its value is still unproven and
will stay so until a run drops mid-transfer -- which is the right outcome to
hope not to need.

Timer interaction verified healthy, not broken: pushme-positron.timer shows
SubState=running with an EMPTY NextElapseUSecRealtime. That is not a lost
schedule -- it fired at 14:01:43, found the service already active, and holds
without queueing a second run until the current one finishes. Persistent=true
means the deferred trigger fires immediately afterwards. No overlapping runs are
possible, which is what we want for a job that can now legitimately run for
hours.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
CONVERGED. The sync completed a full pass and subsequent passes are now cheap, which was the close condition set when this was opened.

Evidence, 2026-08-13:
  13:30:56 -> 19:38:39   6h07m43s   'andoria done'   first full pass
  19:38:39 -> 19:56:00     17m21s   'andoria done'
  20:02:53 -> 20:05:08      2m15s   'andoria done'
All three logged 'Finished Pull' and 'Deactivated successfully' (exit 0). Note
the marker: 'andoria done' with NO '(with errors)' suffix, which every previous
run in this issue's history carried. 6h07m -> 17m -> 2m15s is convergence, not a
single lucky long pass.

Destination reconciled: referenced 275G -> 286G and logicalreferenced 425G ->
447G over the day, while used fell 455G -> 306G as --delete removed content no
longer present on the source. Zero .rsync-partial directories remain.

WHAT ACTUALLY FIXED IT -- not my changes. The root cause was on andoria-08: the
host was up and authenticating us, but closing every session immediately after
auth (verified by ssh -v: kex complete, host key matched, 'Server accepts key',
then FORCE input drain with no command output). Thirteen consecutive runs moved
0 bytes. John restored the host; the very next probe returned exit 0 and the
transfer ran.

Honest accounting of the three changes made here:
  - TimeoutStartSec 4h -> 12h: NECESSARY. The successful pass took 6h07m, which
    would have been killed by the old 4h ceiling. This is the one change that
    demonstrably mattered.
  - restartIfChanged = false: NECESSARY for a different reason -- it prevented a
    switch from restarting the unit mid-pass and from blocking on it. A switch
    did occur at 18:48 during the 6h run and the run survived it.
  - --partial-dir / --timeout=600: UNPROVEN. Nothing was interrupted mid-file
    during the successful passes, so no partial was ever created or resumed.
    The reasoning for it stands (a drop without --partial discards the in-flight
    file), but it has not been demonstrated and should not be claimed as
    load-bearing. It costs nothing to keep.

Follow-on work is tracked separately: nixos-tvl (gitea mirror), and the stale
pre-filter .tmp leftovers in the destination (~3.4G from 2026-06-26), which John
has been asked about and which are not part of this issue.
#+end_example

* DONE [#C] Nagios removal is half done: mirror gone, 302 native checks and 36 .nix files remain

:END:

On 2026-07-31 John gave an explicit directive:

  "I am explicitly choosing today to undo the Nagios/Prometheus duplication. So
  not only would I like to fully remove Nagios, but I would also like to remove
  all mention to it from any and all documentation through this system
  configuration repository."

Seventeen days later that is half done and nothing was tracking the remainder.
Filed during the 2026-08-17 health cycle after a NagiosServicesCriticalIncreased
alert prompted the question "why is a Nagios alert firing on a host where Nagios
was supposedly removed?"

WHAT IS DONE. Commit ed8b436e3 (2026-07-31) removed the Nagios<->Prometheus
mirror -- "499 duplicate checks gone" -- along with
modules/monitoring/services/nagios-mirror-divergence.nix and
scripts/nagios-mirror-divergence.py. Verified complete: objects.cache now
contains 0 PROM-MIRROR services, and the nagios_mirror_* metrics stopped
reporting on 2026-08-02 20:46 UTC with their last values healthy
(reconciler_success=1, divergence_total=0). No alert rule references the dead
mirror metrics, so that removal left no dead rules behind.

WHAT REMAINS, measured today:

    define service blocks in objects.cache : 320   (all native; 0 PROM-MIRROR)
    define host blocks                     :  25
    .nix files referencing nagios          :  36
    systemd units                          :  12   (nagios.service active/running)
    Nagios alert rules loaded in Prometheus:   7   (all inactive/health=ok)

At decision time the memory records 339 native checks across 317 distinct names.
Today it is 320. So roughly 19 native checks have been retired in 17 days -- the
mirror teardown was the easy half, and the native migration has barely started.

THE PRECONDITION JOHN ATTACHED, which is what makes this real work rather than a
delete: every native Nagios check needs a Prometheus equivalent BEFORE removal.
The riskiest part is explicitly the checks against OTHER hosts, which Prometheus
may not scrape at all -- 25 host definitions are still in play.

CURRENT STATE IS HEALTHY, so there is no urgency: 0 critical, 0 warning, 0
unknown, 0 stale, 0 hosts down; the status exporter ran 106s before measurement
with parse_success=1.

WHY THIS IS NOT BEING DONE AUTONOMOUSLY. Removing a monitoring system is
destructive and hard to reverse, and the gating condition requires auditing 320
checks for Prometheus coverage and deciding what to do about cross-host checks.
That is a migration with judgement calls in it, not cleanup.

SUGGESTED SHAPE, if John wants it picked up: inventory the 320 native checks by
distinct check name, diff that set against the Prometheus rule set to produce
the genuine gap list, and treat cross-host checks as their own sub-problem.
That inventory is safe to produce autonomously and would turn this into a
countable backlog -- worth doing first, and separately from any deletion.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Nagios is removed. John approved proceeding on 2026-08-19 after the coverage precondition
he attached on 2026-07-31 was verified satisfied.

EXECUTED IN SIX STAGES, each built, switched, verified and committed separately so any one
could be reverted alone:

  1. Prometheus-side watchers. Rules 489->482, nagios-named rules 7->0, textfile series
     51->50 (floor 45), no rule expression mentions nagios. Done FIRST so every later
     stage was alert-quiet while Nagios degraded and died.
  2. Relocated sops.secrets."monitoring/home-assistant-token" to its sole surviving
     consumer, re-owned root:root, and deleted the HA Nagios check. This had to precede
     stage 4: sops-install-secrets validates EVERY secret before writing ANY, and
     /run/secrets.d is ramfs, so one unresolvable owner aborts the run and the next boot
     comes up with no secrets at all.
  3. Satellite modules and orphans -- 8 files. nagios-tier1-mirror.nix was checked rather
     than trusted, because the comment above its import argued it was load-bearing; it is
     390 non-comment lines declaring nothing but Nagios object definitions.
  4. The core: nagios.nix, the daily report, the flake input, and the nagios user/group.
     Relocated the `z /tank/Backups 0711 johnw johnw -` rule and its mount ordering into
     local-backup.nix first -- nagios.nix was the SOLE declarative assertion of that mode.
  5. On-disk state, with John's explicit approval: /var/lib/nagios (3.6 GB), /var/log/nagios
     (19 MB), the shredded htpasswd bcrypt hash, the orphaned certificate and key, the run
     directory and the timer stamps.
  6. Documentation -- the operational surfaces only. The remainder is nixos-0ar.

FINAL VERIFICATION:
,  * nagios user and group both gone; 0 units, 0 unit files
,  * ZERO files under /var /etc /home /run owned by the removed uid 11 or gid 966
,  * 67 secrets present -- exactly one fewer than before, that one being
    nagios-admin-password
,  * /tank/Backups still johnw:johnw 711, with the rule now deployed from local-backup.nix
,  * 0 active Prometheus targets matching the vhost; certificate exporter emits no nagios
    series after the cert files were removed
,  * 0 failed units; only the Watchdog deadman firing
,  * hass-integration-exporter, the one capability that was genuinely Nagios-unique, still
    exits 0 and writes 68 config-entry and 12 integration series

WHAT REPLACED IT: the 2026-08-19 audit diffed all 302 native checks class-by-class and
found Prometheus watching more in every class -- 46 ICMP targets vs 23 check_ping, 569
systemd units vs 140 check_systemd_service*, 41 cert series vs 30 check_ssl_cert. The one
real gap (ssh 22, imap 143, imaps 993, submission 587) was closed beforehand with a
blackbox_tcp job and a TCPServiceDown rule.

A MISTAKE WORTH RECORDING: in stage 2 a regex using `(?:[^\n]*\n)*?` before an anchor
matched across intervening `define service` blocks and cut 57KB instead of 1.2KB. The byte
count caught it and it was reverted from git immediately. Every later block removal
asserted a maximum line count before cutting -- a guard that would have refused the bad
match outright. Related: `grep -c 'define service'` reports 90 rather than 73 because it
also matches `define servicegroup`.

Remaining: nixos-0ar for the comment and documentation sweep. Nothing functional.
#+end_example

This class needs no Prometheus work at all.\n\n    distinct units checked by Nagios        138\n    distinct units known to Prometheus      569   (node_systemd_unit_state)\n    ---\n    covered by BOTH                         134\n    nagios-only                               4   <-- none of them real, see below\n    prometheus-only                         435\n\nMethod note: unlike the ping class, no normalization was needed. Both sides name\nunits identically (138/138 and 561/569 carry a .service/.timer/.socket suffix),\nso this is a clean set comparison rather than one at risk of a format artifact.\n\nTHE FOUR APPARENT GAPS ARE ALL DEAD CHECKS, not uncovered services. Every one\nreports LoadState=not-found on this host, and all four are assigned to vulcan\nitself rather than to a remote host:\n\n  copyparty.service              The service is RUNNING and MONITORED -- it just\n                                 has a different unit name. copyparty is an\n                                 nspawn container, so the real unit is\n                                 container@copyparty.service (active/running,\n                                 5 series in node_systemd_unit_state). The\n                                 Nagios check names a unit that never existed.\n\n  critical-services-exporter.service\n  rspamd-scan-mailboxes.service\n  rspamd-scan-mailboxes.timer    Pure dead config. `grep` finds these names in\n                                 exactly ONE file each -- modules/services/\n                                 nagios.nix -- i.e. they exist only as check\n                                 definitions pointing at nothing.\n\nWHY NOBODY NOTICED, and this is the part worth carrying into the migration:\ncheck_systemd RETURNS OK FOR A UNIT THAT DOES NOT EXIST. Proven directly against\nthe deployed plugin:\n\n    check_systemd -u sshd.service        -> exit 0  \"sshd.service: active\"\n    check_systemd -u copyparty.service   -> exit 0  \"copyparty.service: inactive\"\n    check_systemd -u this-unit-cannot-possibly-exist-xyz.service\n                                         -> exit 0  \"...: inactive\"\n\nA fictional unit is reported as OK/inactive. So all 122 check_systemd_service\nchecks are structurally blind to the case \"the thing I monitor is gone\", and any\nservice renamed or removed leaves a check that passes forever. That is exactly\nwhat happened here, and it means Nagios's systemd coverage was weaker than its\ncheck count suggests -- 4 of 138 units were being watched by a check that could\nnot fail.\n\nPrometheus is not automatically better on this axis -- a removed unit loses its\nseries, and SystemdServiceFailed cannot fire on a series that is absent -- but\nthe repo already uses absent() guards for the critical set (CriticalServiceDown\nin health-checks.yaml), which is the correct pattern for it.\n\nRUNNING TOTAL OF TRUE COVERAGE GAPS, unchanged at 8:\n    ping class     7 hosts pinged by Nagios with no blackbox ICMP equivalent\n    https/ssl      1 live vhost with no blackbox probe\n    systemd        0\n\nCLASSES STILL TO AUDIT: check_file_age_tier1 (40), check_podman_container* (9),\ncheck_tcp (6), and ~25 single-instance commands (imap/imaps, smtp, ssh, dns,\nzfs, load/procs/users/disk, qdrant, git-workspace, atd). That is 80 checks left\nof the original 302, with the three largest classes now done.\n",

Established by\n    resolving BOTH sides to IPv4 first -- Nagios stores bare addresses while\n    blackbox uses hostnames, and a naive comparison reports 23 uncovered hosts,\n    which is a pure format artifact. Between 5 and 7 in truth, since 2 blackbox\n    targets do not resolve and could account for up to two of them.\n  * 1 live .lan vhost that answers https 301 but is probed by no blackbox job.\n    Confirmed genuine: an exact instance-regex match returns 0 series.\n\nCLASSES CLOSED THIS PASS:\n  * file_age (40): every checked path is a Prometheus textfile-collector file\n    under /var/lib/prometheus-node-exporter-textfiles, and all 40 basenames\n    appear in node_textfile_mtime_seconds. The\n    unit is dovecot.service; dovecot2 is only a prefix on two helper units.\n    Real answer: 5 series.\n  * assumed no ssh coverage from a probe_success{job=~\".*ssh.*\"} miss. sshd is\n    covered as node_systemd_unit_state{name=\"sshd.service\"}, 5 series.\n  * counted `^define service` earlier, which also matches `define servicegroup`\n    and inflated 302 to 320.\nEach was a wrong selector reading as a clean negative -- the same failure mode\nthree times, in a single audit.\n\nWHAT REMAINS IS DECISION AND DELETION, not discovery: close the 8 gaps (or\naccept them), then remove the 36 .nix files, 12 systemd units and 7 alert rules.\nBoth halves are John's call, and the host cannot deploy anything while nixos-chl\nis open.\n",

The precondition John attached to\nremoving Nagios -- a Prometheus equivalent for every native check -- is now met.\n\nWHAT WAS ADDED (modules/services/blackbox-monitoring.nix):\n  * 4 ICMP targets, joining host_group \"local\" via the existing relabel, so\n    HostUnreachable owns them exactly as it owns the rest of that block. That is\n    the same alerting weight Nagios already applied, which is the point.\n    3 are probed BY IP, following the documented TL-WPA8630 precedent in the\n    same file: OPNsense registers only the PTR, so the forward name does not\n    resolve and a name-based probe fails even when the device is up.\n  * 1 HTTPS target, the BARE vulcan.lan vhost. Marginal alone -- nginx failing\n    would take the other 39 probes with it -- but it was the last item in the\n    https/ssl class.\n\nVERIFIED AFTER THE SWITCH:\n    blackbox_icmp targets        42 -> 46\n    blackbox_https_local         34 -> 35\n    probe_success{https://vulcan.lan} = 1\n    local ICMP targets UP 11, DOWN 0\n    ICMP gap re-run: 7 -> 3      vhost gap re-run: 1 -> 0\n\nTHE HEADLINE NUMBER WAS WRONG AND IS NOW CORRECTED. That is THIS HOST, already\n       probed as vulcan.lan. Never a gap; my IPv4 normalisation turned vulcan's\n       own loopback entry into an apparent missing target.\n    2  *.local -- mDNS names. avahi resolves them; unicast DNS returns nothing.\n       A probe works only while Prometheus runs on this host, and a sleeping\n       device would page HostUnreachable as CRITICAL. Deliberately not added.\n    4  genuinely missing -> added.\nSo the real gap was 5 (4 hosts + 1 vhost), not 8.\n\nALSO CORRECTED: I previously called 3 non-resolving blackbox targets a bug\n(\"monitoring nothing, sitting there silently\"). They are not. nest-downstairs\nand nest-family-room are host_group=iot-noping, a group whose own comment says\nthese devices NEVER ANSWER ICMP; ring-doorbell is iot-quiet, which the alert\nrules say MUST NEVER ALERT. They are intentional entries in groups built for\nexactly that behaviour, and no rule fires on them.\n\nREMAINING, all intentional and documented in the module: localhost and the two\nmDNS names. Whether to probe the mDNS pair is a judgement about alert noise vs\ncoverage that belongs to John, not a defect.\n\nNEXT: the staged removal John chose -- alert rules, then systemd units, then the\n36 .nix files, with a switch and health check between each stage.\n",

    "text": "2026-08-19: inventory diff re-run at John's request, BEFORE any deletion. Six commands are 79% of the volume:\ncheck_systemd_service 122, check_file_age_tier1 40, check_ssl_cert 29,\ncheck_ping 23, check_https_vhost 23, check_systemd_service_conditional 17.\n\nCLASS-BY-CLASS DIFF against live Prometheus. Prometheus watches MORE in every class:\n\n    nagios class (count)              prometheus side\n    check_ping (23)                   46  blackbox_icmp targets\n    check_https_vhost/http/https (35) 41  blackbox http+https probe series\n    check_ssl_cert + external (30)    41  probe_ssl_earliest_cert_expiry\n    check_systemd_service* (140)     569  distinct units in node_systemd_unit_state\n    check_file_age + backup_age (43)  51  node_textfile_mtime_seconds\n    check_podman_container* (9)       16  container_health_status\n    check_zfs_pool (1)                 7  node_zfs_zpool_state\n\ncheck_tcp (6) looked like a gap and is NOT one. Each port already has a better\nsignal: 5432 the postgres exporter, 9090 the Prometheus self-scrape, 3000 grafana,\n8123 the hass HTTPS probe, 443 the 35 https_local probes, 80 the http probe.\n\nThe long tail of 17 single-instance commands is covered too: dns 11 series,\nqdrant 1, load/disk/procs/users via node_exporter, git_workspace 9, atd 4,\nhomeassistant_integration_status 12.\n\nTHE ONE REAL GAP, now closed: check_ssh (22), check_imap (143), check_imaps (993)\nand check_smtp (587) had NO Prometheus equivalent. Postfix and Dovecot export 222\nand 141 series respectively, but every one of those measures the DAEMON -- none\nanswers \"does the port accept a connection\", which is what a wedged or unbound\nlistener behind a healthy-looking process breaks, and exactly what those four\nNagios checks were for.\n\nClosed by adding a blackbox_tcp scrape job over those four ports on 127.0.0.1\n(loopback deliberately, so no addresses enter the tracked repo) plus a TCPServiceDown\nrule at 5m. The tcp_connect prober module already existed in blackbox-monitoring.nix\nand simply had no job pointing at it.\n\nThe alert rule is not optional here: ProbeTargetChronicallyFailing would have been\nthe only rule matching the new job, and it is a 1-week average below 0.98 -- days to\nnotice an ssh listener down. HostUnreachable does not apply, being scoped to\nblackbox_icmp. A job with no acute rule would have been coverage that reads as\ncoverage and catches nothing.\n\nVERIFIED: all four probes return probe_success=1; the TCPServiceDown selector sees\nexactly 4 series; the rule loads with health=ok and empty lastError after warmup.\n\nSO: nothing in the 302 native checks is now uncovered. Deletion is unblocked and\nremains John's call -- it was not begun.\n",
    "created_at": "2026-08-20T02:12:42Z"

* DONE [#E] Threshold audit: 77 of 484 rules checked mechanically; method must account for for: clause
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-bkf
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:47]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 08:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-21 Fri 08:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Audit run; no new defects found. Two of the four flagged rules were false alarms from the method ignoring the for: clause, and both are correctly tuned. The one genuine recurrence (GitWorkspaceMetricCollectionSlow) reflects a real slow exporter rather than a bad threshold and was left alone; its noon clustering is recorded as unexplained. Method limitations and two of my own instrument errors are written into the description for the next run.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
Systematic check of alert thresholds against their metrics' observed 30-day ranges,
prompted by the ResticNoRecentSnapshot finding (nixos-3yx), where a threshold sat
inside the normal operating band and produced four spurious events in a month.

METHOD AND ITS COVERAGE, stated honestly: for every alert rule of the simple form
`metric{...} OP constant`, compare the constant against max_over_time (for `>`) of
the same selector over 30d. That form covers 77 of 484 rules; the other 407 are
compound expressions (and/or/absent/multi-line) the mechanical pass cannot parse.
So this is a PARTIAL audit and should not be read as "all thresholds checked".

RESULT: no new defects. Four rules had an observed max at or above their threshold,
and on investigation two were false alarms of my own making and two are correct.

THE METHOD'S FLAW, which is the main thing worth keeping: it IGNORES THE `for:`
CLAUSE. A threshold that looks dangerously tight against the raw maximum is
perfectly safe when `for:` filters transient excursions. Two of the four:
  DNSProbeLatencyHigh   thr 1s, max 5s -- but only 7 five-minute samples above 1s
                        in 30d and `for: 900s`, so a spike must persist 15 minutes.
                        Never fired. Correct.
  PowerOffSensitiveGroupMultipleDown  thr >=2, max 3 -- but only 6 one-minute
                        samples at/above 2 in 30d against `for: 600s`, needing 10
                        consecutive minutes. Never fired. Correct, and this one
                        matters: a real power loss takes the group down for far
                        longer than six minutes, so the `for:` is doing exactly the
                        discrimination it was written for.
Any future run of this audit must join the threshold against `for:` before calling
a rule tight.

TWO INSTRUMENT ERRORS I MADE, recorded because both produced confident wrong answers:
 1. I "verified" the power-off rule with query_range at step=900 and got max=1.0,
    concluding the mechanical pass was wrong. It was not: max_over_time([30d])
    inspects every raw sample and correctly reported 3, while a 15-minute step
    simply stepped over a 6-minute excursion. I doubted the RIGHT tool. This is the
    same sampling-step trap as the ALERTS step=3600 problem in nixos-3yx, in the
    opposite direction.
 2. Twice I queried a metric name that did not exist (power_off_sensitive_device_up,
    git_workspace_metric_collection_duration_seconds) and got clean zeros. A
    nonexistent metric returns "0 samples", which is indistinguishable from a
    healthy result. Always take the metric name from the rule's own expr.

ONE OPEN OBSERVATION, not a defect and not fixed:
GitWorkspaceMetricCollectionSlow (git_workspace_scrape_duration_seconds > 180,
for 600s) fires genuinely. The exporter is normally instant -- median 0.0s, p99
2.0s -- but has a heavy tail to 572s, and spends ~334 one-minute samples above 180s
per 30 days, firing roughly 180 minutes a month. That is a real slow exporter, not
a threshold artifact, so the rule was left alone.
  I hypothesised contention with the nightly ~600-repo git-workspace-archive sweep
  and REFUTED it: the slow samples cluster at 12:00 (28 samples) rather than 00:00
  (4). Something around noon slows this exporter and I do not know what. Low
  severity -- a slow scrape means briefly stale metrics, nothing more -- but it is
  unexplained rather than explained away.
#+end_example

* DONE [#D] Zimit job runner had no mount dependency on its ZFS datasets; llm-primer failed with EACCES
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-cgk
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 16:07]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 16:07]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-20 Thu 16:07]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed and verified in the same cycle; full analysis in the description. RequiresMountsFor + ConditionPathIsMountPoint deployed and confirmed in the generated unit file; llm-primer re-ran and is crawling; ZimitJobsFailed cleared.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

The llm-primer job (a03d993b, https://actionbridge.io/en-US/llmtutorial) did not fail
on its own merits. The job runner could not create its working directory:

  14:55:06  Processing job: a03d993b
  14:55:06  Running Zimit for https://actionbridge.io/en-US/llmtutorial
  14:55:06  mkdir: cannot create directory '/tank/Archives/work/a03d993b': Permission denied
  14:55:06  Main process exited, code=exited, status=1/FAILURE

zimit-progress-monitor then swept the job with "Orphaned: job-runner exited before
completion". That message describes the symptom, not the cause, and sent me looking
at the crawl first -- the crawl never started.

ROOT CAUSE: /tank/Archives/work and /tank/Archives/ZIM are each their OWN ZFS
dataset, and zimit-job-runner declared NO mount dependency on either. The
tank-Archives-ZIM.mount after/wants in this same module belongs to kiwix-serve, not
to the runner, which is easy to misread as coverage. So the runner was free to fire
while a dataset was unmounted, at which point /tank/Archives/work resolves to the
directory SHADOWED beneath the mountpoint -- proven to be root:root 0755 -- which the
zimit user cannot write to. Hence EACCES rather than EROFS or ENOSPC.

THE SHADOW PERMISSIONS ARE THE SAFETY NET, NOT THE BUG. It is tempting to "fix" this
by chowning the shadowed directory to zimit. That would be much worse: an unmounted
run would then silently write a multi-GB crawl into the parent dataset on the root
pool instead of failing. The loud failure was correct behaviour. Recorded in the
module so nobody reverses it.

FIXED with two directives, because the obvious one is not sufficient on this host:
  RequiresMountsFor        gives ORDERING only here. Verified after deploying it:
                           After= gained tank-Archives-work.mount and
                           tank-Archives-ZIM.mount, but Requires= still listed only
                           -.mount and both mount units reported RequiredBy=0 for
                           this service. These ZFS mounts come from the runtime mount
                           table rather than fstab, so systemd orders against them but
                           will not treat them as hard requirements. Ordering alone
                           does not prevent an unmounted start.
  ConditionPathIsMountPoint  is what actually enforces it, and it fails in the better
                           direction: an unmet condition SKIPS the unit rather than
                           failing it, so a pending job waits for the next 5-minute
                           timer instead of being run against an unwritable path and
                           then marked failed.
Both confirmed present in the generated unit file. Note `systemctl show -p
ConditionPathIsMountPoint` returns EMPTY even when the condition is set -- systemd
does not expose conditions under that property name, so read `systemctl cat` instead.
That empty output is a broken instrument, not a missing directive.

NOT ESTABLISHED: what unmounted the dataset at 14:55. tank-Archives-work.mount reports
continuously active since 2026-07-03 and never cycled in the window; nothing in the
14:54-14:55 journal touches /tank/Archives; zpool events retained nothing. This is the
only such failure in the whole retained journal. The fix makes the trigger moot -- an
unmounted state now skips rather than fails -- so I did not chase it further, but it
is unexplained rather than ruled out.

OUTCOME: the job re-ran and is crawling normally. /tank/Archives/work/a03d993b exists,
job status=running since 2026-08-20T23:00:25Z, the container is up, and ZimitJobsFailed
has cleared.

* DONE [#B] agnix 0.49.0 fails to build: uses unstable 'if let' guards against rustc 1.91.1
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-chl
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-18 Tue 23:38]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-19 Wed 18:42]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-19 Wed 18:42]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
With the starship eval error fixed (nixos-5eo), the switch now gets past
evaluation and fails during BUILD instead. Second, independent breakage from the
same day's nix-config updates, and it still blocks every rebuild.

    error[E0658]: `if let` guards are experimental
    error: could not compile `agnix-core` (lib) due to 1 previous error
    error: Cannot build '/nix/store/1vf5...-agnix-0.49.0.drv'
           Reason: builder failed with exit code 101.

THE MISMATCH. agnix 0.49.0 uses `if let` guards in match arms (RFC 2294), which
are still UNSTABLE in stable Rust. It is being built with rustc 1.91.1 from
nixpkgs -- confirmed from the derivation's references (rustc-wrapper-1.91.1,
cargo-1.91.1). So it needs either a nightly toolchain or a newer stable than
this host's nixpkgs pin provides.

THIS IS A REGRESSION, NOT A LONGSTANDING GAP. The running system contains
agnix-0.44.0, built successfully. Today's nix-config-ai bump moved it to 0.49.0.
Older builds are still in the store (0.33.2, a 0.40.0 vendor drv), so this
package has been building fine here for a long time.

agnix comes from nix-config-ai -- `grep -rn agnix --include='*.nix'` finds
nothing in /etc/nixos -- so like nixos-5eo the defect is upstream of this repo
and cannot be fixed or properly verified from here.

OPTIONS, all John's:
  1. Build agnix with a newer toolchain in nix-config-ai. rust-overlay is
     already among the inputs (two rust-overlay nodes are present in
     flake.lock), so the machinery exists; agnix is simply picking up nixpkgs'
     rustc instead. Correct fix if 0.49.0 is wanted.
  2. Pin agnix back to 0.44.0, the version currently running and known good.
     Smallest change, and defers the toolchain question.
  3. Drop agnix from the AI package set if it is not actively used.

NOT DONE AUTONOMOUSLY: the fix belongs in another repo, and choosing between
"newer toolchain" and "older package" is a real decision about John's own
tooling.

CURRENT IMPACT: the host still cannot complete a switch. Everything that has
been verified and committed this session -- the three alert-rule changes, the
immich permissions fix (nixos-g52), and the starship shim itself -- is waiting
on this. The running system is healthy and unaffected.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Fixed upstream in nix-config (commit 6fb53067) and now verified live on vulcan.

Option 1 from the issue was taken: build agnix with a newer toolchain rather than
pinning back to 0.44.0. overlays/ai/30-agnix.nix was replaced with a version taking
{ inherit inputs; } and building against a rustPlatform assembled from the
rust-overlay input at 1.95.0 -- the release in which if_let_guard (RFC 2294)
stabilized -- instead of nixpkgs' rustc 1.91.1. Written platform-agnostically, so
it is not an aarch64-only patch, and proven by building before pushing rather than
pushing and hoping.

VERIFIED ON THIS HOST, not merely upstream:
,  * agnix --version reports 0.49.0, from /etc/profiles/per-user/johnw/bin/agnix
,  * /nix/store/gxxzc9iqn6ljnn0ar42bkvikdcjq676f-agnix-0.49.0 is in the closure of
    /run/current-system
,  * its deriver references rust-minimal-1.95.0 and auditable-rust-minimal-1.95.0 --
    NOT the rustc-wrapper-1.91.1 / cargo-1.91.1 pair named in this issue as the
    cause. That is the actual proof: "it builds now" alone would not distinguish a
    real fix from a cached older artifact.
,  * three full `/etc/nixos/build switch` runs completed on 2026-08-19 with this
    package in the closure

flake.lock here has nix-config and nix-config-ai at f41587a8, which carries the fix.
#+end_example

No local workaround is clean.\n\nTHE VERSION FACT THAT DECIDES EVERYTHING. `if let` guards (`if_let_guard`,\nRFC 2294) stabilized in Rust 1.95.0. So this needs NO nightly toolchain -- a\nnewer STABLE is sufficient. Measured toolchains:\n\n    vulcan's nixpkgs (25.11)     rustc 1.91.1   <-- what agnix is built with\n    nixpkgs-unstable (an input)  rustc 1.96.1   >= 1.95, would work\n    rust-overlay (an input)      rustc 1.97.1   >= 1.95, would work\n    already realised in the store: 1.96.1, 1.97.0, 1.97.1, 1.99.0-nightly\n\nSo the toolchain is not scarce here; agnix is simply being handed the oldest one.\n\nWHY NIX-CONFIG'S OWN MACHINES DO NOT SEE THIS. overlays/ai/default.nix already\nbuilds a newer platform:\n\n    rustPkgs   = import inputs.nixpkgs { system = \"x86_64-linux\";\n                   overlays = [ inputs.rust-overlay.overlays.default ]; };\n    rust195    = rustPkgs.rust-bin.stable.\"1.95.0\".minimal;\n    rustPlatform195 = prev.makeRustPlatform { cargo = rust195; rustc = rust195; };\n\nPinned at EXACTLY 1.95.0, the if_let_guard version -- so this exact class of\nproblem was already anticipated. But it is unusable here for two reasons:\n\n  1. The whole block is wrapped in\n       lib.optionalAttrs (buildPlatform == \"x86_64-linux\"\n                          && hostPlatform == \"x86_64-linux\")\n     and vulcan is aarch64, so on this host it evaluates to {} -- the machinery\n     is not merely unused, it does not exist.\n\n  2. Even where it does exist it is applied only to `qdrant`\n     (`qdrant = prev.qdrant.override { rustPlatform = rustPlatform195; };`),\n     never to agnix.\n\noverlays/ai/30-agnix.nix uses bare `with prev; rustPlatform.buildRustPackage`,\ni.e. whatever rustc the consuming nixpkgs provides. On nix-config's own targets\nthat is new enough; on vulcan, pinned to 25.11, it is 1.91.1 and the build dies.\nSame structural shape as nixos-5eo: nix-config assumes newer inputs than this\nhost pins.\n\nSUGGESTED UPSTREAM FIX (nix-config, one file): drop the x86_64-only gate on the\nrustPlatform195 block, or extend it to aarch64, and apply rustPlatform195 to\nagnix as it already is to qdrant. 30-agnix.nix would need to take rustPlatform\nas an argument rather than closing over `prev.rustPlatform`.\n\nWorth checking while there: the x86_64 gate means qdrant on aarch64 also falls\nback to nixpkgs' rustc, so the same latent problem may exist for it.\n\nWHY NOT FIXED LOCALLY IN /etc/nixos. Three routes, all bad:\n  * `pkgs.agnix.override { rustPlatform = ...; }` -- IMPOSSIBLE. Verified:\n    pkgs.agnix has NO `.override` attribute at all, because the overlay builds\n    it with `with prev; rustPlatform.buildRustPackage` rather than callPackage,\n    so rustPlatform is closed over rather than being a function argument.\n  * Re-declaring agnix in a local overlay -- would duplicate the upstream\n    package definition and silently drift from it on every upstream change.\n  * Overriding rustPlatform globally -- would rebuild every Rust package on the\n    host to fix one.\n\nCANNOT SIMPLY BE SKIPPED: agnix is in johnw's home.packages (verified, 1 entry),\nso it is a hard dependency of the switch. Dropping it would change John's\ntooling, which is his call rather than a workaround I should apply.\n\nSTATUS: nix-config main is at b95bf839b3fc, identical to the rev pinned here, so\nnothing has moved upstream and a re-lock would not help.\n",
    "created_at": "2026-08-19T07:47:54Z"
  },
  {
    "id": 22,
    "issue_id": "nixos-chl",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "KNOCK-ON EFFECT, recorded so it is not re-investigated each cycle:\nAideChangesDetected is now firing, and it is a CONSEQUENCE of this blocker\nrather than independent drift.\n\nThe chain, verified:\n  * aide-update (the re-baseline) is wired as `onSuccess` of the rebuild, and\n    also of nix-gc (modules/security/aide.nix:493). It only runs after a\n    SUCCESSFUL activation.\n  * It last ran 2026-08-17 23:26:13 -- immediately after the last successful\n    switch at 23:25:12. Nothing since, because nothing has switched since.\n  * Meanwhile 8 files have been committed under /etc/nixos, so aide-check now\n    reports drift against a two-day-old baseline: 1 added, 12 changed\n    (ExecMainStatus=5, AIDE's changes-detected code, with Result=success).\n  * The \"1 added\" matches EXACTLY the one new file in those commits,\n    modules/users/home-manager/starship-presets-compat.nix. That correlation is\n    what makes this attributable rather than merely plausible.\n\nSo the alert is reporting real, expected, already-reviewed changes. It will keep\nfiring until a switch succeeds, at which point aide-update re-baselines and it\nclears by itself. No action is needed on the AIDE side, and silencing it would\nalso hide genuine drift while the host is frozen.\n\nThis is worth knowing as a general property: while the host cannot switch, every\ncommitted change to /etc/nixos accumulates into this alert.\n",
    "created_at": "2026-08-19T11:44:47Z"
  },
  {
    "id": 24,
    "issue_id": "nixos-chl",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "READY-TO-APPLY PATCH for nix-config (2026-08-19). Two files. Verified to\nparse, and the toolchain it needs is verified to exist for aarch64.\n\nI cannot apply this myself -- it is a different repo with no local\ncheckout that I am authorised to push to -- so it is written out here in\nfull. Upstream has NOT fixed it: nix-config main moved from b95bf839 to\n177cac6f since our pin, and none of those six commits touch agnix, rust,\nor starship.\n\nWHY THIS SHAPE. default.nix already builds rustPlatform195 from\nrust-overlay pinned at exactly 1.95.0, but it is wrapped in an\nx86_64-linux-only optionalAttrs and applied only to qdrant. Rather than\nrestructure that gated block, this pins the toolchain inside the agnix\noverlay itself, which is self-contained and platform-agnostic.\n\nA NOTE ON WHY .override WAS NOT AN OPTION: pkgs.agnix has no .override\nattribute at all, because the overlay uses `with prev;\nrustPlatform.buildRustPackage` -- rustPlatform is closed over, not a\ncallPackage argument. That is why the file itself has to change.\n\nVERIFIED:\n  * nix-instantiate --parse on the new 30-agnix.nix: PARSES OK\n  * rust-bin.stable.\"1.95.0\".minimal resolves for aarch64-linux ->\n    /nix/store/4igka20bidwf1apcfrfn9s1yyivvdkkg-rust-minimal-1.95.0\n  * NOT verified: that agnix 0.49.0 actually compiles under 1.95.0. It\n    needs >= 1.95 for if_let_guard, but there could be a further\n    incompatibility behind it. The build is the proof.\n\n=== FILE 1: overlays/ai/default.nix -- one line ===\n    -  (import ./30-agnix.nix)\n    +  ((import ./30-agnix.nix) { inherit inputs; })\n\n=== FILE 2: overlays/ai/30-agnix.nix -- full replacement ===\n    { inputs }:\n    _final: prev:\n    let\n      source = (import ../../packages/source-catalog.nix \"ai\").agnix;\n    \n      # agnix >= 0.49 uses `if let` guards (RFC 2294), stabilised in Rust 1.95.0.\n      # nixpkgs' own rustc is older on some consumers of this overlay -- vulcan\n      # pins nixpkgs 25.11, which ships 1.91.1 -- so building against\n      # prev.rustPlatform fails there with:\n      #     error[E0658]: `if let` guards are experimental\n      # Pin the toolchain here rather than relying on whatever nixpkgs the\n      # consumer happens to have. Mirrors the rustPlatform195 pattern already used\n      # for qdrant in ./default.nix, but is NOT gated to x86_64 -- vulcan is\n      # aarch64 and was the host that broke.\n      rustPkgs = import inputs.nixpkgs {\n        inherit (prev.stdenv.hostPlatform) system;\n        overlays = [ inputs.rust-overlay.overlays.default ];\n      };\n      rust195 = rustPkgs.rust-bin.stable.\"1.95.0\".minimal;\n      rustPlatform195 = prev.makeRustPlatform {\n        cargo = rust195;\n        rustc = rust195;\n      };\n    in\n    {\n    \n      agnix = rustPlatform195.buildRustPackage rec {\n        pname = \"agnix\";\n        inherit (source) version;\n    \n        src =\n          assert source.source.fetcher == \"fetchFromGitHub\";\n          prev.fetchFromGitHub source.source.args;\n    \n        cargoHash = source.hashes.cargoHash;\n    \n        # Build all workspace binaries (CLI, LSP, MCP server)\n        cargoBuildFlags = [\n          \"--package\"\n          \"agnix-cli\"\n          \"--package\"\n          \"agnix-lsp\"\n          \"--package\"\n          \"agnix-mcp\"\n        ];\n    \n        doCheck = false;\n    \n        meta = with prev.lib; {\n          description = \"Linter and LSP for AI coding assistant config files (CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, hooks, MCP)\";\n          homepage = \"https://github.com/avifenesh/agnix\";\n          license = with licenses; [\n            mit\n            asl20\n          ];\n          mainProgram = \"agnix\";\n        };\n      };\n    \n    }\n",
    "created_at": "2026-08-19T15:46:19Z"
  }
]
#+end_src

* DONE [#B] Firewall: 17 TCP + 6 UDP ports are declared interface-agnostically
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-cmq
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 16:39]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-11 Tue 22:25]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-11 Tue 22:25]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

networking.firewall opens 17 TCP (22,53,80,139,443,445,465,587,853,993,1883,2222,4190,5357,5432,8096,21063)
and 6 UDP (53,137,138,3702,5353,5540) with trustedInterfaces=[lo] only.
NixOS emits allowedTCPPorts/allowedUDPPorts rules WITHOUT any '-i <iface>' filter
(firewall-iptables.nix:161-165,191-196), so they apply to every present and future interface.

Consequence: the moment any new interface appears (tailscale0, a VPN, a bridge), SMB 445, PostgreSQL 5432,
DNS 53, every nginx vhost on 80/443 and MQTT 1883 become reachable from it.
This is a latent property of the host, independent of any Tailscale decision, and worth scoping the
global port list to end0/wlp1s0f0 explicitly.

** Close Reason

Implemented as a default-deny guard chain (John's choice). nixos-fw-ifguard is inserted at nixos-fw position 3 — after NixOS's invariant lo and ESTABLISHED,RELATED accepts, before every port accept — with a RETURN per legitimately-served interface (end0, wlp1s0f0, podman0, hermes-br0, ve+, vm+) and nixos-fw-refuse at the end. Any FUTURE interface therefore inherits nothing.

Implementation note: the obvious form 'iptables ! -i a ! -i b ...' is IMPOSSIBLE — iptables 1.8.11 rejects it with 'multiple --in-interface options not allowed'. Caught by validating the syntax in a throwaway chain before switching; it would otherwise have broken the firewall start script.

Verified post-switch: guard at position 3 ahead of hermes-isolate and all port accepts, v4 and v6 both present, exactly one jump (no stacking across restarts), refuse rule at 0 packets/0 bytes after 45s while the RETURN rules matched real inbound traffic, zero failed units.

* DEFER [#D] august-lock-garage-door chronically at ~87% ICMP: works fine in HA, needs John's physical-or-reclassify decision
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-cnc
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 10:08]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 12:00]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:DEFERRED: [2026-11-18 Wed 09:00]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

IoTDeviceChronicallyUnreachable fires periodically for august-lock-garage-door.lan
and there is no open issue for anyone to act on. nixos-mn4 built the rule that
detects this and closed 2026-08-16 leaving "the underlying device question is
unchanged and still John's" -- but left nothing open, so the alert recurs pointing
at a closed ticket.

THE DEVICE IS NOT BROKEN, checked rather than assumed, and this is why it is a
decision rather than a repair:
  lock.garage  state=locked, and ZERO unavailable transitions in HA over 7 days.
So it locks, reports, and never drops out of Home Assistant. Only ICMP is degraded.

ICMP availability, remarkably stable rather than decaying:
  1h 86.7%   24h 89.3%   7d 87.8%   30d 87.0%
Siblings for contrast: side-door 96.8%/24h, front-door 99.2%/24h. So this is a
property of this one lock's radio or position, not a fabric-wide condition.

ACTUAL ALERT BEHAVIOUR, which is milder than nixos-mn4 predicted: it fired 1050
minutes over the last 7 days (~10% of the week), not the "almost continuously" that
issue expected from 497 of 720 qualifying hours. The `for: 30m` plus the 24h
averaging window smooth it considerably. Worth recording because mn4's own caution
was "if this ever becomes a permanent fixture for several devices at once it has
stopped being a control" -- it has not: exactly ONE device is under the 0.9
threshold right now, so the control is still meaningful.

FOR JOHN -- the same two options nixos-mn4 framed, unchanged:
  (a) Physical: move or re-seat the lock / improve its radio path. It is a
      security device, so I will not touch it.
  (b) Reclassify it host_group="iot-noping" (or accept it as chronic), which
      silences the alert for this device without weakening it for others.
Choosing (b) is a real loss of visibility on a lock, which is exactly why it is
not mine to decide.

Filed and deferred to match the other physical/RF items (nixos-ly2, h2u, rsn) so
they surface together rather than one at a time.

* DONE [#B] Dovecot 2.4 migration: NixOS module rewritten, 2.3-style config no longer valid
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-crb
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 21:11]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-09-01 Tue 00:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Not a Dovecot 2.4 migration -- the stable module was never rewritten. Caused by the userPkgs routing (nixos-7bp) handing dovecot.nix unstable pigeonhole 2.4.5. Fixed by reverting the routing; dovecot units now reference only dovecot-2.3.21.1 + pigeonhole-0.5.21.1, service active with NRestarts=0 and IMAP answering on 143. No migration work required.
:DEFERRED: [2026-10-01 Thu 09:00]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
THIS ONE TOOK MAIL DOWN. Surfaced by the same 2026-08-31 flake update
(generation 2611). Mail was unavailable 20:28-20:58 and postfix queued 44
messages / 855 KB before the rollback restored it.

SYMPTOM: dovecot.service fails in ExecStartPre, five restarts, then 'Start
request repeated too quickly'.
  doveconf: Fatal: Error in configuration file /etc/dovecot/dovecot.conf line 1:
  The first setting must be dovecot_config_version

CAUSE: nixpkgs has rewritten the NixOS Dovecot module for Dovecot 2.4. Confirmed
by evaluation: services.dovecot2.package now errors with 'can no longer be used
since it's been removed'. The new module supports dovecot_config_version
(nixos/modules/services/mail/dovecot.nix). The generated /etc/dovecot/dovecot.conf
is still 2.3-style (starts 'base_dir = /run/dovecot2', no version line), and a
2.4 doveconf rejects it.

NOTE THE CLOSURE CARRIES BOTH: dovecot-2.3.21.1 (what the unit's ExecStart used)
AND dovecot-2.4.5 + dovecot-pigeonhole-2.4.5 (pulled in by the new module).
pkgs.dovecot=2.3.21.1 and pkgs.dovecot_pigeonhole=0.5.21.1 remain a consistent
2.3 pair, so the mismatch is between the module and the package set.

WHY THIS IS NOT A QUICK FIX. 2.4 is a config-language change, not a version bump,
and modules/services/dovecot.nix carries a lot that has to survive it:
,  * sieve scripts (default.sieve, process-good.sieve) compiled with sievec
,  * imapsieve + rspamd retraining integration
,  * FTS indexing
,  * LMTP delivery from postfix (mail delivery depends on this working)
,  * per-user mail location under /var/mail

REQUIREMENTS BEFORE ATTEMPTING:
  1. Back up the mail spool first. This is live mail.
  2. Have a rollback plan and expect several iterations.
  3. Do it ALONE, not bundled with other upgrades -- bundling is what produced
     the three-way failure on 2026-08-31.

BLOCKS: 'nix flake update' cannot be deployed until this is resolved, since any
update that carries the new module breaks mail on switch.
#+end_example
