* DONE [#E] nocobase is SIGKILLed by the container updater: shutdown exceeds the 90s stop timeout
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-h6j
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 00:49]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 04:46]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-20 Thu 04:46]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
nocobase is SIGKILLed (status=137) by the daily container updater whenever its
image actually changes, because its shutdown exceeds TimeoutStopUSec=1min 30s.

MEASURED over 30 days of journal:
  containers exiting 137: nocobase only -- 2 occurrences, no other container ever
  dates: 2026-08-16 and 2026-08-20 00:27:14
  trigger both times: "Update and restart Podman containers"
    (modules/maintenance/timers.nix, OnCalendar=daily). The updater runs daily
    but only restarts a container whose image changed, which is why this is
    twice in 30 days and not thirty.

RULED OUT, because 137 is ambiguous and the obvious reading is wrong here:
,  * NOT an OOM kill -- zero out-of-memory/oom-kill/oom_reaper entries in the
    window, and MemoryMax=infinity against MemoryPeak=795MB.
,  * NOT a crash -- it restarts in the same second and comes back healthy.

CURRENT STATE IS FINE: active/running since 00:27:14, Result=success,
NRestarts=0, https://nocobase.vulcan.lan/ returns 200.

WHY IT IS STILL WORTH FIXING, at low priority:
  1. Every occurrence writes a genuine "Failed with result exit-code" record. A
     recurring benign failure is how a real one gets ignored.
  2. An ungraceful kill of a Node app mid-write is a small but real risk. The
     durable state is in PostgreSQL, which is why this is P4 and not higher.

NOT FIXED, because raising TimeoutStopUSec is treating the symptom without
knowing why nocobase needs >90s to stop -- and 90s is already generous. The
question to answer first is what it is doing during shutdown. If the answer is
"nothing, it just ignores SIGTERM", the fix is upstream or a STOPSIGNAL override,
not a longer wait.

DISCOVERED-FROM: the 4-hour service-failure scan in the 2026-08-20 health cycle.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

Diagnosed. Closing as accepted-and-documented rather than fixed, because the fix
this issue proposed does not work and would have made things worse.

MY ORIGINAL DIAGNOSIS WAS WRONG. I filed this saying "shutdown exceeds
TimeoutStopUSec=1min 30s". systemd's 90s is never reached. The real clock is the
IMAGE's own StopTimeout=10, enforced by podman:

  00:27:03  Stopping Podman container nocobase...
  00:27:13  podman warning: "StopSignal SIGTERM failed to stop container"
  00:27:14  Main process exited, code=exited, status=137

Exactly 10 seconds, matching podman inspect StopSignal=SIGTERM StopTimeout=10.

WHY NO TIMEOUT VALUE FIXES IT -- the part that makes this won't-fix rather than
not-yet-fixed. PID 1 in this image is docker-entrypoint.sh with no `trap`. The
kernel installs no default signal action for PID 1, so a SIGTERM with no handler
is discarded outright. Nothing is waiting to act on it, so a longer timeout only
delays the identical SIGKILL and slows every image update.

The script does `exec "$@"` at line 163, which would normally hand PID 1 to the
app -- but Entrypoint and Cmd are BOTH this same script, so it execs into itself
and PID 1 stays the shell. Verified with podman top: the real app is five
processes deep (1 -> node -> sh -> node -> sh -> node -> node /app/nocobase).

`--init` / catatonit does not help either: it forwards SIGTERM to PID 1, which is
still the trap-less shell.

SCOPE, re-measured: 2 occurrences in 30 days (2026-08-16, 2026-08-20), this
container only, no other container on the host has ever exited 137. The trigger
is the daily container updater, which only restarts a container whose image
actually changed -- hence twice, not thirty times.

RISK, stated honestly rather than dismissed: durable state is in PostgreSQL, but
there IS one rw bind mount, /var/lib/nocobase -> /app/nocobase/storage (file
storage). A SIGKILL mid-write could in principle damage a file there. It is
accepted because the kill lands during a scheduled update rather than under load,
and because no timeout change reduces that risk -- the process never gets a
chance to flush either way.

RECORDED IN THE CODE, not just here: modules/users/home-manager/nocobase.nix now
carries a DO-NOT-ADD-StopTimeout comment with the measurement and the PID-1
reasoning, next to TimeoutStartSec where someone would reach to "fix" this.

A real fix belongs upstream in the image's entrypoint (trap and forward, or exec
the app so it becomes PID 1).

* DOING [#C] VTherm eco setpoint does not reach the Nest; eco silently defeated for 10h on 08-17
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-i4e
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 01:01]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-25 Tue 23:53]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
Setting climate.upstairs_vtherm to eco does not reliably push the eco setpoint
to the Google Nest (climate.upstairs). Reported by John 2026-08-19. Diagnosed,
NOT fixed -- the remedy changes HVAC behaviour and the config lives in Home
Assistant's UI config entries, not this repo.

WHAT JOHN SAW WAS REAL, BUT TONIGHT'S INSTANCE WAS A RED HERRING:
    00:53:02  VTherm -> eco, setpoint 80 -> 88   (context 01a019026571, user)
    00:54:14  Nest   -> 88                       (context 01a019037977, user)
The two contexts are DIFFERENT. Home Assistant propagates context through
service calls, so a VTherm-initiated write would share that context or parent
it. It did not: tonight's Nest change was a SEPARATE user action 72 seconds
later. Over 7 days only 2 of 945 Nest state rows carry a user context at all,
and both are John. So the VTherm did not propagate tonight either -- John set it
himself while checking.

THE REAL FAILURE, with cost attached. On 2026-08-17 eco was set at 23:29:00 and
held until 09:33 the next morning. Across that 10-hour window the Nest's
setpoint never left 81, and the Nest ACTIVELY COOLED:

    hvac_action=idle      21 samples   room 78-81   setpoint 81
    hvac_action=cooling    8 samples   room 78-82   setpoint 81

Eco wanted no cooling until 88. The house was cooled to 78-82 for ten hours
instead, so this is not cosmetic -- it silently defeats eco and burns AC runtime.

THE FAILURE IS SILENT. Checked and ruled out at both eco transitions:
,  * A-91 (the known `unsupported operand type(s) for -: 'float' and 'NoneType'`
    at nest/climate.py, which silently discards setpoints): ZERO occurrences in
    3 days. Instrument verified working -- the same journal holds 25 TypeErrors,
    66 Tracebacks and 2575 HA lines over that period, so the absence is real.
    The 25 TypeErrors are all `'SmartErrorCode' object is not subscriptable`,
    which is Kasa, not Nest.
,  * Nest OAuth failure (google_nest_sdm AuthException, 400 Bad Request): 2
    occurrences, both on Aug 16 only.
,  * nest/climate.py traceback (set_cool / async_set_temperature): 1 occurrence,
    Aug 16 only.
None of these fall on 08-17 23:29 or 08-19 00:53. Nothing was logged at all.

MECHANISM, hypothesis not yet proven. The Nest setpoint is rewritten constantly
by the integration when the VTherm has demand -- the 79/80/81/82 oscillation
visible all day is auto-regulation at work, and the VTherm currently reports
regulated_target_temperature = 90.0. During eco with the room already BELOW the
eco setpoint there is no demand, and no writes occur at all. The Nest is left in
`cool` mode carrying its last setpoint (81), so the Nest's own control loop
resumes cooling at 82 regardless of what the VTherm wants.

That fits every observation, but it is inference from the state history rather
than something I have confirmed against the versatile_thermostat configuration.

WHY NOT FIXED HERE:
,  * The candidate remedies all CHANGE COOLING BEHAVIOUR: having the VTherm drive
    the underlying entity to `off` in eco rather than leaving it in `cool`,
    changing the auto-regulation mode/threshold so a setpoint is pushed even
    with no demand, or adding an automation to mirror the eco setpoint onto the
    Nest. Which is right depends on what John wants the Nest to do when the
    VTherm is idle.
,  * The VTherm is a UI config entry. Its settings live under
    /var/lib/hass/.storage/, which this repo's rules forbid reading or editing.
,  * Nothing in /etc/nixos ties these two entities together, so there is no
    repo-side change that would fix it.
,  * Independently, no NixOS change can be deployed right now (nixos-chl).

SUGGESTED NEXT STEP: confirm the mechanism by watching one eco transition where
the room is ABOVE 88 (so demand exists). If the setpoint propagates in that case
and not otherwise, the no-demand hypothesis is confirmed and the fix is a VTherm
configuration change rather than anything in this repo.
#+end_example

* DONE [#A] DATA LOSS: Databases and Home restic repos lost all but the newest snapshot on 2026-08-22
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-i94
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-22 Sat 20:52]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-22 Sat 22:05]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-22 Sat 22:05]
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
NEEDS JOHN. Cause is OFF-HOST; I have put a reversible hold on the two timers and taken no other action.

WHAT HAPPENED: between 08:51 and 14:05 PDT on 2026-08-22, jwiegley-Databases went from 17 snapshots to 1, and jwiegley-Home from 16 to 1. In both repos the sole survivor is that morning's own backup (Databases 02:50, Home 03:50); every older snapshot is gone.

EVIDENCE IT IS REAL, NOT A MEASUREMENT BUG (this was my first hypothesis, since I refactored the collector the same day, and I disproved it):
 - restic_restore_size_bytes{Databases} fell 1383.7 -> 82.1 GiB, a factor of 16.9, exactly matching 17 snapshots -> 1. A count-only bug cannot move restore_size.
 - restic_unique_size_bytes fell 170.3 -> 74.7 GiB, so unique CONTENT is gone, not just an index entry.
 - restic_repo_size_bytes: Databases 97.9 -> 53.3 GiB, Home 240.7 -> 58.2 GiB.
 - I diffed the old and new collector scripts in the nix store (y3yh07ji... vs i1hy4pp...). The only changes are the repo list and the URL lookup, and the resulting URLs are BYTE-IDENTICAL for both repos. The collector queried exactly what it always did.
 - check_success=1 throughout, and the other 9 repos are unaffected and normal.

WHAT DID NOT DO IT: no restic unit ran against either repo in the window. Last runs were restic-backups-Databases 02:52:34 and restic-backups-Home 03:54:46, and each logged 'Applying Policy: keep 7 daily, 5 weekly, 12 monthly, 3 yearly' with 'keep 17 snapshots: remove 1' and 'keep 16 snapshots: remove 1'. That policy cannot reduce a repo to one snapshot, and the daily logs show a rock-steady 17/16 every day from 08-15 through 08-22. Only restic-backups-Machines (a different repo) and the read-only, --no-lock metrics collector ran in the window.

So something deleted them SERVER-SIDE or from another client: a B2 lifecycle rule on those two buckets, a manual forget/prune elsewhere, or B2-side action. Only John can check the B2 account.

TIME-CRITICAL, WHICH IS WHY I ACTED: tonight's runs (02:52 and 03:54) execute 'forget --prune'. Prune permanently deletes data no surviving snapshot references, which is now the entire contents of the 16 and 15 lost snapshots. That would destroy any chance of recovery via B2 file versions. I therefore ran 'systemctl stop' on restic-backups-Databases.timer and restic-backups-Home.timer -- a hold, not a config change, reversible with 'systemctl start'. The other 9 timers are untouched and still scheduled.

CAVEAT ON THE HOLD: a nixos-rebuild switch WILL re-arm those timers, because the stop is runtime-only. If you rebuild before deciding, stop them again or the prune runs.

COST OF THE HOLD: those two get no offsite backup until released. Local fallback is thin for exactly these: tank/Home and tank/Backups/PostgreSQL each hold 33 ZFS snapshots but only back to Aug 19, i.e. 3 days. The offsite repo was the deep history.

SUGGESTED NEXT STEPS (John's call): check B2 for lifecycle rules on jwiegley-Databases and jwiegley-Home and whether hidden/older file versions still exist; decide whether to attempt recovery before allowing any prune; then release the timers.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

NOT A FAULT — John trimmed these two restic repos deliberately on 2026-08-22. Closing as expected behaviour.

My diagnosis of WHAT happened was correct: jwiegley-Databases went 17 snapshots -> 1 and jwiegley-Home 16 -> 1, with restore_size collapsing 1383.7 -> 82.1 GiB (factor 16.9, matching 17 -> 1) and unique content dropping 170.3 -> 74.7 GiB. My conclusion that it originated off-host was also right, in that nothing on vulcan did it. I was wrong to escalate it as data loss without considering deliberate operator action as a candidate cause alongside lifecycle rules and stray clients.

HOLD RELEASED: restic-backups-Databases.timer and restic-backups-Home.timer were stopped to prevent tonight's forget --prune from permanently deleting the unreferenced data. Both restarted and verified active, next runs 02:50 and 03:50; all 11 restic timers armed. No config was ever changed and no repo was touched.

FOLLOW-ON HANDLED: ResticRepoSizeShrunk compares against a 14-day average that still carries the pre-trim sizes, so it would keep firing until those samples age out — measured ~3.5 days for Databases and ~11.1 days for Home. Silence 5470ae8c covers alertname=ResticRepoSizeShrunk with repository=~'Databases|Home' for 12 days, after which the average reflects the new baseline. If it fires again after that expiry, it is worth a real look.

Also self-inflicted and resolved: BackupTimerInactive fired because the backup-status-exporter (15-min cadence) sampled while the timers were held. A forced exporter run put backup_timer_active back to 1 for both; the rule group evaluates every 300s so it clears on the following evaluation.

* DONE [#D] Nest setpoint mirror silently drops writes on API 429 (no retry/catch); divergence itself is by design
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-ii9
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 12:56]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 15:27]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-20 Thu 15:27]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
The Node-RED node that mirrors the Upstairs VTherm setpoint onto the Nest fails
intermittently, and when it fails the setpoint write is LOST -- not retried, not
caught, not surfaced anywhere except one log line.

FOUND BY: alert NodeRedFlowErrors firing 2026-08-20T19:30Z. The alert did its job;
this is what was behind it.

TWO DISTINCT FAILURE CLASSES on [api-call-service:mirror setpoint to Nest], across
the whole retained journal (3 errors on this node):

  2026-08-20 12:14:49  HomeAssistantError -- Google SDM API returned
      "Too Many Requests response from API (429): RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED (429): Rate limited"
      while setting climate.upstairs to {'temperature': 25.0}. Google's SDM API has
      hard per-project quotas; the write was rejected outright.
  2026-08-19 12:52:04 and 12:52:45  ValidationError (x2)

Per-day error counts for the node's tab over 7 days: 0,2,0,0,1,5,1 -- intermittent,
not constant, which is why it has not been obvious.

WHY THE FAILURE IS SILENT -- this is the actual defect, verified against flows.json:
,  * the node's wires are [[]] -- its output is connected to NOTHING
,  * its tab (Office) has ZERO catch nodes
,  * its tab has ZERO subflow instances, so the repo's existing "act until observed"
    retry subflow is not in the path
So a 429 throws, nothing handles it, and the intended setpoint is discarded. The
VTherm believes it pushed a value the Nest never received.

OBSERVABLE CONSEQUENCE: the two thermostats diverge and stay diverged until some
later write succeeds. From HA's recorder on 2026-08-19: at 14:53 the VTherm read 86
while the Nest read 87; at 21:03 the Nest read 88 with no corresponding VTherm
change. John noticed exactly this divergence and asked about it on 2026-08-20.

INCIDENTAL, NOT ASSERTED AS A BUG: the node lives on the *Office* tab while
targeting climate.upstairs. That may be deliberate placement, but it is worth a
glance -- a node whose name, tab and target disagree is hard to find when it breaks.

NOT FIXED, and deliberately so. This is HVAC behaviour and the fix is a real design
choice, not a strike:
,  * a bounded catch -> delay -> retry would ride out a 429, but a retry that lands
    minutes later re-applies a setpoint the schedule may have already superseded;
,  * the repo's existing retry subflow exists precisely for this shape, but adding it
    changes when and how the Nest is commanded;
,  * rate-limit backoff might belong upstream of the node instead, since hammering a
    quota-limited API is what produced the 429 in the first place.
Also relevant: prior experience on this host is that eco-preset writes should be
routed to the VTherm and never to the Nest, so the mirror direction itself deserves
scrutiny before adding retries to it.

Note the alert threshold is >0 errors/hour, so it fires on a single occurrence and
self-resolves an hour later. That is working as designed and was left alone.
#+end_example

** Notes

#+begin_example
CORRECTION 2026-08-20 15:2x -- one of this issue's two claims was WRONG, and it is
the one that made the issue look alarming. Correcting before anyone acts on it.

WHAT I GOT WRONG: the issue's "OBSERVABLE CONSEQUENCE" section attributed the
VTherm/Nest divergence (VTherm 86, Nest 87/88) to dropped mirror writes. It is not
that. It is the system working exactly as designed.

,  * The +2 offset is DELIBERATE, not drift. Every VTherm change today is followed by
    the Nest within the same minute at +2: 09:16 80/82, 11:16 86/88, 11:22 80/82,
    11:47 86/88, 15:08 86/88. The mirror is functioning.
,  * The 86 is an AWAY SETBACK, correctly applied. The VTherm's presence feature is
    driven by binary_sensor.office_occupied_debounced_15m
    (home-assistant.nix:902, delay_off = 00:15:00) which follows the FP300 mmWave.
    Every 86 today lands EXACTLY 15 minutes after an occupancy 'off', and every
    return to 80 is immediate on 'on':
        11:01 off -> 11:16 setpoint 86
        11:32 off -> 11:47 setpoint 86
        14:53 off -> 15:08 setpoint 86   (current state)
        09:16 on  -> 09:16 setpoint 80
        11:22 on  -> 11:22 setpoint 80
        13:29 on  -> 13:29 setpoint 80
    A 13:17->13:29 absence of 12 minutes correctly did NOT trigger the setback,
    which is the debounce doing precisely what its comment says it exists to do.

So "86 and 88 in the middle of the day" is not a fault at all: it means the office
has been empty 15+ minutes. John asked about exactly this pair on 2026-08-20 and we
both set it aside as stale data; it was live, and it was correct.

WHAT REMAINS TRUE, and is still the actual issue: when the mirror DOES fail, the
write is discarded silently. Verified against flows.json -- the node's output is
wired to nothing ([[]]), its tab has zero catch nodes and zero subflow instances.
Three failures in the retained journal:
  2026-08-20 12:14:49  HomeAssistantError, Google SDM 429 RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED
  2026-08-19 12:52:04  ValidationError
  2026-08-19 12:52:45  ValidationError
On those occasions the Nest keeps its previous setpoint and nothing retries or
reports. The exposure is real but NARROWER than this issue first claimed: it is a
missed setback or a missed restore at the moment of a presence transition, not a
standing divergence.

SEVERITY UNCHANGED at P3, but for a different reason than originally written: not
"the thermostats are diverging", but "roughly 1 in N presence transitions silently
fails to reach the Nest, and nothing tells you which".
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Implemented option 2 (bounded retry WITH a freshness check) at John's direction
2026-08-20.

WHAT WAS BUILT -- 5 nodes added to the Office tab, wired as:
  catch "catch Nest mirror failure"   scope=[ccf5d02161b0136d], i.e. the mirror node only
    -> function "nest mirror retry gate"      bounded at MAX=3; increments msg.retryCount,
                                              node.warn + give up beyond that
    -> delay "wait 60s (rate-limit backoff)"  60s; SDM quotas are per-minute
    -> api-current-state "re-read VTherm setpoint"  reads climate.upstairs_vtherm
    -> function "rebuild fresh setpoint"      THE FRESHNESS CHECK
    -> back into the mirror node (a further failure re-enters the catch, bounded by the gate)

WHY THE FRESHNESS CHECK IS THE WHOLE POINT: the retry does NOT replay the setpoint
captured when the call failed. By the time it runs (60s+) presence may have flipped,
and re-applying a stale value would actively fight the VTherm -- you walk back into
the office, the retry lands, and it re-applies the away setback you just cancelled.
It re-reads the VTherm's CURRENT setpoint instead: if that changed, the newer value
is the right one to send; if not, it is a faithful retry. That was the tradeoff John
chose between, and it is the reason this is not a plain retry.

HOW IT WAS APPLIED: by editing /var/lib/node-red/flows.json directly and restarting
node-red. The admin API was NOT used -- adminAuth reads its bearer token from
/run/secrets/node-red/api-tokens, and reading anything under /run/secrets is
forbidden on this host. flows.json had not been touched since 2026-08-19 12:54, so
there were no pending UI edits to clobber. A timestamped backup was taken first.

SCHEMAS COPIED, NOT INVENTED: catch/delay/api-current-state node JSON was taken from
existing working nodes of the same type in the same flows.json rather than written
from memory, because these are plugin-specific and version-sensitive
(node-red-contrib-home-assistant-websocket v7, api-current-state version 3).

VERIFIED:
,  * JSON parses; 424 nodes; zero duplicate ids; every wire target resolves.
,  * Graph traced end to end; catch scope is exactly the mirror node; the ORIGINAL
    path (function c04ec2fa32b125ea -> mirror) is unchanged.
,  * node-red restarted clean: ActiveState=active, Result=success, "Started flows",
    ZERO error lines, no "missing type" warnings.
,  * All 5 nodes still present after node-red's own startup write.
,  * msg.data.attributes shape confirmed BY PRECEDENT, not assumption: the existing
    "read OpenUV state" -> "compute window" pair uses the same node type and reads
    msg.data / data.attributes. Optional chaining (?.) already appears in this
    flow's function nodes, so the syntax is supported by the runtime in use.
,  * Setpoints unchanged across the restart (VTherm 86 / Nest 88), so the change
    caused no stray writes.
,  * Captured in the backup mirror: /tank/Backups/NodeRED/flows.json now 353,954
    bytes containing the new node (node-red-backup.service, Result=success).

NOT VERIFIED, stated plainly: the retry path has NOT been exercised end to end,
because doing so requires a genuine Google SDM 429 and I will not manufacture one.
What is verified is that it loads, wires correctly, and cannot fire spuriously (it
is reachable ONLY through a catch scoped to a single node). Both function nodes call
node.warn on their failure branches, so a malfunctioning retry surfaces through the
existing NodeRedFlowErrors alert rather than silently -- which is a strict
improvement on the previous state, where the failure was silent by construction.

STILL OPEN, and deliberately not chased here: the +2 offset between VTherm and Nest
is NOT produced by this mirror, which sends the VTherm setpoint 1:1
(msg.payload = { temperature: sp }). Several other nodes also write
climate.upstairs -- "Set HVAC Temp", "Turn on HVAC", "Set HVAC Mode", each targeting
five climate entities at once. One of those is the likelier source. Worth a separate
look; it is cosmetic rather than harmful, and this retry does not depend on it.
#+end_example

7. Lines 338-344 say two Nix-cited docs paths dangle and both are annotated at the citation site. Neither half holds now: modules/core/memory-limits.nix no longer cites OOM_ANALYSIS_2025-11-12.md at all, and a NEW unannotated dangle appeared -- see the discovered-from issue.

* DEFER [#D] budget-board-server dies on rebuilds: libgssapi_krb5.so.2 missing from the image
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-jhl
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 20:45]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 10:32]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:DEFERRED: [2026-11-18 Wed 09:00]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
budget-board-server.service exits during rebuilds with:

    Error: libgssapi_krb5.so.2: cannot open shared object file: No such file or directory

It is NOT currently broken -- the service is active, Result=success, and it self-heals.
Found during the 2026-08-19 health cycle by scanning the last 4 hours for units that
entered a failed state, which is a class `systemctl --failed` cannot show after the fact.

SHAPE OF THE FAILURE. 10 occurrences in the whole journal, clustered in two bursts:
6 on 2026-08-15 and 4 on 2026-08-19. Both days had several `/etc/nixos/build switch`
runs. The one today was at 19:25:00, between two Grist switches; the unit came back
at 19:26:20, 80 seconds later, and has NRestarts=0 since.

WHY IT SELF-HEALS: Restart=always. That is the only thing standing between this and a
real outage -- if a burst ever exceeded StartLimitBurst the service would stay down.

WHERE THE LIBRARY IS MISSING. ExecStart is podman, so this is a container: the missing
libgssapi_krb5.so.2 is inside the IMAGE, not on the host. LD_LIBRARY_PATH is not set on
the unit, so there is no host-side shim involved.

MOST LIKELY MECHANISM, not yet proven: budget-board uses Npgsql to reach the host
PostgreSQL. A rebuild restarts PostgreSQL; the reconnect path attempts GSSAPI/Kerberos
negotiation, hits the absent library, and the process exits. That would explain both the
clustering around switches and why it works fine the rest of the time -- the GSSAPI path
is only taken during renegotiation. Worth confirming by correlating the exact timestamps
against postgresql.service restarts before acting.

CANDIDATE FIXES, none applied:
  1. Add a connection-string option to stop Npgsql attempting GSSAPI. Cheapest and
     entirely on our side of the boundary -- but see the caveat below.
  2. Ship krb5 in the image. Not possible without forking or layering the upstream image.
  3. Order budget-board-server after postgresql.service so it does not reconnect
     mid-restart. Treats the trigger rather than the cause, and races are still possible.

NOT DONE AUTONOMOUSLY: option 1 means editing the database connection string, which on
this host is likely SOPS-managed, and touching secrets is John's call. Option 2 changes
what image we run. Neither is a health-cycle change.

There IS already an alert for the resulting outage shape --
BudgetBoardDatabaseConnectionFailed joins the unit state against the blackbox probe -- so
a sustained failure would be caught. This issue is about the underlying fragility, not a
monitoring gap.
#+end_example

    "author": "johnw",

Recording before anyone implements something redundant.\n\nmodules/containers/budgetboard-quadlet.nix already has, on the server unit:\n  * an ExecStartPre that POLLS pg_isready in a 60-iteration loop rather than a one-shot\n    check -- the same idiom every DB-dependent container here uses\n  * Restart = \"always\" with RestartSec = 15s\n\nSo budget-board does not start until PostgreSQL is accepting connections, and the\n\"reconnect races a restarting PostgreSQL\" story does not fit as cleanly as the issue body\nsuggests: the failure happens AFTER pg_isready has already succeeded. * DEFER [#D] Gitea->GitHub auto-mirror automation does not exist; 3 repos need GitHub repos created
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-jqs
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: feature
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 12:35]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-19 Wed 16:47]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

John expects every public Gitea repo to get a GitHub mirror automatically,
created daily if absent. That automation DOES NOT EXIST and never did. Recorded
on his instruction NOT to build it now.

WHAT EXISTS, in modules/services/github-gitea-mirror.nix (382 lines, 3 units):
  github-mirror              GitHub -> Gitea. Discovers GitHub repos and creates
                             them IN GITEA. Its own option is documented as
                             "Gitea user to create repositories under" (line 235).
                             This is the OPPOSITE direction.
  sync-push-mirrors          POSTs push_mirrors-sync to mirrors that ALREADY
                             exist. The only POST in the module (line 43-45).
  update-github-mirror-tokens  token refresh.

So restarting sync-push-mirrors legitimately does nothing for a repo with no
push mirror -- it iterates repos, finds no mirror to kick, and exits 0. That is
exactly the "nothing is happening" symptom.

NOTHING in the module creates a GitHub repo, and nothing creates a Gitea push
mirror. The initial wiring per repo has always been manual; only the ongoing
syncing is automatic (sync-push-mirrors discovers repos dynamically, so a mirror
starts being synced with no config change once it exists).

THE CURRENT GAP, measured 2026-08-19 against the Gitea public API (queried
ANONYMOUSLY -- no token read, and anonymous access returns exactly the public
set, which is the filter John specified):

    public Gitea repos          180
      of which pull mirrors     165   already originate on GitHub
      Gitea-native              15
        with a push mirror      12
        WITHOUT                  3   <-- need a GitHub repo created

    icp             updated 2025-11-10   "Code relating to the Internet Compute..."
    org-agents      updated 2026-08-19   "An implementation of Tinderbox agents"
    pushme-config   updated 2026-08-12

Self-check: the reverse difference -- push mirrors belonging to something other
than a public-native repo -- is EMPTY, so 15 = 12 + 3 accounts for everything.

IF THIS IS BUILT LATER, the design question that stopped it is worth keeping:
creating a public GitHub repo is PUBLISHING, and it is not reversible in any
meaningful sense (forks, caches, archives outlive a delete). A daily unit that
auto-creates from "is public in Gitea" fails OPEN -- a repo made public in Gitea
by mistake is on GitHub by morning. The options considered were: dry-run then
arm, live with a maintained exclude list (still fails open), or opt-in via a
Gitea topic (fails closed, but contradicts "every public repo"). John declined
all of them for now.

Note also that private Gitea repos are outside this count by construction, since
the enumeration was anonymous.

* DONE [#D] session-gather re-hashes the whole tree EVERY run (--checksum): ~1h, ~125 GB nightly
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-k2g
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: question
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 00:45]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-15 Sat 23:18]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-15 Sat 23:17]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

bin/gather-sessions passes --checksum, which makes rsync read EVERY file on both
sides on every run: locally off the archive, and remotely over SSH on each
source. Runs are therefore not cheap deltas -- the whole tree is re-hashed
nightly.

MEASURED, first full pass on 2026-08-14:
    started 21:55:16, ended 22:45:01  =  49m45s
    27 rows attempted, 8 skipped (clio travelling), 0 failed, exit 0
    124.5 GB read from disk by the unit
    archive: 118G used / 476G apparent (sparse, so apparent >> allocated)

That cost recurs every night at 02:30, and it is paid on the remote side too:
each source host hashes its own copy, including a VPS over the public internet
and a laptop that travels.

WHY IT IS THERE, and why this is a trade rather than a bug: --checksum is what
catches an edit that keeps the same size AND mtime. The repo has a deliberate
regression test for exactly that case (same-stat.bin in
test/gather-sessions.bash), so removing the flag would silently weaken a
property someone chose to protect.

THE TRADE:
  keep --checksum  -- correct for same-size/same-mtime edits; hours of I/O
                      nightly on vulcan and on every source host
  drop --checksum  -- runs become fast size/mtime deltas; risks missing an
                      in-place edit that preserves both

Session transcripts are largely append-only, which argues the practical risk of
dropping it is low -- an append changes size. But that is a judgement about
John's data, not a mechanical fact, so it is his call.

A middle option exists: keep --checksum but stop re-hashing what cannot have
changed, e.g. --size-only for the archived/immutable roots (codex-cli-archived)
while keeping full checksums on live ones. That needs per-row configuration the
TSV does not currently express.

PENDING: the second consecutive run started 2026-08-15 00:43:48 will give the
INCREMENTAL cost, which is the number that actually decides this. If an
unchanged pass still takes ~50 minutes and reads ~124 GB, the case for changing
something is much stronger than if it drops sharply.

** Notes

bin/gather-sessions passes --checksum, which makes rsync read EVERY file on both
sides on every run: locally off the archive, and remotely over SSH on each
source. Runs are therefore not cheap deltas -- the whole tree is re-hashed
nightly.

MEASURED, first full pass on 2026-08-14:
    started 21:55:16, ended 22:45:01  =  49m45s
    27 rows attempted, 8 skipped (clio travelling), 0 failed, exit 0
    124.5 GB read from disk by the unit
    archive: 118G used / 476G apparent (sparse, so apparent >> allocated)

That cost recurs every night at 02:30, and it is paid on the remote side too:
each source host hashes its own copy, including a VPS over the public internet
and a laptop that travels.

WHY IT IS THERE, and why this is a trade rather than a bug: --checksum is what
catches an edit that keeps the same size AND mtime. The repo has a deliberate
regression test for exactly that case (same-stat.bin in
test/gather-sessions.bash), so removing the flag would silently weaken a
property someone chose to protect.

THE TRADE:
  keep --checksum  -- correct for same-size/same-mtime edits; hours of I/O
                      nightly on vulcan and on every source host
  drop --checksum  -- runs become fast size/mtime deltas; risks missing an
                      in-place edit that preserves both

Session transcripts are largely append-only, which argues the practical risk of
dropping it is low -- an append changes size. But that is a judgement about
John's data, not a mechanical fact, so it is his call.

A middle option exists: keep --checksum but stop re-hashing what cannot have
changed, e.g. --size-only for the archived/immutable roots (codex-cli-archived)
while keeping full checksums on live ones. That needs per-row configuration the
TSV does not currently express.

PENDING: the second consecutive run started 2026-08-15 00:43:48 will give the
INCREMENTAL cost, which is the number that actually decides this. If an
unchanged pass still takes ~50 minutes and reads ~124 GB, the case for changing
something is much stronger than if it drops sharply.

2026-08-15 -- THE INCREMENTAL COST IS THE SAME AS THE FIRST PASS. This is the
number the question was waiting on, and it settles it: the cost does not decay.

  run1  21:55:16 -> 22:45:01   49m45s   124.5 GB read   (first full pass)
  run2  00:43:48 -> 01:34:10   50m22s                   (nothing changed since run1)
  run3  02:30:02 -> 03:32:17   62m15s   125.9 GB read   (nightly timer)

All three exit 0, 27 rows attempted, 8 skipped, 0 failed. run3 was longer while
reading marginally more, so the variance is I/O contention rather than work
done.

So a nightly run re-reads ~125 GB locally and re-hashes every source tree
remotely, for an archive of 482 GB apparent / 121 GB allocated, whether or not
anything changed. That is roughly an hour of sustained disk and network activity
every night, paid on vulcan AND on each source: a VPS over the public internet
and a laptop that travels.

Worth noting the two costs are separable. --checksum is what forces the READ;
the transfer itself is already minimal (the archive grew 118G -> 121G across
three runs, nearly all of it real new session data). Dropping --checksum would
not reduce what is copied, only what is hashed.

The trade recorded above is unchanged, and remains John's call:
  keep  -- catches an edit preserving both size and mtime; ~1h/night of I/O on
           four machines
  drop  -- runs become fast size/mtime deltas; risks missing an in-place edit
           that preserves both

Session transcripts are overwhelmingly append-only and an append changes size,
so the practical exposure looks small -- but that is a judgement about the value
of John's data, not a mechanical fact, which is why this stays a question.

The middle option is still available and is probably the best value: keep
--checksum on live roots and use --size-only for ones that cannot change, e.g.
hera/codex-cli-archived. That needs a per-row column the TSV does not currently
have, so it is real work rather than a flag flip.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
RESOLVED by dropping --checksum, per John's decision 2026-08-16. sessions commit 520713d.

MEASURED BEFORE AND AFTER on the real job, not in a test harness.

  before (3 runs)   49m45s / 50m22s / 62m15s, ~125 GB read each
  after  (1 run)     2m30s,                     1.77 GB read

That is ~25x faster and ~71x less disk I/O, and the before-runs had already
established the cost did NOT decay: the third was timer-fired with nothing
changed on any source and still took 62m15s. The saving is paid on vulcan AND on
every source, including a VPS over the public internet and a laptop that travels.

CORRECTNESS PRESERVED, verified rather than assumed:
  - 27 rows gathered, 8 skipped (hosts offline), 0 failed -- identical shape to
    every previous run
  - 27 destination pairs, unchanged
  - the archive GREW: 122,191 -> 122,855 files, 121G -> 124G used, 482G -> 490G
    apparent. So it is still transferring changes, not silently skipping
    everything, which is the failure mode that would make a fast run meaningless

ACCEPTED LIMITATION, pinned in the test suite rather than dropped: a file
rewritten in place with BOTH size and mtime preserved is no longer detected. The
same-stat regression test is INVERTED to assert exactly that, so the limitation
stays provable and re-adding --checksum fails loudly with an explanation.
Session transcripts are overwhelmingly append-only and an append changes size, so
the missed case is narrow.

TWO LATENT TEST BUGS FIXED as a side effect. Both fake rsync stubs addressed
argv POSITIONALLY -- one tested , the other logged "$5"
as the source operand. Removing one flag shifted the operand from $5 to $4, so
the badhost transfer stopped failing and the relative-path assertion compared
against the destination. Neither failure pointed anywhere near the real change.
Both now log full argv and address the source as the penultimate argument.

Full suite passes (exit 0, 'all tests passed').
#+end_example

The mangled line reads \"one tested , the other logged\" --\nit should read:\n\n  Both fake rsync stubs addressed argv POSITIONALLY -- one tested\n  [[ $5 != badhost:* ]] to decide which transfer should fail, and the other\n  logged \"$5\" as the source operand. The fix is\nto pass prose via --description-file / a comment file, which is how this\ncorrection is being written.\n",

:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-kvr
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 12:48]

:FINISHED: [2026-08-12 Wed 17:50]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
The work/positron fileset has NO filters at all: rsync's argv contains no --exclude, --include or --filter, and the deployed fileset yaml declares none. It therefore mirrors andoria's ENTIRE home directory, including machine-regenerable caches.

MEASURED 2026-08-12:
  /tank/Backups/Contracts/Positron/nfs/.cache  =  55 GB
and still growing — the run in progress was writing
  .cache/huggingface/hub/models--google--gemma-4-12B-it/blobs/...
i.e. LLM model weights, which are re-downloadable from HuggingFace at any time.

CONSEQUENCES:
,  * Sync runtime. Runs take hours. The 04:43 run hit the old 50m ceiling; the current run has been
    going 2h43m. TimeoutStartSec had to be raised to 4h partly because of this volume.
,  * Throughput collapse. Early transfer ran at 7-8 MB/s; while pulling model blobs it fell to
    ~950 KB/s, so the tail of every sync is dominated by the least valuable data.
,  * Storage. 55 GB of the 432 GB destination is cache, and it is also captured by every ZFS snapshot
    of tank/Backups, multiplying the cost.

PROPOSED FIX (not applied — this is John's call about what his backup should contain):
add a Filters block to the fileset, e.g.

  Common:
    Filters: |
      - .cache/
      - .cargo/registry/
      - .npm/
      - node_modules/
      - .venv/
      - __pycache__/

pushme passes Filters to rsync via --include-from (Main.hs withFilters), so the syntax is rsync
filter rules. Start with .cache/ alone, which is the measured 55 GB, and add others only with evidence.

NOTE: excluding a path that is already in the destination does NOT delete it — rsync --delete only
removes files that are absent from the source AND not excluded. Excluded paths are protected, so the
existing 55 GB would remain until removed deliberately.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

Fixed and verified. Applied the filter list hera used when it held this mirror, verbatim — 65 rules covering caches (.cache, .npm, .bun, .cabal, .rustup, .cargo, .elan), toolchain state (/.local/share/{cargo,rustup,uv}, /.local/state, /go), build artifacts (target/, dist/, build/, node_modules/, dist-newstyle/, .venv/, .direnv/) and compiler outputs (*.o, *.hi, *.vo, ...). Reproduced rather than reinvented, since guessing at build directories is how a backup quietly loses something that matters.

Because filters PROTECT what they exclude (rsync --delete removes only files absent from the source AND not excluded), the ~150 GiB already copied would have persisted. --delete-excluded was added TEMPORARILY for one run to clear it, then removed the same day; a comment in the module records why it must not become permanent — left in place, any future filter edit becomes an immediate mass deletion.

Before deleting anything, previewed with rsync -n -v driven directly (pushme's own --dry-run is useless here: execute() short-circuits before rsync runs, so it prints 'done' having done nothing). The preview listed 298,613 deletions, every one mapping to an intended rule: .cache 107757, .rustup 50748, .cargo 19049, .codex 17460, .elan 14491, .local 12344, .npm 9659. Checked the three narrow ones — .ssh entries were strictly under sockets/, and the .claude/.config entries were .git/ai/ working logs matched by that rule, not by .claude/worktrees/.

VERIFIED AFTER THE RUN:
  referenced 424G -> 274G (150 GiB reclaimed; used stays high until ZFS snapshots expire)
  every excluded tree gone: .cache .rustup .cargo .codex .elan .npm .bun .cabal
    .local/state .local/share/{uv,rustup} .local/lib go nix-cache .ssh/sockets
    .git-ai .claude/worktrees .direnv .venv
  real data preserved: .ssh/ .config/ .local/ .gitconfig, 85 top-level entries
  deployed fileset parses with no Options key and all 65 filter rules present

Process note: the first deletion pass was killed partway through by my own cleanup command, which matched the running service rather than only the preview processes. Harmless (rsync resumes) but it is why the deletion arrived in two passes.

* DEFER [#D] Three WiFi/RF devices degraded together on 08-19/08-20: side-door lock, Ring doorbell, outdoor plug
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-ly2
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 04:51]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 10:08]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:DEFERRED: [2026-11-18 Wed 09:00]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
Three RF/WiFi devices changed behaviour within the same 08-19/08-20 window. Filed
as one issue because three simultaneous coincidences is a worse hypothesis than
one shared cause, and because the remedy for all three is physical.

1. august-lock-side-door.lan -- ICMP degraded, and WORSENING
     08-13..08-19  98.2-99.7% every day
     08-20         89.5% over 24h, 96.6% over 12h, 93.2% over 6h, 59.2% over 1h
   IoTDeviceChronicallyUnreachable is firing on it (startsAt 2026-08-20T11:44Z).
   This is NOT the same device as nixos-mn4's august-lock-garage-door.

2. ring-doorbell.lan -- ICMP flat zero for ~38 hours
     08-13..08-18  100.0% every day
     then 40.5%, then 0.0%
   Deliberately unalerted, and correctly so: it is host_group="iot-quiet", which
   network.yaml:115-120 pins the down-rules away from on purpose, warning that an
   earlier draft including that group "would have paged on traeger-grill.lan at
   95.5%. Do not widen this selector." I did not widen it.

3. Matter node 26, the outdoor plug (nixos-h2u) -- relapsed the same night after
   ~26 hours of quiet, 75 of 75 CASE failures in the hour I checked.

NOT AN OUTAGE -- checked before escalating, because ping is a poor health proxy
for this device class and the alert's own runbook says so:
,  * lock.side_door reads state=locked, battery 78, operator "Manual Lock", and has
    ZERO unavailable transitions in HA over 7 days.
,  * The august and ring config entries are both state=loaded.
,  * Ring devices reach HA through Ring's cloud API, not the local address being
    pinged, so local ICMP silence does not imply the doorbell is dead.
So all three are RF/reachability degradation, not loss of function. That is why
this is P3 and not urgent.

FOR JOHN -- PHYSICAL, and deliberately not acted on. All three are radio-side:
node 26 is WiFi (FeatureMap=1), and the lock and doorbell are battery WiFi. The
shared-cause candidates are AP/channel/interference changes, or something that
altered RF conditions around 08-19. Worth checking whether anything moved,
changed channel, or was added to the network that night.

TWO THINGS I DID NOT DO, on purpose:
,  * Did not touch the lock or doorbell in any way -- these are security devices.
,  * Did not widen the iot-quiet alert selector, which is documented as
    load-bearing with a worked example of what widening breaks.

INSTRUMENT NOTES, both of which bit me here:
,  * Searching HA entities for 'ring|doorbell' matched "spRINGkler" and
    "deliveRING". Word-boundary the pattern or the noise reads as a hit.
,  * probe_success carries host_group as a LABEL, and ring-doorbell.lan is
    "iot-quiet" while the lock is "iot". A rule scoped to host_group="iot" is
    structurally incapable of firing for the doorbell -- which looks exactly like
    a broken rule until you check the label.
#+end_example

* DONE [#C] microvm@hermes hit the 2m30s stop timeout on both of today's restarts
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-m90
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 16:44]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-30 Sun 22:29]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-30 Sun 22:29]
:CLOSE_REASON: Fixed and verified 2026-08-30 (commit 3a3da5591). Root cause: microvm.nix sends a QMP input-send-event (Ctrl-Alt-Del) to stop the VM, but emits -device i8042 only for x86_64-linux; the aarch64 branch adds no input device, so QEMU rejected the event ('Input handler not found for event type key') and the guest never learned to stop. Replaced the instance's ExecStop with a QMP system_powerdown, which on -M virt with direct-kernel boot and no firmware is delivered over a PL061 GPIO into /gpio-keys/poweroff (KEY_POWER) -- all built into the guest kernel, handled by logind's default HandlePowerKey=poweroff. Measured: stop went from 150s (SIGKILL at TimeoutSec) to 3.0s, Result=success, and the guest journal reaches 'System Power Off' rather than being killed. The empty first ExecStop element is required: microvm@ and microvm@hermes are different units, so mkForce does nothing and systemd accumulates ExecStop= across drop-ins -- the first attempt ran both scripts and still took 2m30s.
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

OBSERVATION, not yet a confirmed defect. Recorded so it is not rediscovered from scratch.

microvm@hermes.service logged "Failed with result 'timeout'" on stop during BOTH restarts on 2026-08-26 (10:35:08 and 12:45:08), each time hitting TimeoutStopUSec=2min 30s before the VM was killed and restarted. It recovered immediately on both occasions -- active/running within ~14s of the failure line -- so nothing was left broken.

RATE: 2 of the 6 VM starts in the preceding 7 days timed out, and both are today's. The four earlier restarts did not.

LIKELY BENIGN EXPLANATION, untested: both timeouts were stopping a VM whose upstream model was returning HTTP 404 (see nixos-ots and the models.nix fix in b968ad654). An agent wedged on a dead upstream plausibly does not respond to SIGTERM promptly. If that is the cause, the next restart -- the first with a working upstream -- should stop cleanly.

WHY IT IS WORTH TRACKING ANYWAY: the cost is real and lands on every rebuild. A switch that touches the Hermes units waits the full 2m30s for the VM to stop, which is most of the switch's wall-clock time.

TO CONFIRM OR CLEAR: on the next microvm@hermes restart, check whether the stop completes cleanly:
  journalctl -u microvm@hermes.service | grep -E "stop-sigterm|Failed with result"
Two consecutive clean stops with a healthy upstream closes this. A timeout with a healthy upstream means the shutdown path itself is slow and TimeoutStopUSec or the guest's shutdown handling deserves a look.

  {
    "id": 52,
    "issue_id": "nixos-m90",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "HYPOTHESIS REFUTED 2026-08-27 by direct test. This is a real shutdown-path defect, not fallout from the dead upstream.\n\nI predicted the two 08-26 timeouts were benign, caused by stopping a VM whose upstream model was returning HTTP 404, and that a restart with a healthy upstream would stop cleanly. So I ran that test.\n\nMETHOD: confirmed the upstream first -- POST to the gateway with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731-MXFP4-MLX returned HTTP 200 -- then 'systemctl restart microvm@hermes.service'.\n\nRESULT: it timed out again, identically.\n  15:44:53  Stopping MicroVM 'hermes'...\n  15:47:23  microvm@hermes.service: Stopping timed out. Terminating.\n  15:47:24  microvm@hermes.service: Failed with result 'timeout'.\n  15:47:24  Stopped MicroVM 'hermes'.\nExactly TimeoutStopUSec=2min 30s, with a healthy upstream. That kills the benign explanation: the guest does not respond to shutdown within 2.5 minutes regardless of upstream state, and systemd has to terminate it.\n\nSO THE RATE IS NOW 3 OF 3 tested restarts, not 2 of 6 -- the four earlier clean starts in the 7-day window were boots or starts, not stops.\n\nTWO CONSEQUENCES, both observed live:\n 1. Every switch that touches the Hermes units pays a flat 2m30s, which is most of that switch's wall clock.\n 2. The unit transits through ActiveState=failed / Result=timeout before restarting. systemd DID complete the restart on its own (VM active again at 15:47:30, six seconds after the stop finished), but any observer sampling in that window sees a failed unit -- and a switch would clear it, which is exactly the pattern that hides this.\n\nNOT INVESTIGATED FURTHER YET. Likely candidates, in order: the guest's own systemd shutdown running long (a service inside with a large TimeoutStopSec), microvm.nix's shutdown mechanism not delivering ACPI powerdown, or the qemu process ignoring SIGTERM. The next step is to watch the GUEST's shutdown, not the host's -- host-side logs only show that nothing happened for 150s.\n\nCOST OF THIS TEST: Hermes was down about 2.5 minutes. Verified fully healthy afterwards -- api_server_ok=1, discord heartbeat 1.8s old, MCP SSE ok, Qdrant reachable, 0 stuck incidents.\n\nRaised P3 -> P2: confirmed reproducible, with a measurable cost on every rebuild.",
    "created_at": "2026-08-27T22:49:55Z"
  },
  {
    "id": 56,
    "issue_id": "nixos-m90",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "ROOT CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-30 (host-side; the guest-side investigation this issue\nwas waiting on is not needed).\n\nThe VM never stops gracefully because the shutdown signal never reaches it.\n\n/var/lib/microvms/hermes/booted/bin/microvm-shutdown sends a QMP *key event*\nsimulating Ctrl-Alt-Del:\n  {\"arguments\":{\"events\":[{\"data\":{\"down\":true,\"key\":{\"data\":\"ctrl\",\"type\":\"qcode\"}},\"type\":\"key\"}...\n\nQEMU rejects it. Captured live during the 20:19 stop:\n  {\"error\": {\"class\": \"GenericError\", \"desc\": \"Input handler not found for event type key\"}}\n\nThe guest has no input device, so there is no handler for key events. The\nkeypress is discarded, the guest never initiates shutdown, and the script's\ntail -- a loop that polls the QMP socket waiting for QEMU to exit -- blocks\nforever. systemd then hits TimeoutStopUSec=2min 30s and SIGKILLs.\n\nDETERMINISTIC, NOT INTERMITTENT. Every stop attempt on 2026-08-30 timed out at\nexactly 2m30s: 10:03:22->10:05:52, 10:11:38->10:14:08, 10:15:56->10:18:26,\n20:19:54->20:22:24. Four of four. The original title says 'both of today's\nrestarts'; it is in fact every restart, always, and the 2m30s is the timeout\nfiring rather than anything the guest does.\n\nCONSEQUENCE: every switch that touches hermes costs 2m30s of wall clock and ends\nin SIGKILL, so the guest never flushes cleanly. The 2026-08-30 20:19 occurrence\ncame from a nixos-rebuild run directly rather than through ./build (no build.log\nentries in that hour), which is also why it is not visible in the build log.\n\nFIX OPTIONS (a real choice, not mechanical -- operator's call):\n  1. Give the guest an input device so the Ctrl-Alt-Del actually lands. Smallest\n     change, keeps upstream's shutdown path intact.\n  2. Replace the shutdown command with QMP system_powerdown (ACPI). Cleaner in\n     principle, but needs ACPI handling in the guest, which a microVM may not have.\n  3. Lower TimeoutStopSec and accept SIGKILL as the shutdown mechanism. Removes\n     the 2m30s stall but keeps the unclean stop -- masks rather than fixes.\n\nThe shutdown script is generated by microvm.nix upstream, so option 1 or 2 means\neither configuring the guest or overriding the generated script.",
    "created_at": "2026-08-31T03:45:14Z"
  }
]

* DONE [#E] Explain GitWorkspaceMetricCollectionSlow's noon firing: two collectors share one metric and one file
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-mfh
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: docs
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 12:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 12:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-21 Fri 12:48]
:CLOSE_REASON: Explained and documented in the alert's own comment. The noon clustering is git-workspace-metrics-slow's 12:00 run (the other spike is its 00:45 run); fast runs every 15 min at ~0s and both write the same metric into the same textfile. Not retuned: it is severity info, fires for a real event, and a failed slow run is already covered by GitWorkspaceMetricsCollectionFailed. Verified comment-only — expr still '> 180', for 600s, health=ok.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Resolves the "unexplained noon clustering" left open in nixos-bkf. It is not a
fault: two collectors share one metric name and one output file.

  git-workspace-metrics-fast   OnUnitActiveUSec=15min -> 96 runs/day, duration ~0s
  git-workspace-metrics-slow   OnCalendar 00:45 and 12:00 -> 2 runs/day, up to 572s

Both write git_workspace_scrape_duration_seconds into the SAME textfile
(git_workspace.prom), each ending in an atomic mv that replaces the other's output.
So the gauge reads ~0 almost always and spikes twice daily when the thorough pass
runs, which is exactly the distribution measured over 30d: median 0.0s, p99 2.0s,
max 572s, ~334 one-minute samples above the 180s threshold, clustering at 12:00
(28 samples) and 00:00-01:00 (4). My earlier hypothesis -- contention with the
nightly ~600-repo git-workspace-archive sweep -- was wrong and is now properly
explained.

THE SAME SHARED FILE HAS A SECOND CONSEQUENCE, and this one is already known and
handled: the slow collector's per-repo metrics survive only until the next fast run
15 minutes later. Measured: git_workspace_repo_age_seconds is present 113 of 10,080
minutes over 7 days (1.1%) and is absent right now. git-workspace-alerts.nix:120-125
documents this and GitWorkspaceRepoStale reads
max_over_time(git_workspace_repo_age_seconds[24h]) rather than an instantaneous
value. VERIFIED that workaround still works: instantaneous count is absent, the 24h
window recovers 50 series, and the threshold comparison returns 0 (no repo is more
than 14 days stale). That rule is NOT dead.

NOT RETUNED, deliberately. GitWorkspaceMetricCollectionSlow is severity: info, it
fires for a real and explainable event, and the obvious fix -- labelling the metric
by collector -- is awkward precisely because the two share one file, so only one
collector's series exists at any instant. A genuinely FAILED slow run is already
covered by GitWorkspaceMetricsCollectionFailed, since both collectors emit
git_workspace_scrape_success. Changing behaviour here would trade a small,
explainable info alert for a more fragile rule.

WHAT WAS DONE: documented it in the alert's own comment, including the measured
distribution and the twice-daily schedule, so the next reader does not spend a
cycle rediscovering it. I spent parts of two cycles on this; the comment previously
said only "Alert if high scrape duration (collection taking too long)".

VERIFIED comment-only: after the switch the rule still reads
expr git_workspace_scrape_duration_seconds > 180, for 600s, health=ok,
state=inactive, and the sibling rule still recovers its 50 series.

:PROPERTIES:

:LABELS:   []

:CREATED:  [2026-08-16 Sun 08:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-16 Sun 10:59]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-16 Sun 10:59]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

august-lock-garage-door.lan sits at 74.8% ICMP availability over 24h and 78.1%
over 7d, while its two siblings are at 99.9% (front door) and 98.9% (side door).
BlackboxICMPIoTDeviceDown has NEVER fired for it in 14 days.

That is not a rule defect so much as a shape the rule cannot see. Its clause is
`probe_success{host_group="iot"} == 0` with for=3600s, so it needs a CONTINUOUS
hour down. This lock flaps in bursts of minutes -- currently down 9 minutes, then
back -- so it never accumulates an unbroken hour. The rule catches DEATH, not
DEGRADATION.

THE DEVICE IS FINE, checked with a better instrument than ping: Home Assistant
reports lock.garage as 'locked' with ZERO 'unavailable' rows in 24h. Same story
as the three nest-*.lan entries, which live in an `iot-noping` group precisely
because they do not answer ICMP while working perfectly. Ping is a poor health
proxy for this class of device.

RECORDED RATHER THAN FIXED, and deliberately so. I have now investigated this
twice across two cycles and reached "no action" both times; the point of the
issue is to stop a third. Any fix is a judgement call about alert design that is
John's, not mine:

  a) leave it -- the device works, the noise is zero (nothing fires), and IoT ping
     flakiness is normal on this network (the iot group shows jitter to 4.2s)
  b) add an availability rule, e.g. avg_over_time(probe_success[6h]) < 0.9, which
     would catch chronic degradation the current rule structurally cannot -- at
     the cost of firing on devices that are working
  c) move this lock into iot-noping alongside the Nests, conceding that ICMP is
     not a useful signal for it

(a) is the status quo and defensible. I lean (c) if anything, since it makes the
existing classification honest, but it hides a real signal if the lock ever does
die. Not acting.

** Close Reason

IMPLEMENTED, per John's choice of the availability-rule approach (option b).

New rule IoTDeviceChronicallyUnreachable in modules/monitoring/alerts/network.yaml,
sitting beside BlackboxICMPIoTDeviceDown in the network_connectivity group:

  avg_over_time(probe_success{job="blackbox_icmp",host_group="iot"}[24h]) < 0.9
  for: 30m, severity: warning

THE HOLE IT CLOSES, confirmed by reading both existing rules rather than assuming:
  BlackboxICMPIoTDeviceDown  probe_success == 0, for 1h  -> catches DEATH
  HighPacketLoss             rate < 0.95, for 8h, but with
                             host_group!~"iot|iot-noping|iot-quiet"  -> excludes ALL IoT
So a device down a quarter of the time in bursts of MINUTES was invisible to both:
it never accumulates an unbroken hour, and it is not eligible for the packet-loss
rule. That is exactly august-lock-garage-door, which sat at 74.8%/24h while
BlackboxICMPIoTDeviceDown fired for it zero times in 14 days.

TWO THINGS I GOT WRONG AND CORRECTED BEFORE COMMITTING:

1. Scope. My first draft used host_group=~"iot|iot-quiet". The comments in that
   file state TWICE that iot-quiet devices (grill, doorbell) are powered off or
   deep-asleep for long routine stretches and MUST NEVER ALERT. That draft would
   have paged on traeger-grill.lan at 95.5%. Scoped to host_group="iot" only,
   matching the sibling rule exactly. Reading the surrounding comments caught
   this, not the metrics.

2. Window. I sized 6h/0.9 on the CURRENT instant, where it returns exactly one
   series with the next worst 14 points away -- which reads as a perfectly
   isolated rule. A 30-day replay said otherwise: 7 devices, 669 hours. Comparing
   properly:
       6h /0.9  -> 7 devices, 669h      24h/0.9 -> 5 devices, 631h
       6h /0.8  -> 5 devices, 245h      24h/0.8 -> 3 devices, 163h
   Chose 24h/0.9 because it separates CHRONIC from TRANSIENT rather than merely
   being quieter. Moving 6h -> 24h at the same threshold drops the purely
   transient trippers to zero (ring-chime-kitchen 13 -> 0, flume-water-meter
   5 -> 0), leaves the genuinely chronic case untouched (garage 490 -> 497), and
   INCREASES two devices whose degradation is actually sustained (GW1200B
   23 -> 41, hubspace-porch-light 8 -> 22). Lowering the threshold to 0.8 instead
   would have been the cruder knob -- it just hides everything but the worst.

   The replay itself needed a correction: my first query used
   `count by (instance) (count_over_time(...))`, which counts SERIES not samples
   and returned a suspicious "1" for every device. Reading the value directly
   gave the real distribution.

VERIFIED: promtool 32 rules SUCCESS; Prometheus loaded 486 rules (was 485); the
rule is health=ok and correctly pending on august-lock-garage-door at 76.4%/24h,
the only device currently under threshold.

EXPECT IT TO FIRE ALMOST CONTINUOUSLY for that lock -- 497 of the last 720 hours
qualify. That is correct for a chronic-degradation rule and is why this issue
existed, but it is documented in the rule as a caution: if this ever becomes a
permanent fixture for several devices at once it has stopped being a control.

The underlying device question is unchanged and still John's: the lock WORKS (HA
reports it locked with zero unavailable entries), so the options remain fixing its
radio/position or reclassifying it as iot-noping. The rule makes the decay
visible; it does not decide that.

* DONE [#E] Baseline: matter-server emits ~4000 error-priority log lines per day
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-mqk
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-17 Mon 16:50]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 08:47]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-19 Wed 23:56]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Recording a measured baseline so a future cycle can tell a real regression from
normal noise. No failure found; nothing is broken today.

matter-server emits roughly 4,000 journal lines at priority<=3 (err) EVERY DAY,
and has for the whole retained window:

    Aug 12 : 3937      Aug 15 : 4788
    Aug 13 : 4024      Aug 16 : 4182
    Aug 14 : 4376      Aug 17 : 2034 (partial day at time of measurement)

That makes matter-server the single largest source of error-priority log volume
on the host -- during the 2026-08-17 cycle it was 143 lines in 4 hours, which
looked alarming at first glance and is in fact BELOW its own daily baseline.

WHAT THE LINES ARE. Predominantly CHIP-native retries, e.g.

    CHIP_ERROR [chip.native.SC] Received error (protocol code 4) during pairing
    process: src/protocols/secure_channel/...

These carry no node label, so they cannot be attributed to a specific device
from the journal alone. Pairing-error lines specifically run 5-70/day with no
trend (Aug 13: 18, Aug 14: 20, Aug 15: 26, Aug 16: 5, Aug 17: 70).

WHY IT IS FILED AT P4 RATHER THAN IGNORED. Volume this size is a masking
hazard: a genuinely new matter fault has to compete with ~4,000 daily lines of
upstream chatter, and the only reason node 17's Aug 16 episode (nixos-rsn) was
findable at all is that it carries an explicit <Node:17> label. It is NOT
evidence of a fault by itself -- both matter-server and home-assistant are
active, and node 17 logged zero CASE failures on Aug 17.

POSSIBLE ACTION, not taken: lowering CHIP log verbosity for the native layer.
Deliberately left alone because turning down error-level logging on the Matter
fabric could hide the next real fault, and the Aqara Hub M3 being the sole
Thread border router already makes this fabric a single point of failure worth
over-observing rather than under-observing.

** Close Reason

Baseline established, re-measured a third time, and -- more usefully -- ATTRIBUTED.
Closing because the deliverable was the baseline, and the number this issue is
named for is no longer current or independent.

CORRECTED SERIES (calendar days, local):
  08-14  3485    08-17  2351
  08-15  4787    08-18   613
  08-16  4181    08-19   799
Sums to 16222, matching the independent 7d total exactly.

These do not match the earlier comment's day -1/-2 figures (1388/1428) because that
pass used rolling 24h windows and this one uses calendar days. Different instrument,
not a contradiction -- worth recording so the next reader does not treat it as one.

THE REAL FINDING: this was never an independent issue. Breaking the lines down by
peer node id shows node 0x1A = 26 = the outdoor plug is the dominant driver, and
total volume tracks its collapse almost exactly:

  date     node17  node26  node0   total
  08-14        14    1577    546    3485
  08-15         7    2231    760    4787
  08-16        30    1872    664    4181
  08-17         0     782    291    2351
  08-18         3       0      6     613
  08-19        51       9     73     799

So "matter-server emits ~4000 error lines/day" was, in substance, a measurement of
the outdoor plug flapping -- i.e. of nixos-h2u seen from the log-volume side. The
~83% decline is node 26 going quiet, not a general improvement in matter-server.

NOT A DEVICE LOSS, checked because a quiet log is exactly what a dead device looks
like: both nodes are alive in HA's recorder. switch.smart_outdoor_plug_switch_1/2
wrote state at 23:01 today, and the door sensors at 19:26 (the HA restart). Neither
reads unavailable.

INSTRUMENT NOTE, and I walked into it before catching it: node references are
`peer <000000000000001A` with NO closing bracket. My first pattern required one and
returned a confident zero for EVERY node on every day -- including days that
demonstrably had thousands of hits. Positive-control the pattern against a known-busy
day before believing any zero here. This is the same trap recorded on nixos-rsn,
which I re-hit despite having written the warning.

Remaining volume is dominated by "Subscription Liveness timeout" (530 of 799 today),
a different class from the CASE failures -- if anyone wants to reduce this further,
that is where the mass is, not CASE.

Not investigated further because this is a\nP4 baseline task and the trend is in the right direction; re-measure before acting.\n",
