* DONE [#E] openproject container logs successful /metrics scrapes at error priority (~1440/day)
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-t2q
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: chore
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 23:27]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-13 Thu 23:02]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-13 Thu 23:02]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
`journalctl -p 3` attributes ~1440 error-priority lines/day to user@928.service
(the rootless `openproject` container, uid 928). Every one of them is a
SUCCESSFUL Prometheus scrape:

    <ip> - - [../Aug/..] "GET /metrics HTTP/1.1" 200 - 0.0

240 lines per 4h = exactly one per minute = the scrape interval. All 240 in the
sampled window were HTTP 200, and the `openproject` Prometheus target reports
health=up with no lastError.

Cause: conmon maps a container's stderr to syslog priority 3. OpenProject writes
its HTTP access log to stderr, so ordinary access logging is indexed as "error".

Impact is triage noise, not a malfunction:
  - `journalctl -p 3` is useless for this unit -- real openproject errors are
    buried under a 1/min heartbeat.
  - A host-wide "what failed recently" sweep ranks openproject #2 behind
    matter-server, which is misleading. This cost one full investigation during
    the 2026-08-12 23:30 health cycle before it was shown benign; without this
    issue on record, every future cycle repeats that.
  - It inflates Loki ingest by ~1440 lines/day of zero information.

Verified NOT to corrupt alerting: the only broad rule is
SystemdJournalHighErrorRate (systemd-errors.yaml), which matches
`level=error|ERROR|failed`. The access-log line contains none of those tokens,
so it does not feed that rule. No Loki or Prometheus rule mentions openproject.

Possible fixes, none urgent:
  (a) point OpenProject's access log at stdout (priority 6) instead of stderr --
      correct at the source, needs a container env/config change;
  (b) drop `"GET /metrics` lines for this unit in the promtail pipeline --
      cheap, but hides a real 5xx on /metrics too;
  (c) leave it, now that it is documented as benign.

Prefer (a) if OpenProject exposes the knob; otherwise (c) is defensible.
#+end_example

** Notes

#+begin_example
2026-08-13 -- investigated. The noise is real but the recorded mitigation was NOT
working, which is the more important finding.

`modules/users/home-manager/openproject.nix` carried:

    # Filter out info-level HTTP access logs to reduce log volume
    # Saves ~4,200 lines/day by only logging warnings and above
    LogLevelMax = "warning";

Measured over a 4h window with that setting live and confirmed effective on the
unit (`systemctl --user show -p LogLevelMax` returned 4):

    prio 3 err      240
    prio 5 notice    15
    prio 6 info    1334

Nothing was being filtered -- ~8k info lines/day were flowing through a setting
documented as suppressing them.

MECHANISM: LogLevelMax caps messages systemd relays from the stream it manages.
podman's default journald log driver has conmon write to journald DIRECTLY; the
records carry _COMM=conmon and SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER=openproject and never traverse
the unit's StandardOutput, so the cap has nothing to act on. Verified by
grouping the info-priority records by _COMM.

This also explains why the earlier 30s->60s scrape-interval change (documented
in modules/containers/openproject-quadlet.nix) was the only mitigation that
actually reduced anything: it cut the requests at the source rather than trying
to filter the output.

ACTION TAKEN: removed the dead LogLevelMax and replaced it with the measurement
above plus the two approaches that would genuinely work. A setting that looks
like a working mitigation is worse than no setting -- it stops the next person
from looking. Verified after deploy: LogLevelMax reads -1 (unset), openproject
restarted cleanly (ActiveEnter 00:51:23, NRestarts 0), Prometheus target up,
https://openproject.vulcan.lan/ returns 302.

STILL OPEN -- the volume itself is unchanged (~1440 err + ~5760 info lines/day).
Two real options, both deliberate observability trade-offs, so neither applied
on spec:
  (a) LogDriver=passthrough on the quadlet -- routes container output back
      through the unit's stdout/stderr and so back under LogLevelMax, at the
      cost of `podman logs` no longer working;
  (b) a journald/promtail drop rule keyed on the access-line shape -- cheap, but
      it would hide a genuine 5xx on /metrics too.
Needs a call on which trade is acceptable before doing either.

2026-08-14 -- traced the actual source, which corrects two guesses in the
original description.

NOT Apache. The container runs apache2, but its access.log is 0 bytes and the
only active Apache file is other_vhosts_access.log, a real file inside the
container that never reaches journald. Apache is a red herring.

The line comes from the Ruby process serving the metrics port. Container port
9394 is published to 127.0.0.1:9394 (the Prometheus target); it answers HTTP 200
with ~88 KB of metrics. The log format is Rack::CommonLogger exactly:
    <ip> - - [14/Aug/2026:05:54:58 +0000] "GET /metrics HTTP/1.1" 200 - 0.0016
i.e. host, user, time, request, status, length ("-" when zero), duration. So
OpenProject's prometheus_exporter web server logs every scrape through Rack and
out the container's stderr. There is no Apache directive to change.

WHY NO CLEAN FIX EXISTS -- the priority is decided by the STREAM, never by the
content:
  - Today, with podman's journald log driver, conmon writes container stderr to
    journald as priority 3 (err) and stdout as priority 6 (info). Routine access
    logs land at err purely because Rack writes them to stderr.
  - With LogDriver=passthrough the streams instead go through the unit, and
    systemd assigns ONE priority to both via SyslogLevel= (default info). The
    access noise would leave `journalctl -p 3` -- but so would every genuine
    OpenProject error, because systemd does not mark stderr as err. And pairing
    that with LogLevelMax=warning, as this module used to attempt, would then
    DISCARD real errors outright.
So passthrough does not separate signal from noise, it just relabels everything.
Either way `journalctl -p 3` for this unit is uninformative: today it is all
noise, afterwards it would be all silence.

Only CONTENT-based filtering can actually separate them, and that lives outside
journald:
  (b) a promtail/Loki drop rule keyed on the access-line shape. IMPORTANT
      CORRECTION to the original description: this fixes Loki ingest volume
      ONLY. It does not touch what journald stores, so `journalctl -p 3` stays
      exactly as noisy. It also hides a genuine 5xx on /metrics.
  (a) is not available as described -- there is no OpenProject knob to move the
      exporter's Rack log to stdout, and the Apache route does not apply.

REVISED RECOMMENDATION: (c), leave it, is now the strongest option rather than
merely defensible. The noise is documented, it corrupts no alert (verified: the
only broad rule, SystemdJournalHighErrorRate, matches level=error|ERROR|failed
and the access line contains none of those), and every alternative trades real
error visibility for tidiness. The 30s -> 60s scrape interval already halved it
at the source and 60s is the floor set by OpenProjectNoWorkers' for: 2m.

The one thing worth keeping is what was already done: the dead LogLevelMax is
gone, so nobody is misled into thinking this is handled.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
EXPECTED BEHAVIOUR -- closed by John's decision, 2026-08-14. Not fixed, and deliberately so.

The ~1440 error-priority lines/day are successful Prometheus scrapes logged by
Rack::CommonLogger from the Ruby process serving the metrics port (container
9394 -> 127.0.0.1:9394, HTTP 200, ~88KB). They land at priority 3 only because
Rack writes to stderr and podman's journald driver has conmon tag stderr as err.

There is no clean fix, because the priority is decided by the STREAM and never
by the content:
  - conmon maps stderr -> err(3), stdout -> info(6);
  - LogDriver=passthrough would instead let systemd assign ONE priority to both
    (SyslogLevel=, default info), moving the noise out of -p 3 along with every
    genuine OpenProject error -- and combined with LogLevelMax=warning it would
    discard real errors outright;
  - a promtail/Loki drop rule fixes ingest volume ONLY, leaves journalctl
    untouched, and would hide a real 5xx on /metrics;
  - there is no OpenProject knob to move the exporter's Rack log to stdout, and
    Apache is not involved (its access.log is 0 bytes).
Every alternative trades real error visibility for tidiness.

Verified harmless before closing: no alert is corrupted. The only broad rule is
SystemdJournalHighErrorRate (systemd-errors.yaml), which matches
level=error|ERROR|failed; the access line contains none of those tokens. No Loki
or Prometheus rule mentions openproject. The Prometheus target is healthy.

Already mitigated as far as is sensible: the scrape interval was cut 30s -> 60s,
halving the volume at the source, and 60s is the FLOOR -- OpenProjectNoWorkers
uses for: 2m and needs two samples inside that window.

The one real defect found along the way IS fixed (commit b1e3b3f72): the module
carried LogLevelMax = "warning" documented as saving ~4,200 lines/day, which
filtered nothing -- 1334 info and 15 notice lines were flowing straight past it,
because conmon writes to journald directly and never traverses the unit's
StandardOutput. That dead setting is gone and replaced with the measurement, so
nobody is misled into thinking this is handled.

Standing note for future health cycles: openproject ranking high in a
'journalctl -p 3' sweep is EXPECTED and benign. Do not re-investigate it.
#+end_example

* TODO [#B] Running PostgreSQL is unstable 17.11/glibc-2.42; config specifies stable 17.10/glibc-2.40
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-t5w
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 05:31]
:MODIFIED: [2026-09-01 Tue 08:45]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Left over from the nixos-7bp userPkgs routing regression. The routing is fixed
and the CONFIG is correct, but the running server was never restarted onto it.

  running process : /nix/store/71vrayh2...-postgresql-17.11/bin/postgres
                    maps glibc-2.42-84, started 2026-08-31 20:58:10
  live unit says  : postgresql-and-plugins-17.10  (stable, glibc 2.40)
  restartIfChanged: true, stopIfChanged: true, NeedDaemonReload: no

None of the three switches on 2026-09-01 restarted postgresql -- it never
appeared in any switch's stop/start list despite the unit's ExecStart changing
from 17.11 to 17.10. Mechanism not yet established.

CONSEQUENCE: 23 of 23 non-template databases report a collation version
mismatch, recorded 2.40 vs actual 2.42, and pg_dump logs a WARNING + HINT for
each on every nightly backup:
  SRP budgetboard db flume-data gitea grist hass immich mailarchiver metabase
  monica nextcloud nodered_events open_webui openproject org paperless postgres
  ragflow rspamd speedtest_tracker vanna wallabag

Indexes were built under glibc 2.40 collation; the server has been serving and
writing under 2.42 since 2026-08-31 20:58 (~8h). PostgreSQL derives the glibc
collation version from the library version string, not from actual locale data,
so a mismatch flags a POSSIBLE ordering change rather than proving one -- no
known locale-data overhaul landed between 2.40 and 2.42 (the historically
disruptive one was 2.28). Risk is therefore low but not zero for text indexes.

RESOLUTION -- restart postgresql so it runs the configured stable 17.10/glibc
2.40 binary. That makes recorded and actual agree again at 2.40 with no REINDEX,
because it returns the server to the collation the indexes were built under.

DO NOT run ALTER DATABASE ... REFRESH COLLATION VERSION. That rubber-stamps 2.42
as authoritative while leaving indexes built under 2.40 marked valid, converting
a visible warning into a silent inconsistency.

The restart is also a PostgreSQL minor DOWNGRADE 17.11 -> 17.10. Safe on-disk
(minor releases never change catalog or page format), but it is a downgrade.

NEEDS OPERATOR DECISION: a postgresql restart takes down every DB-backed service
-- hass, gitea, immich, grist, wallabag, nextcloud, openproject, mailarchiver,
metabase, paperless, open-webui and more. Not taken unilaterally. Until then the
system is functioning normally; this is a consistency issue, not an outage.

EVIDENCE: Daily row counts in nodered_events.public.msg_events_2026_08: Aug 1-3 at 10,205-14,156/day, then Aug 4 = 623,133, Aug 5 = 1,706,987, Aug 6 = 2,081,824, Aug 7 = 2,082,095, Aug 8 = 2,081,618, Aug 9 = 1,753,315, Aug 10 = 1,569,849, then Aug 11 = 15,659 and back to a 13,301-17,620/day baseline through Aug 20. The partition now holds 12,076,366 rows at 3963 MB versus msg_events_2026_07 at 501,326 rows / 163 MB for a full month. Top contributors on a burst day (Aug 6) were a tight repeat loop at ~4 events/sec: chronos-repeat 'repeat', api-current-state 'Check State' and api-call-service 'Perform Action' at 345,260 events each, plus function 'mirror notify → persistent' and server-events call_service at 345,341 each. This inflates the nightly pg_dump — /tank/Backups/PostgreSQL/db/nodered_events is 3.8G apparent, second only to mailarchiver — and it went unnoticed because the only size alert is pg_database_size_bytes > 20GB (alerts/database.yaml:277) and the database sits at 4204 MB. The burst stopped on its own; nothing in the config records a fix, so the loop's stop semantics remain unconfirmed. Space will not be reclaimed until the partition ages out of the 'drop >30d' rotation (node-red-event-logger-rotate.timer, last ran successfully today 03:30).

COMMAND: sudo -u postgres psql -qAtX -d nodered_events -c "select to_char(ts,'YYYY-MM-DD'), count(*) from public.msg_events_2026_08 group by 1 order by 1;"; sudo -u postgres psql -qAtX -d nodered_events -c "select hook, node_type, node_name, count(*) from public.msg_events_2026_08 where ts >= '2026-08-06' and ts < '2026-08-07' group by 1,2,3 order by 4 desc limit 8;"

VERIFIER: Independently reproduced. (1) INSTRUMENT: direct SQL against public.msg_events_2026_08, not journald/Prometheus/regex, so none of this host's documented instrument traps apply. My re-run returned byte-identical daily counts (Aug 4=623,133; Aug 5=1,706,987; Aug 6=2,081,824; Aug 7=2,082,095; Aug 8=2,081,618; Aug 9=1,753,315; Aug 10=1,569,849; Aug 11=15,659; then 13,301-17,620/day through Aug 20). Contributor breakdown reproduced exactly (chronos-repeat 'repeat', api-current-state 'Check State', api-call-service 'Perform Action' at 345,260 each; server-events call_service and function 'mirror notify -> persistent' at 345,341). Sizes verified: msg_events_2026_08 = 3963 MB total relation / 12,079,884 rows (slightly above the reported 12,076,366 because baseline inserts continue); msg_events_2026_07 = 501,326 rows / 163 MB; nodered_events DB = 4204 MB. Positive controls: the July partition returns a sane small nonzero for a comparable full-month window, and the error-count probe returns 53 for August (nonzero), so a zero from it would have been meaningful.

(2) NOT ALREADY KNOWN. I traced the loop to node ids <tab>-10650db548d5da66 across tabs 46c722b4b729c4c4, baaed203968dfd8d, 93dd1999f70f45c5, 2a858d6ad567a73d - the four 'Act until observed' subflow instances driving the outdoor/patio plug switches. That made 'this is really the deferred MatterNodeSessionFailures on node 26 (outdoor plug)' the strongest refutation, so I tested it: over Aug 4-10 those nodes logged 40 errors out o

** Close Reason

Closing the monitoring half, which is what was actionable. The runaway itself ended
on its own on 2026-08-10 and the data is aging out; what mattered was that seven
days of 130x-normal writes produced NO alert at all.

WHY NOTHING FIRED: the only size rule is PostgreSQLDatabaseLarge at 20 GiB, and
nodered_events peaked at 4.2 GB. Size answers "is it too big"; it cannot answer "did
something just change". A 130x rate anomaly never came near an absolute threshold.

FIXED: added PostgreSQLDatabaseGrowthSpike --
  deriv(pg_database_size_bytes[6h]) * 86400 / 1024 / 1024 > 100, for: 2h

THRESHOLD SIZED BY REPLAY over 30 days of retained data, not guessed:
  nodered_events quiet baseline   +4..+5 MB/day, flat for 3 weeks either side
  nodered_events during 08-04..10 +205/+445/+681/+682/+681/+682/+453 MB/day
  busiest DB on the host, quiet   mailarchiver 7.6 MB/day, then nodered 5.4,
                                  hass 1.5, everything else <=0.7
So 100 MB/day sits ~13x above the busiest legitimate baseline and ~6.8x below the
observed fault. That separation is why one generic rule works instead of a
per-database threshold.

deriv() not increase(): pg_database_size_bytes is a gauge that also DROPS -- it fell
134 MB on 2026-08-01 when a monthly partition aged out -- and deriv fits a slope
rather than assuming monotonicity. A partition drop gives a negative slope and
cannot trip a > threshold.

VERIFIED BY REPLAYING THE RULE AGAINST REAL HISTORY, which is stronger than "the
rule loads":
  2026-08-02 12:00  0 series firing   (before the burst)
  2026-08-06 12:00  1 series firing   nodered_events
  2026-08-08 12:00  1 series firing   nodered_events
  2026-08-11 12:00  0 series firing   (after it stopped)
  2026-08-16 12:00  0 series firing   (quiet baseline)
True positive across the fault window, true negative either side.

ALSO VERIFIED LIVE after switch: rule health=ok with empty lastError in group
database_alerts (30s interval), state=inactive, and the underlying metric has 30d of
history for 32 databases so it is not a dead rule. It first read health=unknown with
an empty lastError, which is this host's documented post-restart warmup signature; I
polled until it settled to ok rather than reporting the unknown as fine.

NOT DONE, and deliberately left: the CAUSE is not fixed. The burst came from a
chronos-repeat loop ('repeat' -> 'Check State' -> 'Perform Action' at ~4 events/sec,
345,260 events each on 08-06) whose stop semantics were never confirmed, and nothing
in the config records why it stopped. That is a Node-RED flow change, so it is
John's call, and the alert now catches a recurrence within two hours instead of
seven days. Space will not return until the partition ages out of the >30d rotation.

* DONE [#D] nix-config mirror race: resolved by John (gitea sole writer), pending clean-window confirmation
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-tvl
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 08:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-13 Thu 20:45]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-13 Thu 20:45]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

GiteaPushMirrorFailing fires on johnw/nix-config -> github.com/jwiegley/nix-config
while the two repositories are, in fact, byte-identical.

State at 2026-08-13 08:50:
  gitea  johnw/nix-config  main = e0afc16bd31d
  github jwiegley/nix-config main = e0afc16bd31d
  github's tip is present in gitea's object store (verified with cat-file -e)
So there is nothing to push and nothing is behind.

WHY IT STILL ALERTS: gitea_push_mirror_failed encodes Gitea's `last_error`
field, which is level-triggered -- set on a rejected push, cleared only on a
LATER SUCCESSFUL push (documented in scripts/gitea-push-mirror-exporter.py).
Four pushes were rejected today (00:40:23, 01:07:53, 03:54:08, 05:04:29) and
none since. With the repo now quiescent there is no new commit to trigger a
successful push, so nothing clears last_error and the alert stays lit even
though the mirror is healthy. The rule's `for: 2h` is designed to ride out
transients but cannot help here: the condition is latched, not flapping.

ROOT CAUSE OF THE REJECTIONS -- two writers. Every rejection is a
compare-and-swap failure:
  ! [remote rejected] main -> main (cannot lock ref 'refs/heads/main':
      is at <X> but expected <Y>)
and the "is at" hash is DIFFERENT on every attempt. That means GitHub's ref is
moving between Gitea's read and its push, i.e. something other than the mirror
is pushing to github.com/jwiegley/nix-config -- almost certainly direct pushes
from hera, since nix-config is the shared Darwin config repo. A push mirror and
a human pushing to the same branch will race indefinitely; the mirror always
loses because it pushes with an expected-old-value lease.

NOT caused by this host's commits: /etc/nixos pushes to johnw/nixos-config
(with an S), a different repository, whose mirror reports healthy (value 0).
All 11 other mirrors are also 0.

NEEDS JOHN -- this is a workflow decision, not a bug to patch:
  (a) treat GitHub as authoritative for nix-config and delete the push mirror;
  (b) treat Gitea as authoritative and stop pushing to GitHub directly from
      hera;
  (c) keep both and accept periodic rejections plus a latched alert.
Any of these changes where John's own commits are expected to land, so none can
be chosen here.

Recorded so future health cycles do not re-derive this: seeing this alert with
both refs equal means the latched-last_error case, not a broken mirror. Compare
the two tips before investigating further.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
2026-08-13 12:45 -- the SYMPTOM self-resolved, the CAUSE did not.

gitea_push_mirror_failed{repo="nix-config"} is now 0 and sum() over all mirrors
is 0, so GiteaPushMirrorFailing has cleared. That is exactly what the exporter's
level-triggered design predicts: a later successful push cleared Gitea's
last_error. No intervention was made here.

What has NOT changed is why the pushes were rejected in the first place. All
four rejections today were compare-and-swap failures whose "is at" hash differed
every time, i.e. github.com/jwiegley/nix-config is being written by something
other than the mirror. A push mirror and a direct pusher on the same branch race
indefinitely and the mirror always loses, because it pushes with an expected-old
-value lease. Expect this alert to return whenever both writers are active in
the same window.

Retitled to the decision that actually needs making rather than the symptom.
The three options in the description stand; all three change where John's own
commits are expected to land, so none can be taken here.

REPOSITORY IDENTIFICATION (asked 2026-08-13) -- exactly ONE repo is affected.

  Gitea:  johnw/nix-config   (/var/lib/gitea/repositories/johnw/nix-config.git)
  GitHub: github.com/jwiegley/nix-config

18 PushRejected events over the last 7 days, ALL on that one repository:
  2026-08-08 12:34
  2026-08-09 20:02
  2026-08-12 14:35, 15:33, 15:39
  2026-08-13 00:40, 01:07, 03:54, 05:04
Every one is the same compare-and-swap signature ("cannot lock ref
'refs/heads/main': is at <X> but expected <Y>") with a different <X> each time.

NOT affected, despite looking similar:

  johnw/nixos-config  (github.com/jwiegley/nixos-config) -- note the S. This is
  the repo /etc/nixos itself pushes to. Its mirror has never been rejected and
  reports healthy. My own commits are not involved in this problem.

  johnw/hf -- one "Error pushing" on 2026-08-12 04:08, but a DIFFERENT failure
  entirely and not a rejection: exit status 128, "unable to access
  'https://github.com/jwiegley/hf.git/': SSL: certificate subject name
  'dotcom.glb' does not match target hostname 'github.com'". That is a TLS
  interception / edge-certificate artifact, one occurrence, self-resolved. It
  shares no cause with the nix-config race and should not be bundled into this
  issue.

All 12 push mirrors currently report 0 (healthy).

DECISION (John, 2026-08-13): option (b). His other session will push nix-config
only to the hera/Gitea side and no longer directly to github.com. That leaves
the Gitea push mirror as the SOLE writer to github.com/jwiegley/nix-config, so
the compare-and-swap race has no second party and cannot recur by this
mechanism.

No change is needed on vulcan -- the mirror config was never wrong, it was
losing a race it could not win. Nothing to build or deploy here.

VERIFICATION PENDING, deliberately not closed yet. The fix is the absence of an
event, and absence needs a window to be meaningful. Close this when a clean
window has actually been observed:
  - no new PushRejected for johnw/nix-config in the gitea journal, AND
  - at least one SUCCESSFUL mirror push in that window (so we know the path is
    live and we are not just looking at a quiescent repo, which is exactly the
    latched-metric trap this issue already documents).
Check with:
  journalctl -u gitea.service --since '<window>' | grep -c 'nix-config.*PushRejected'
  gitea_push_mirror_failed{repo="nix-config"}   # expect 0
Baseline for comparison: 18 rejections in the 7 days to 2026-08-13, the most
recent at 05:04:29 that day.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

CONFIRMED FIXED. Both close conditions met, including the one specifically designed to rule out a false clean read.

Condition 1 -- no new rejections: zero 'nix-config.*PushRejected' in the gitea
journal since 2026-08-13 13:00, against a baseline of 18 in the preceding 7 days
(most recent 05:04:29 that morning). sum(gitea_push_mirror_failed) = 0.

Condition 2 -- at least one SUCCESSFUL mirror push, so that a quiescent repo
cannot masquerade as a fixed one. Satisfied by evidence:
    gitea  johnw/nix-config  main = a82a21a5d816
    github jwiegley/nix-config main = a82a21a5d816   (identical)
    2 new commits since this afternoon's e0afc16bd31d, and merge-base
    --is-ancestor confirms a82a21a5 descends from it, i.e. a fast-forward and
    not a history rewrite. Newest commit 2026-08-13 20:30:47 -0700.
So the repo advanced by two commits AFTER John's change and the mirror carried
both to GitHub cleanly.

INSTRUMENT CORRECTION worth recording: the check originally planned here --
grepping the gitea journal for successful SyncPushMirror lines -- returns 0 and
is WORTHLESS, because Gitea logs mirror FAILURES only and never logs a success.
Relying on it would have produced 'no successful push observed' and held this
issue open indefinitely. The correct instrument is comparing the two refs and
proving the advance is a fast-forward.

Root cause was two writers racing on the same GitHub branch; every rejection was
a compare-and-swap failure with a different 'is at' hash. John changed his other
session to push nix-config only to the hera/Gitea side, leaving the push mirror
as the sole writer to github.com/jwiegley/nix-config. No change was needed on
vulcan -- the mirror config was never wrong, it was losing a race it could not
win.

Scope confirmed earlier: only johnw/nix-config was ever affected.
johnw/nixos-config (what /etc/nixos pushes to) is a different repository and was
always healthy; johnw/hf's single failure was an unrelated TLS-interception
error.

:MODIFIED: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:38]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:38]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Six directories under /etc/nixos are 0700 johnw:johnw: config, templates, configs, modules, scripts, docs.
At least modules/ and modules/monitoring were changed at 2026-08-10 21:13:33 (pure metadata change — ctime
moved while mtime did not, so a chmod/chown rather than an entry change).

Consequences so far: ALL Loki log-based alerting dead 17.5h (fixed, commit a2685a68e), and
vdirsyncer-status.service left as a latent failure that would have died at the next reboot (fixed, this issue).
Both were fixed structurally by serving files from the Nix store, so the 0700 modes are no longer
load-bearing — but the cause is still unknown and nothing prevents a recurrence on some future path.

Worth identifying: an activation script, a hook, a tool run as johnw, or a manual command. Consider whether
/etc/nixos subdirs should have a declared mode at all, given a root-owned flake repo read by non-root services.

** Close Reason

PREMISE FALSIFIED — nothing chmod'ed anything.

This issue assumed a chmod event on 2026-08-10 21:13:33 that could recur. AIDE disproves it. AIDE covers /etc/nixos (aide.conf:98) and its CONFIG ruleset is p+i+n+u+g+s+b+m+c+sha256 — it tracks PERMISSIONS. Across every run since 2026-08-09 it reported ZERO entries with a p flag; the /etc/nixos directory entries show 'mc..' only, i.e. mtime and ctime, never permissions. So /etc/nixos/modules and modules/monitoring were already 0700 before the incident, not changed during it.

Supporting: johnw's login umask is 0022, so no interactive shell created 0700 directories. The ctime-only timestamps I originally read as chmod evidence are shared by seven 0755 directories too (all ctime 2026-08-03 11:37:18), so they mark some bulk metadata operation, not a mode change.

WHAT WAS ACTUALLY WRONG was architectural, not a permission event: services running as non-root users read files from /etc/nixos at runtime, and /etc/nixos/modules is 0700 johnw:johnw by long standing. Any such service breaks. That is now fixed for both victims — loki serves rules from the Nix store (commit a2685a68e), vdirsyncer-status runs its script from the store (commit 02ab44ea1).

VERIFIED at close: exactly one /etc/nixos reference remains in any deployed unit — hermes-nightly-report.service reading /etc/nixos/certs, which runs as root AND is 0755, so it is safe on both counts. Loki has all 10 rule groups loaded with zero ruler errors and every symlink resolving into the store.

THE DURABLE INVARIANT, which is the real output of this work: NO SERVICE MAY READ FROM /etc/nixos AT RUNTIME. Reference repo files as Nix paths so they are copied into the world-readable store. The 0700 modes need no correcting — they are simply not load-bearing any more.

Not determined: why loki's ruler began erroring at 21:13:51 specifically, given the modes predate it. Left unresolved deliberately — it no longer has any consequence, and the architectural fix makes it unable to recur.

* DONE [#E] Verified: textfile-collector staleness tiers cover all 50 collectors exactly
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-voq
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 16:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-21 Fri 16:46]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-21 Fri 16:46]
:CLOSE_REASON: Verification complete; no defect. 17 classified + 33 backstopped = 50 total, every collector covered by exactly one tier, oldest 5.5d against a 14d backstop, all six rules inactive/healthy. Recorded chiefly for the three instrument errors documented in the description — especially truncating a 304-char expression at 300 chars, which turned a 14-day threshold into an apparent 120-second one.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Verification, not a defect: the textfile-collector staleness scheme is complete.
Recorded so a future cycle does not redo it, and because getting to that answer
took three instrument errors of mine that each produced a confident, alarming,
WRONG result.

THE SCHEME, thresholds read in full:
  TextfileCollectorStaleFast          1800s   (0.5h)   7 collectors
  TextfileCollectorStaleHourly       10800s   (3h)     3 collectors
  TextfileCollectorStaleDaily        93600s   (26h)    7 collectors
  TextfileCollectorUnclassifiedStale 1209600s (14d)   33 collectors (backstop)
  TextfileCollectorMassLoss          count(node_textfile_mtime_seconds) < 45
  TextfileCollectorScrapeError       node_textfile_scrape_error > 0

COVERAGE IS EXACT: 17 explicitly classified + 33 caught by the backstop = 50 =
total collectors reporting mtime. Every collector is covered by exactly one tier.
Oldest collector on the host is 5.5 days (container_cve) against a 14-day
backstop. All six rules read state=inactive, health=ok.

The design is deliberate and sound: tight thresholds for collectors whose cadence
matters, and a loose 14-day backstop so an ABANDONED collector is still caught
without demanding that all 50 be individually classified.

THREE INSTRUMENT ERRORS I MADE, all worth knowing because each looked like a real
finding:

1. REGEX ESCAPING. I hand-retyped the rule's expression from the API's JSON, where
   the regex appears as `\\.prom`. That JSON escaping represents `\.prom`; passing
   `\\.prom` through to Prometheus means "backslash followed by any char", which
   matches nothing, so the NEGATIVE match `!~` became true for every series. Result:
   28 phantom "uncovered" collectors. Take the expression verbatim from the API,
   never retype it.

2. TRUNCATION -- the worst of the three. I printed the expression with [:300]. The
   expression is 304 characters. The four characters I cut were "9600" off the end
   of "1209600", so I read the threshold as 120 SECONDS instead of 14 DAYS. That
   single truncation turned a correct, well-designed rule into an apparent
   catastrophe: 28 collectors "far past" a 2-minute threshold with the rule
   somehow not firing. Never truncate an expression you are about to reason about.

3. NAIVE PARSING. Extracting the collector list with `\(([^)]*)\)` matched the
   empty parens of `time()` first and returned one blank name. Harmless here only
   because I had already spotted error 2.

The through-line: every one of these made a HEALTHY system look broken, which is
the opposite of the usual failure mode on this host (a broken instrument reading
clean). Both directions are worth guarding against.

NOTHING CHANGED. No configuration was modified; there is nothing here to fix.

* DONE [#D] Two alert rules apply rate() to a 0/1 gauge and can never fire
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-w3w
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-17 Mon 16:50]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-18 Tue 19:46]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-18 Tue 19:46]
:CLOSE_REASON: Both rules rewritten on node_systemd_service_restart_total. Generic rule is now a 6h/>8 chronic-churn detector, genuinely disjoint from ServiceRestartLooping; the fetchmail duplicate was deleted with its rationale recorded in place.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
Two alert rules apply rate() to node_systemd_unit_state, which is a 0/1 GAUGE,
not a counter. Both are provably incapable of firing. Found during the
2026-08-17 health cycle while investigating fetchmail-good restarts.

THE DEFECT. rate() is defined for monotonically increasing counters. Applied to
a gauge that flips 0 -> 1 -> 0, it reads each fall as a counter reset, so the
result is a tiny artifact value rather than a restart frequency. The thresholds
were chosen as if the value were "restarts per second", which it is not.

  modules/services/fetchmail-alerts.nix:45   FetchmailGoodFlapping
      expr: rate(node_systemd_unit_state{name="fetchmail-good.service",
                 state="activating"}[15m]) > 0.05

  modules/monitoring/alerts/systemd.yaml:32  ServiceRestartingFrequently
      expr: rate(node_systemd_unit_state{state="activating",
                 name=~".*\\.service"}[15m]) > 0.1

MEASURED, against 7 days of TSDB (retention reaches back to 2026-01-10):

  FetchmailGoodFlapping      max value over 7d = 0.00112   threshold 0.05
                             -> 45x below the threshold
  ServiceRestartingFrequently max across ALL services 7d = 0.00897  threshold 0.1
                             -> 11x below the threshold

  0.05/s sustained over a 15m window means 45 increases in 15 minutes. Reality
  for fetchmail-good is 8 state changes in 7 DAYS.

  Firing series in the last 14 days: FetchmailGoodFlapping 0.

WHY THIS IS LOW SEVERITY RATHER THAN A COVERAGE HOLE. ServiceRestartLooping
(systemd.yaml:49) does the same job correctly, on the right metric:

    increase(node_systemd_service_restart_total{...}[30m]) > 3

It covers fetchmail-good (the counter series exists for it), it HAS fired in
the last 14 days for other units, and the comment at fetchmail.nix:120 records
that it already caught a real fetchmail restart loop ("restart-looped 23x on
Name or service not known"). It is also well calibrated for this unit:
fetchmail-good peaks at 2.01 restarts per 30m over the full 30-day window,
against a >3 threshold, so it stays correctly quiet at the current baseline and
would fire at 4.

So the working rule already covers the case. The two dead rules add nothing but
the appearance of coverage.

THE OPTIONS -- this needs John because they are genuine alternatives, not a
defect with one obvious repair:

  1. Delete both. They are redundant with ServiceRestartLooping, and deleting a
     rule that provably cannot fire changes exactly zero notifications. Fewest
     moving parts. My recommendation for FetchmailGoodFlapping.

  2. Rewrite them on node_systemd_service_restart_total. Preserves the intent of
     having a fetchmail-specific sensitivity, but any threshold tighter than the
     generic >3/30m needs choosing, and a rewritten rule would be close to a
     duplicate of the generic one.

  3. Leave them. Costs nothing at runtime, but a dead rule is worse than no rule
     because it reads as coverage during a review.

NOTE on option 1 for ServiceRestartingFrequently specifically: the comment
directly above ServiceRestartLooping says "The two rules are complementary --
keep both." That was a deliberate prior decision, so deleting that one in
particular should be John's call rather than a cleanup.
#+end_example

** Comments

#+begin_src json

  {
    "id": 14,
    "issue_id": "nixos-w3w",
    "author": "johnw",
    "text": "Both rate()-on-a-gauge rules resolved per John's decision (2026-08-18): rewrite\nboth properly, on the correct counter metric.\n\n1. ServiceRestartingFrequently (modules/monitoring/alerts/systemd.yaml) is now a\n   CHRONIC-CHURN detector, deliberately not another burst detector:\n\n       increase(node_systemd_service_restart_total{\n                 name=~\".*\\\\.service\",\n                 name!~\"[0-9a-f]{64}-.*\\\\.service\"}[6h]) > 8      for: 15m\n\n   This is what earns the \"keep both\" note above ServiceRestartLooping. A\n   rewrite at any similar window/threshold would have duplicated that rule\n   (30m/>3); a 6h window catches a shape the burst rule STRUCTURALLY CANNOT --\n   a service restarting steadily without ever reaching 3-in-30m.\n   fetchmail-good is exactly that shape: peak 2.01 per 30m, but 7 per 24h.\n\n   Calibrated on 30 days: >8 in 6h matches 1 service, and NOT the one the burst\n   rule catches, so the two now cover disjoint failure modes. >5 was rejected --\n   it sits one restart above fetchmail-good's own benign peak of 4.00/6h and\n   would fire on known-good behaviour.\n\n2. FetchmailGoodFlapping (modules/services/fetchmail-alerts.nix) DELETED rather\n   than rewritten, per John's follow-up answer. The generic chronic rule now\n   covers this unit's failure shape, and a fetchmail-specific rule would need a\n   threshold in the narrow 5-8 band with no incident to calibrate against --\n   which is precisely how the dead rule came to exist. A comment recording the\n   old expression, why it could never fire, and why it was not replaced is left\n   in place at the deletion site.\n\nWHY THEY WERE DEAD, restated for the record: node_systemd_unit_state is a 0/1\nGAUGE. rate() reads every 1->0 fall as a counter reset, so the output was a\nunitless artifact rather than restarts/second, while both thresholds had been\nwritten as if it meant restarts/second. Measured maxima over 7 days: 0.00112\nagainst a 0.05 threshold (45x below) and 0.00897 against 0.1 (11x below). Zero\nfiring series for FetchmailGoodFlapping in 14 days.\n\nVERIFICATION:\n  * new expression queried live: status=success, no error, 0 series now\n  * 7-day replay of the new expression: 0 services would have fired (quiet),\n    against 1 over 30 days -- i.e. it is live but not noisy\n  * promtool accepted both files; the built store copies confirm the new content:\n      .../alerts/systemd.yaml            contains the churning rule\n      .../fetchmail-alerts.yml           has 0 active FetchmailGoodFlapping\n                                         alert lines, FetchmailGoodDown intact\n\nNOT YET ACTIVE, blocked on nixos-5eo (unrelated P1: the host cannot switch).",

* DONE [#D] rclone-cloud-backup passes --track-renames to copy, logging ERROR every run
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-x0u
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 04:48]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-13 Thu 04:48]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-13 Thu 04:48]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

rclone-cloud-backup carried `--track-renames` in its shared `baseFlags`, so the
flag reached the `rclone copy` call sites as well as the `rclone sync` ones.

rclone honours --track-renames only for `sync`. For `copy` and `move` it ignores
the flag and logs, at ERROR priority, once per invocation:

    ERROR : Local file system at <dest>/SharedWithMe: Ignoring --track-renames
            as it doesn't work with copy or move, only sync

So every nightly pass emitted ERROR-priority journal lines about a flag that was
doing nothing. Surfaced during the 2026-08-13 04:43 health cycle as the sole new
entry in a 4h `journalctl -p 3` sweep; the unit itself exits 0, so this never
failed anything -- it just polluted the error stream, which is exactly what makes
a real rclone error harder to spot.

Two `copy` call sites were affected (SharedWithMe, and the per-shared-drive copy
inside the drives loop); the two `sync` sites (Google MyDrive, OneDrive) were
using it correctly.

FIX: lifted --track-renames out of baseFlags into its own `trackRenames` binding
and applied it at the two sync call sites only.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Fixed and verified in the DEPLOYED unit, not just the source.

modules/services/rclone-cloud-backup.nix: --track-renames lifted out of baseFlags
into a separate `trackRenames` binding, applied only at the two `rclone sync`
call sites (Google MyDrive line 211, OneDrive line 244). Both `rclone copy` sites
(SharedWithMe, and the per-shared-drive copy in the drives loop) no longer receive it.

Verified against the generated ExecStart script (rclone-cloud-backup-start) after
./build switch, rather than trusting the Nix source:
  - total occurrences of --track-renames in the deployed script: 2
  - both fall on `rclone sync` lines (54, 87)
  - copy-context occurrences: 0

The unit already exited 0 before this change, so nothing was broken; the value is
that the nightly pass stops writing ERROR-priority lines about an ignored flag,
which was making a genuine rclone error harder to see.
#+end_example

* DONE [#D] Gitea push mirror for 'hf' failing on a TLS hostname mismatch
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-x8j
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 09:55]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-12 Wed 12:23]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-12 Wed 12:23]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
GiteaPushMirrorFailing on repo hf. Stored last_error is a TLS mismatch:
  certificate subject name 'dotcom.glb' does not match target hostname 'github.com'
(dotcom.glb is GitHub's internal LB default cert, normally the signature of SNI not being sent).

INVESTIGATION 2026-08-12 — every structural explanation is RULED OUT:

,  * NOT environmental. 12 push mirrors exist; 11 succeed. HTTPS to github.com works from both johnw
    and the gitea user with the correct CN=github.com certificate.
,  * NOT configuration. All 12 remotes are structurally identical: scheme=https, host=github.com,
    no credentials embedded in remote_address.
,  * NOT timing. Ten mirrors synced at exactly 08-12 04:08. NINE SUCCEEDED, hf failed — same second,
    same host, same config.
,  * NOT the remote. GitHub API (read-only) reports jwiegley/hf exists, public, not archived, not
    disabled, push permission true, default branch main, pushed_at 2026-05-20.

What remains: a sub-second transient hitting one push in a sequential batch of twelve, or a stale
last_error that a successful attempt would clear. The mirror interval is 8h and gitea derives
gitea_push_mirror_failed from last_error, so one failure keeps the alert lit for hours regardless.

The repo is dormant: 227 kB in gitea, updated 2026-03-16.

NEXT: the scheduled retry (~12:08) is the discriminator. If it clears, close as transient. If it fails
again with the same TLS error while siblings succeed alongside it, that is genuinely anomalous and
worth deeper work. Deliberately did NOT force a manual sync — that is outward-facing against John's
GitHub account.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Resolved — transient, exactly as the elimination predicted. The mirror retried at 2026-08-12 12:14 and succeeded; gitea's last_error is now empty and all 12 push mirrors report clean.

The four eliminations that led here, none of which needed changing anything:
,  * NOT environmental — 11 of 12 mirrors succeeded throughout; HTTPS to github.com worked from both johnw and the gitea user with the correct CN=github.com certificate.
,  * NOT configuration — all 12 remotes structurally identical (https, github.com, no embedded credentials).
,  * NOT timing — ten mirrors synced at exactly 04:08; NINE succeeded and only hf failed, same second, same host.
,  * NOT the remote — GitHub API confirmed jwiegley/hf exists, public, not archived, push permission true.

MONITORING LAG WORTH REMEMBERING, and the reason the alert outlived the fault by ~50 minutes: gitea_push_mirror_failed comes from a textfile exporter on an HOURLY timer (gitea-push-mirror-exporter.timer, OnCalendar=hourly). The mirror recovered at 12:14 but the textfile still said 1, having last been written at 12:03:57; the next scheduled write was 13:04. Forcing the exporter (read-only) rewrote it to 0 and the alert cleared.

So the total worst-case lag between a real recovery and the alert clearing is up to 8h (the mirror interval, before it even retries) plus up to 1h (the exporter interval). For a single transient that means an alert can stay lit most of a day with nothing wrong. Worth knowing before treating a firing GiteaPushMirrorFailing as current.
#+end_example

While quantifying the mbsync pages (nixos-mwl) I ranked every unit tripping the
generic SystemdServiceFailed rule over 14 days. mbsync was not close to the top:

  1955 samples  pushme-positron.service
   669 samples  hermes-fallback-counter.service
   644 samples  logwatch.service
   400 samples  git-workspace-archive.service
   254 samples  mbsync-rbcca.service      <- fixed in nixos-mwl
   109 samples  mbsync-bia.service        <- fixed in nixos-mwl
    16 samples  container-health-exporter.service
     6 samples  NetworkManager-wait-online.service

SystemdServiceFailed is severity=critical, so pushme-positron alone accounts for
roughly 5x the noise that nixos-mwl was filed for, and the top four together are
~3600 of the ~4000 samples.

Not investigated yet -- this is a census, not a diagnosis. Each needs its own
look, and the right fix will differ: some of these are oneshots whose transient
failure is genuinely uninteresting (the mbsync pattern, fixable by in-process
retry), while others may be reporting something real that nobody is acting on.

pushme-positron is worth taking first on volume alone. Note it was recently made
restartIfChanged = false after it blocked two switches, so tread carefully around
its unit semantics.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
THE PREMISE OF THIS ISSUE IS OBSOLETE. I filed it from a 14-day count, which
aggregated resolved incidents and read as a live noise problem. Re-measured
2026-08-16 with a recent window beside the historical one:

  unit                                     14d     48h
  pushme-positron.service                 1955       0
  hermes-fallback-counter.service           669       0
  logwatch.service                          644       0
  git-workspace-archive.service             400       0
  mbsync-rbcca.service                      225      57
  mbsync-bia.service                        109      81
  container-health-exporter.service          16       6
  NetworkManager-wait-online.service          6       0
  postgresql-nocobase-setup.service           4       4
  microvm-state-share-exporter.service        1       0

The top four -- 3668 of roughly 4000 samples -- have gone COMPLETELY SILENT in
48h. SystemdServiceFailed is inactive with 0 active alerts right now.

pushme-positron, the headline number, was traced properly:
  - 19 exits with status=1 (rsync "connection unexpectedly closed", error 255 --
    andoria unreachable) on 08-10, 08-12 and 08-13
  - 8 start-timeouts on 08-11 and 08-12
  - the 12h ceiling landed 2026-08-12 23:23 (cf28397aa), and there have been ZERO
    timeouts since
  - ZERO failures of any kind since 08-13; the exporter and systemd agree the
    unit is inactive/success, not latched
So both halves were already fixed before this issue was written. I misread a
1337-sample "last 3 days" figure as current when every sample sat at the 08-13
edge of the window.

The two mbsync entries are the case fixed under nixos-mwl, and the fix holds:
57 -> 0 for rbcca and 81 -> 0 for bia comparing the 48h before the retry deployed
against the 12h after, with 93 successful syncs and 0 exhausted-retry failures.
Honest caveat: retry activations were also 0, so no transient has occurred to
exercise the retry path -- the noise stopped, but the retry itself is not yet
proven in production.

postgresql-nocobase-setup's 4 samples are my own, from the ordering race fixed in
d714e4237.

CLOSING as resolved-by-other-work rather than leaving a P2 open against a number
that no longer describes anything. The measurement lesson is worth keeping: a
14-day count over ALERTS aggregates episodes that are already over, so pair it
with a recent window before calling anything noisy.

container-health-exporter (6 in 48h) is the only genuinely live residue and is
small; worth a look if it grows.
#+end_example

#+begin_example
HermesExtractFailing fires on normal operation. Whether to retune or drop it is
a judgement call about what John wants to be told, so it is filed rather than
decided.

NOT A BUG REPORT. The alert works exactly as written. The question is whether
what it is written to detect is worth an alert.

MEASURED BASELINE, across all four agent-log rotations (2026-08-04 to
2026-08-17), which is the full retained history:

  batches        105   -- fully ok 74, partial 19, zero-success 12  (11% zero)
  URLs attempted 157   -- extracted 121                             (77% ok)
  worker-level errors  0   (all four rotations)

So roughly one batch in nine returns nothing, and in every observed case the
cause was a page the extractor could not render rather than a broken extractor.
Zero worker errors in two weeks.

The rule fires when the newest batch had zero successes, that failure is newer
than the last success, and it happened within the last hour. With extraction
running at only a few batches a day, an 11% zero-rate means it will keep firing
indefinitely on healthy operation. Over the 7 days sampled when nixos-ygr was
filed: 116 firing samples in two episodes.

WHAT CHANGED SINCE FILING. The log line now carries the failure reasons
(nixos-ygr, commit adding _failure_summary to pkgs/hermes-local-extract/
provider.py), so any future firing can be diagnosed in one grep instead of
being unfalsifiable. That removes the reason the original issue could not be
acted on, but it does not by itself decide the alert's fate.

THE OPTIONS, in the order I would rank them:

  1. Require a SUSTAINED failure rather than a single batch -- e.g. no
     successful extraction for N hours, instead of one zero-success batch.
     Keeps a genuine outage detectable (guest lost network, DNS down, worker
     dead) while ignoring the ordinary unrenderable page. My recommendation.

  2. Leave it. It is bounded and self-clearing within the hour, and it is
     honest about what it observed. Costs an occasional notification that
     needs no action.

  3. Drop it. The worker-health metrics (hermes_extract_worker_ok, the probe
     duration) already cover the failure modes that are actually faults, and
     zero worker errors in two weeks argues they are quiet for a good reason.

Option 1 needs a threshold chosen, which is exactly the input needed here.

DO NOT act on this without John. It changes what he gets told about, which is
his call and not an obvious defect to be repaired.
#+end_example

* DONE [#C] Adding any new restic backup spuriously fires BackupNotRunRecently until its first run completes
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-ycp
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-22 Sat 14:07]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-22 Sat 16:53]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-22 Sat 16:53]
:SOURCE_REPO: .
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Found 2026-08-22 while adding /tank/Machines. Within minutes of deploying the new backup, BackupNotRunRecently fired with 'Backup Machines last ran 20687d 21h ago' -- the epoch-0 signature (time() - 0), same shape as the previously-seen '20628d'.

CAUSE: backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds is 0 for a unit that has never COMPLETED. A newly added backup is in exactly that state from deploy until its first run finishes, which for a large dataset is hours. The rule is (time() - backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds) > 129600, for 5m, so it fires almost immediately and is guaranteed noise for every future backup addition.

NOTE the label trap: this alert carries backup="Machines" from the backup-status exporter, whereas restic-metrics emits repository="Machines". A silence scoped to one does NOT cover the other; I had to create a second silence.

POSSIBLE FIXES (needs a judgement call, hence not done autonomously):
 a) have the exporter omit the series entirely for a unit with no completion timestamp, so absent() semantics apply rather than a false 1970 reading;
 b) add 'and backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds > 0' to the rule, which keeps a genuinely stuck new backup invisible;
 c) leave it and silence on each addition.
(a) looks right -- a metric asserting 'last ran in 1970' is worse than no metric -- but it changes exporter semantics that other rules may rely on, so it deserves a deliberate decision.

** Close Reason

FIXED by splitting the rule, and my original framing of this issue was wrong.

I filed it assuming the exporter's epoch-0 was a defect. It is not. health-check-exporters.nix:78-79 documents the zero as DELIBERATE -- 'All three are n/a only for a unit that has genuinely never started, which correctly yields 0 so a never-run backup still alerts.' Both fixes I proposed last cycle (omit the series, or guard the rule with > 0 alone) would have defeated that intent and made a backup that never runs silent. The alert firing was CORRECT; only its rendering was nonsense: 'Backup Machines last ran 20687d 21h 1m 0s ago'.

So the conflated rule was split into two, in modules/monitoring/alerts/health-checks.yaml:
  BackupNotRunRecently  (time() - backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds) > 129600 and backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds > 0
  BackupNeverRun        backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds == 0        [new, 5m dwell, warning]
The guard is not novel: BackupLastRunFailed immediately below already carried the identical '> 0' for this same reason.

COVERAGE PRESERVED, verified against history rather than argued: over the window Machines sat at 0 (19:51-21:01 UTC, 15 samples) the old rule went pending 19:51 and fired 19:56-21:01, and 'backup_last_run_timestamp_seconds == 0' matches that window exactly. The 5m dwell is kept, so timing is unchanged too.

VERIFIED after switch: both rules health=ok, lastError=none, evaluated at 16:50:50 with 0 active alerts (correct -- all 11 tracked backups have run and none is stale); 486 rules loaded, up from 485; no rule in health=err.

NOT CHANGED, and left as John's call if he ever wants it: a newly added backup still alerts within 5m rather than getting a grace period until its first scheduled run. That is the pre-existing behaviour and the exporter's stated intent, so I preserved it rather than quietly introducing a grace window.

* DONE [#E] New rootless quadlet container needs a manual user daemon-reload on its first switch
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-yfp
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 23:17]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-16 Sun 04:50]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-16 Sun 04:50]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Introducing a NEW rootless quadlet container leaves its user unit unusable until
a manual `systemctl --user daemon-reload`, so the container does not start on the
switch that creates it.

Observed 2026-08-16 enabling NocoBase. After switch-to-configuration completed:

  systemctl --user start nocobase.service
  -> Failed to start nocobase.service: Unit nocobase.service has a bad unit file setting.

The quadlet-nix stub in ~/.config/systemd/user/default.target.wants/nocobase.service
was present (just [Install] + a config hash), and the .container file was correct
and structurally identical to wallabag's. The podman quadlet GENERATOR had simply
not re-run in the freshly-started user manager, so no real service existed behind
the stub. A `systemctl --user daemon-reload` as the nocobase user cleared it
immediately (LoadState=loaded, no LoadError) and the container then started and
has run since.

NOT a persistent defect: nocobase.service now reports is-enabled=enabled,
is-active=active, WantedBy=default.target -- identical to wallabag and
openproject -- so it will come up normally on boot. Low priority because it costs
one manual command, once, only on the switch that first introduces a container.

Worth fixing only if adding rootless containers becomes routine; the obvious
approach is a daemon-reload in the home-manager activation for these users.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
FIXED by adding modules/users/home-manager/quadlet-daemon-reload.nix as a
home-manager sharedModule.

ROOT CAUSE CONFIRMED IN THE GENERATED CODE, not inferred. quadlet-nix's own
activation step does three things (lines 302-304 of the generated activate
script): note the step, mkdir ~/.config/quadlet-nix/, and symlink
~/.config/quadlet-nix/out at the user manager's generator directory. It never
reloads the user manager -- `grep -c daemon-reload` on that script returned
exactly 0. But the real unit behind quadlet-nix's [Install]-only stub is produced
by podman's quadlet GENERATOR, and generators only run on a daemon-reload. So on
the switch that first introduces a container the stub exists with nothing behind
it, and systemd reports "Unit <name>.service has a bad unit file setting".

VERIFIED AFTER SWITCHING to gwjik8iil9fff5dmr0q159ml7c03gs6z:
  - nocobase's activation script now contains daemon-reload exactly once
  - ordering is right: quadletNix at line 302, quadletDaemonReload at 307, the
    reload itself at 314 -- so the generator sees the .container file that
    quadlet-nix just placed
  - johnw's activation script contains it ZERO times, so the self-gating works:
    the module emits nothing for users with no quadlet containers, matching how
    rootless-podman-image-prune.nix self-targets rather than keeping a user list
  - 0 failed units, nocobase container still Up, NocoBase still serving HTTP 200

HONEST LIMIT OF THIS VERIFICATION: what is proven is that the reload now runs in
the right place for the right users. What is NOT yet proven is the end-to-end
symptom fix, because that requires introducing a brand-new container, and no new
one exists to introduce. The next container added to this host is the real test.
If it still fails to start on its first switch, the guard (`[ -d
$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd ]`) is the first thing to check -- during a very early
activation that directory may not exist yet, in which case the reload is skipped
by design and the generator runs at boot instead.

Deliberately best-effort: the reload is wrapped in `|| true` and guarded, because
failing a home-manager activation over a refresh that the next boot would perform
anyway would be strictly worse than doing nothing.
#+end_example

* DONE [#D] HermesExtractFailing: bounded and self-clearing, but the metrics cannot say WHY extraction failed
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-ygr
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: task
:CREATED:  [2026-08-16 Sun 20:46]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-17 Mon 12:12]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-17 Mon 12:12]
:CLOSE_REASON: Instrumentation gap closed: the extract log line now names WHY each failure happened. Verified end-to-end on the deployed store path.
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

#+begin_example
HermesExtractFailing fired at 2026-08-17T03:17:56Z (20:17 PDT). Investigated; not
acted on, because the evidence needed to choose a fix does not exist.

WHAT IS HAPPENING. The rule fires when the most recent extraction batch had zero
successes AND that failure is newer than the last success AND within the last
hour:

  hermes_extract_last_failure_timestamp_seconds > 0
  and (time() - ...last_failure...) < 3600
  and ...last_failure... > ...last_success...

Measured state at the time of the alert:
  last failure  2026-08-16 20:09:57 PDT
  last success  2026-08-16 19:39:50 PDT   (30 min earlier)
  worker_ok     1        -- the worker itself is runnable
  worker probe  0.239s   -- and responsive
  VM uptime     58.4 h, microvm@hermes active

So this is NOT a dead worker or a dead VM. One batch returned nothing.

IT IS BOUNDED AND SELF-CLEARING, which is good design worth recording so nobody
"fixes" it: the < 3600 clause means the alert releases one hour after the failing
batch regardless of when the next batch runs. This instance was due to clear at
21:09 PDT. That matters because extraction is LOW VOLUME -- 2 successes and 2
failures in the last 24h, four batches total -- so without that clause a single
failure would hold the alert for the ~6 hours until the next batch.

FREQUENCY: 116 firing samples over 7 days, in exactly two episodes (08-14: 60,
08-16: 56). Not continuous, not new.

Roughly half of recent batches failed (2 of 4 in 24h). Treat that ratio as
indicative only -- four batches is far too small a sample to call it a rate.

THE REAL GAP, and why this is filed rather than fixed: the published metrics are
timestamps only. There is no per-URL outcome, no error class, and the VM journal
carries no extract lines at all. The rule's own description names three causes
needing completely different responses:

  - the guest lost outbound network
  - DNS is failing
  - the pages requested are JavaScript-only, which this extractor cannot render
    BY DESIGN (see pkgs/hermes-local-extract/src/extract_worker.py)

Nothing in the current instrumentation discriminates between them. The third is
not a fault at all; the first two are. So the alert can tell John that extraction
failed but not whether anything is broken.

WHAT WOULD MAKE IT ACTIONABLE: publish an error class or per-URL outcome counter
alongside the timestamps -- e.g. hermes_extract_last_error_kind or a small
counter split by reason. That is a change to the Hermes health exporter and to
what the worker reports, so it is real work rather than a metric rename, and it
needs John's call on whether the capability is worth that instrumentation.
#+end_example

** Close Reason

Done and verified through the deployed path, not a proxy for it.

WHAT CHANGED. modules/lib/resticOperations.nix loses the `snapshots` operation
entirely (its only consumer was this report section). modules/services/monitoring.nix
gains `b2Buckets`, derived by parsing config.services.restic.backups[*].repository
rather than hardcoding a list, and `resticBucketTable`, a writeShellApplication that
lists each bucket with rclone. logwatch.service gains
LoadCredential=aws-keys:/run/secrets/aws-keys paired with an empty SetCredential.

VERIFIED, live, via systemd-run with the real LoadCredential and SetCredential
properties -- the same credential path the deployed unit uses, not rclone with
env-sourced keys, which is a different code path and the one both agents had
originally exercised:

    Bucket                   Size on B2  Last Updated
    jwiegley-Audio              101.5GB  2026-08-19 02:10
    jwiegley-Backups-Misc       367.9GB  2026-08-19 03:30
    jwiegley-Databases          110.8GB  2026-08-19 02:51
    jwiegley-Documents           14.5GB  2026-08-19 04:25
    jwiegley-Home               270.8GB  2026-08-19 03:54
    jwiegley-Photos             418.9GB  2026-08-19 04:40
    jwiegley-Public               8.0GB  2026-08-19 04:10
    jwiegley-Video              258.2GB  2026-08-19 05:30
    jwiegley-doc                 42.3GB  2026-08-19 02:30
    jwiegley-src                 15.7GB  2026-08-19 03:12
    TOTAL (10 buckets)            1.6TB

exit 0, 16.1s wall, 38.7MB received. Well inside the 180s section budget.

FAILURE PATH VERIFIED TOO, and this mattered more than the happy path. Run with
the empty SetCredential fallback and no LoadCredential, the section prints
"ERROR: B2 credentials unavailable -- bucket sizes were not collected." and exits
1. It does NOT emit a table of zeros. That distinction is the whole point on this
host: a monitoring path that fails in a way that reads as a clean result is a
recurring defect class here, and a bucket table silently reporting 0 bytes would
read as "the backups shrank".

The implementation degrades visibly at every level: per-bucket timeout 60s and
section budget 180s, with buckets not reached labelled "not reached (section
budget spent)"; a failed or timed-out listing gives an ERROR row; a bucket that
lists successfully but is empty gives "EMPTY -- no objects found -- INVESTIGATE",
with a trailing note that a restic repository is never legitimately empty; and a
partial total is labelled "INCOMPLETE" rather than presented as the total.

CROSS-CHECKED. A second agent measured all ten buckets independently with
`rclone size --json` -- a different code path from the `lsf --files-only | awk`
pipeline the implementation uses -- before it knew the implementation existed.
Public came to 8,001,422,466 bytes from both. Total ~1.609 TB across ~94k objects.

Worth recording: restic's own restic_repo_size_bytes runs LOW against the real B2
footprint, by 0.03% to 4.8% depending on the repository. So this table will not
agree with that metric, and that is expected rather than a bug in either.

Two flags in the script are load-bearing and both were arrived at the hard way:
--use-server-modtime (without it rclone issues a HEAD per object and the Photos
listing blows past two minutes; with it the whole run is 16s), and
RCLONE_CONFIG=/dev/null (johnw's real rclone config is password-encrypted, so a
bare rclone prompts for a configuration password on stdin -- under logwatch,
non-interactive, that is a hang, not untidiness).

The 'upstairs exit eco' node is triggered by the OFFICE occupancy chain
(mac active 1min / office door opened / 06:00 daily -> john home? -> office door closed?).
It fires 6+ times a day and acts on climate.upstairs_vtherm.

Unresolved: is that coupling deliberate (pre-cooling upstairs when work starts) or leftover from the
earlier office HVAC work? If leftover, the right fix is deleting the node rather than editing its preset.

* DEFER [#D] Alert Notifier drops iPhone pushes when HA is unreachable instead of retrying
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-zpm
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 23:50]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-20 Thu 10:32]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:DEFERRED: [2026-11-18 Wed 09:00]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

The 'notify iphone' api-call-service node on the Alert Notifier tab fails with NoConnectionError whenever the Home Assistant websocket is down, and the message is DROPPED rather than queued or retried.

MEASURED from nodered_events.msg_events (retention floor 2026-06-30, so ~7 weeks):
  all-time  905 notifications, 9 failed = 0.99%
  2026-07-03   93 total,  6 failed   <- the cold-reboot day
  2026-08-04   42 total,  1 failed
  2026-08-19    6 total,  2 failed   <- a nixos-rebuild switch

The failures are not random: they cluster exactly on reboots and rebuilds, i.e. precisely when the host is most likely to be generating alerts worth seeing. Today's two were CRITICAL SystemdServiceFailed and CRITICAL WebServiceDown, dropped 32s after a switch started.

NOT AN OUTAGE, and deliberately filed at P3 rather than higher: Alertmanager carries 3 email_configs of its own, so the email path is INDEPENDENT of Node-RED and Home Assistant entirely. Nothing went unnotified -- the iPhone push is a secondary, lower-latency channel. Verified via /api/v2/status receiver list.

PROPOSED FIX, needs John because it changes notification behaviour: this repo already has an 'act until observed' retry subflow built for exactly this shape. Wrapping the notify call in it -- or a catch -> delay -> retry with a bounded attempt count -- would ride out the ~3 minute window where HA is restarting. Note that subflow edits need a full POST /flows, not a partial deploy.

Worth deciding rather than assuming: a retry that outlives the restart could also deliver a stale alert minutes late, which for a transient 'WebServiceDown (+12 more)' during a rebuild may be noise. A bounded retry with a freshness check is probably the right shape.

DISCOVERED-FROM: investigating three Node-RED errors John reported, which turned out to be ordinary restart fallout from my own 19:22:50 build.

* DONE [#D] open-webui container-health rules can never fire: health status is permanently 3
:PROPERTIES:
:OBR_SCHEMA_VERSION: 17
:ID:       nixos-zpx
:LABELS:   []
:ISSUE_TYPE: bug
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 22:58]
:MODIFIED: [2026-08-19 Wed 23:26]
:CREATED_BY: johnw
:FINISHED: [2026-08-19 Wed 23:26]
:SOURCE_REPO: nixos
:SOURCE_REPO_PATH: /etc/nixos
:ORIGINAL_SIZE: 0
:END:

Two of the open-webui container-health rules can never fire, verified live 2026-08-19.

container_health_status{container="open-webui"} is permanently 3, because the image's
healthCmd is commented out and 3 encodes "no healthcheck configured" (0=healthy,
1=unhealthy, 2=starting, 3=none). Against that:

  modules/monitoring/alerts/open-webui.yaml:8   ... or container_health_status{...} == 1
  modules/monitoring/alerts/open-webui.yaml:37  ... > 0 and container_health_status{...} < 3

Line 8's disjunct cannot match, since the value is 3 and never 1. Line 37 cannot match
either: 3 is not < 3. So the only live coverage for this container is
container_running == 0 and the absent() guard on line 7 -- which do work, so this is not
an outage, it is a rule that reads as coverage and catches nothing.

Same dead-metric class the repo remediated in June, when 123 rules were found unfirable
because of a metric-name mismatch. Worth checking whether OTHER containers with no
healthcheck have the same shape -- 15 of 17 containers on this host report 3.

FOUND BY: an agent sweeping Nagios comments out of an adjacent file. It correctly did not
touch open-webui.yaml, which was outside its assigned scope. Verified independently
before filing rather than taken on report.

NOT FIXED because the right fix is a real decision, not a strike: either drop the two dead
disjuncts and rely on container_running, or give the image a healthCmd so the existing
rules become meaningful. The second is better monitoring and a behaviour change to a
running container.

** Close Reason

#+begin_example
Fixed, and the premise the issue was filed on turned out to be wrong in a way that made the fix easier.

I filed this saying the right fix was 'a real decision': drop the dead disjuncts, or give the image a healthCmd so the rules become meaningful. modules/users/home-manager/open-webui.nix:205-217 settles it -- the healthCmd is not missing, it is DELIBERATELY DISABLED and left commented out in place, because each 30s check created a systemd-logind session, 3 log lines a time, ~12,960 lines/day. So the second alternative was already decided against, with a measured reason. That collapses the fork into mechanical alignment.

DONE:
,  * removed the 'or container_health_status{...} == 1' disjunct from OpenWebUIContainerDown
,  * deleted OpenWebUIUnhealthy entirely ('> 0 and < 3' against a value pinned at 3)
,  * rewrote OpenWebUIContainerDown's description, which claimed to cover 'down or unhealthy'
,  * added a file header stating why there is no health rule, and a back-reference in the quadlet saying re-enabling healthCmd is a TWO-FILE change

VERIFIED live after switch, not from the source:
,  * running Prometheus now loads 3 open_webui rules, all health=ok, lastError empty
,  * OpenWebUIUnhealthy: 0 occurrences in /api/v1/rules
,  * instrument checked too -- container_running{open-webui}=1 with 172,639 samples over 30d, so the surviving disjunct is a proven series, not a fresh one
,  * the full OpenWebUIContainerDown expr evaluates status=success, empty result (correct: container is up)

CHECKED THE WIDER CLAIM: the issue asked whether other containers share this shape. They do not. The generic rules in alerts/container-health.yaml select {name!=""}, and container_health_status carries BOTH a container and a name label -- 17 series match now, 24 over 30d. Those rules are live; they simply only ever match the 2 containers that define a healthcheck (budget-board-client/server). Correct behaviour, not a defect. open-webui.yaml was the only file with dead health rules.
#+end_example
